!flag The essence, the formula of which is a definition | Ross's "essence" is τὸ τί ἦν εἶναι, "what it is to be", and his "formula" is λόγος, "account": what it is to be for a thing is what its definition states.
!note "Divine beings" (δαιμόνια): the heavenly bodies, which Aristotle thought living and divine. "Some say": the Pythagoreans and the Platonists, who made the limits of bodies, the plane, the line, the point and number, prior to bodies and their substance; Book Β (chapter 5) sets out the puzzle, and Book Μ (chapters 2 and 3) answers it.

@p
@greek συμβαίνει δὴ κατὰ δύο τρόπους
It follows, then, that substance is said in two ways: the last underlying thing, which is no longer said of anything else; and whatever, being a this, is also separable. Of this kind is the shape and form of each thing.
!flag It follows, then, that substance has two senses, (a) the ultimate substratum | Ross's "substratum" is τὸ ὑποκείμενον, "the underlying thing". The two ways are the inquiry of Book Ζ in brief: substance as the underlying thing (Ζ 3) and as the form, which is a this and separable (Ζ 4-17).
!note "A this" (τόδε τι): something definite that can be pointed to, an individual, as against a "such", a quality. "Separable" (χωριστόν): able to be on its own. The Platonists' Forms are separable in the highest degree; Aristotle's own question, in Books Ζ and Λ, is which substance is separable and in what way.

@ch Metaph.V.9 | Same, other, different, like
Things are the same by accident, as the pale and the musical, but only of individuals; and in themselves, in as many ways as they are one: sameness is a kind of oneness. "Other" is said in the opposite ways. Things are different that are other while being the same in some respect; like that have the same affections, all or most or the chief ones.

@p
Things are said to be the same by accident: the pale and the musical are the same because they are accidents of the same thing; and man and musical because one is an accident of the other; and the musical is a man because it is an accident of the man. And the whole is the same as each of these, and each of them as it: man and musical are both said to be the same as musical man, and it as them. That is why none of these is said universally: it is not true to say that every man is the same as the musical. For universal attributes belong to things in themselves, but accidents do not belong in themselves, and are said simply only of particulars: Socrates and musical Socrates seem to be the same; but "Socrates" is not said of many, and so we do not say "every Socrates" as we say "every man".

@p
@greek καὶ τὰ μὲν οὕτως λέγεται ταὐτά
Some things are said to be the same in this way, others in themselves, in as many ways as the one is: things whose matter is one, in kind or in number, are said to be the same, and so are those whose substance is one. So it is plain that sameness is a kind of oneness of being, either of more things than one, or of one thing when it is treated as more than one, as when one says that a thing is the same as itself: one treats it as two. Things are called other whose forms are more than one, or their matter, or the account of their substance; and in general "other" is said in ways opposite to "same".
!flag Clearly, therefore, sameness is a unity of the being | Ross's "unity" renders ἑνότης, "oneness", the word Aristotle coins from "one"; the modern version says "oneness", and keeps "the One" for Plato's principle.
!note Plato's Sophist (254d-256d) sets the same and the other among the five greatest kinds, with being, motion and rest; the Platonists read this chapter beside it.

@p
@greek διάφορα δὲ λέγεται ὅσʼ ἕτερά ἐστι
Things are called different that are other while being the same in some respect, not only in number but in species or in genus or by analogy; again, those whose genus is other, and contraries, and whatever has its otherness in its substance. Things are called like that are affected in the same way in every respect; and that are affected in more ways the same than other; and whose quality is one. And a thing is like another if it has more of the contraries, or the more important of them, in respect of which things can be altered. Unlike is said in ways opposite to like.

@ch Metaph.V.10 | Opposite, contrary, other in species
Opposites are contradictories, contraries, relatives, privation and state, and the extremes of coming to be and perishing; and what cannot be present together in what can receive both. Contraries are what differ most within one genus, one receiver or one power. Things other in species are of the same genus but not one under the other, or differ, or have a contrariety in their substance.

@p
Contradiction is called opposite, and contraries, and relatives, and privation and state, and the last things from which and into which comings to be and perishings go. And things that cannot be present together in what can receive both are said to be opposed, either themselves or what they are made of. Grey and white do not belong together to the same thing, and so what they are made of is opposed.
!flag Grey and white colour do not belong at the same time to the same thing | Ross's "grey" is φαιόν, a dark, dusky colour, made, on the theory of the time, of white and black; it is the white and the black of which grey and white are made that are opposed, not grey and white themselves.
!note The four kinds of opposite, contradictories, contraries, relatives, and privation and state, are set out in the Categories (chapter 10). The Platonists read the list beside the "first and greatest kinds" of the Sophist, and the opposites from which all things come in the Phaedo (70d-71a).

@p
@greek ἐναντία λέγεται τά τε μὴ δυνατὰ
Things are called contrary that differ in genus and cannot be present together in the same thing; and those that differ most of the things in the same genus; and those that differ most of the things in the same receiver; and those that differ most of the things under the same power; and those whose difference is greatest, either simply, or in genus, or in species. Other things are called contrary because they have such contraries, or can receive them, or can produce them or be acted on by them, or are producing or being acted on by them, or are losses or gains, or states or privations, of them. And since one and being are said in many ways, the other things said by reference to them must follow them, and so the same, the other and the contrary too, so that they are different in each category.
!flag to the most different of the things that fall under the same faculty | Ross's "faculty" renders δύναμις, "power", here the power or art that deals with both contraries, as medicine with health and disease; the modern version says "power".

@p
@greek ἕτερα δὲ τῷ εἴδει λέγεται ὅσα τε
Things are called other in species that are of the same genus but are not one under the other; and those that, being in the same genus, have a difference; and those that have a contrariety in their substance. And contraries are other in species from each other, either all of them or those called contraries in the first sense; and so are things whose accounts are other in the last species of the genus (as man and horse are indivisible in genus, but their accounts are other); and things that, being in the same substance, have a difference. Things the same in species are said in ways opposite to these.
!note Ross's page has lost the words from "either all of them" to "whose accounts" (at 1018b), and the modern version follows the Greek there. Ross's own note says that the last clause, "things that, being in the same substance, have a difference", has no satisfactory explanation.

@ch Metaph.V.11 | Prior and posterior
Things are prior by being nearer to some first principle: in place, in time, in motion, in power, in order. Things are prior in knowledge: in account the universal, in sense the particular. Affections of prior things are prior. And things are prior in nature and substance that can be without others, but not they without them, the distinction Plato used; in a way every other sense comes back to this.

@p
Prior and posterior are said, in some cases, on the assumption that there is something first, a principle, in each kind, by being nearer to some principle marked out either simply and by nature, or relative to something, or somewhere, or by certain people. So things are prior in place by being nearer either to some place marked out by nature, such as the middle or the end, or to something chosen at random, and what is further off is posterior. Others are prior in time: some by being further from the present, as with past events (the Trojan war is prior to the Persian, because it is further from the present); others by being nearer to the present, as with future events (the Nemean games are prior to the Pythian, because they are nearer the present, if we treat the present as the principle and first point). Others in motion: what is nearer to the first mover is prior, as the boy is prior to the man; and this too is a principle in an absolute sense. Others in power: what exceeds in power is prior, the more powerful; and such is that by whose choice the other, the posterior, must follow, so that if it does not move the other is not moved, and if it moves it is moved; and here choice is the principle. Others in order: these are the things set at intervals by some rule relative to some one marked-out thing, as the second man in the chorus is prior to the third, and the next-to-the-last string to the last; for in the one case the leader is the principle, and in the other the middle string.
!flag and the prime mover also is a beginning absolutely | Ross's "prime mover" is not the first mover of Book Λ: the Greek says "what first moved", here the begetter, to whom the boy is nearer than the man is, since he came from him more lately. This too, Aristotle says, is a principle in an absolute sense.
!note The Nemean games were held in the second and fourth years of each Olympiad, the Pythian games at Delphi in the third: in the year after the Pythian, the Nemean came first. The chorus is the tragic chorus, drawn up in rows, its leader (κορυφαῖος) at the head; the strings are those of the lyre, whose middle string (μέση) gave the key.

@p
@greek ταῦτα μὲν οὖν πρότερα τοῦτον λέγεται
These, then, are called prior in this way; and in another way what is prior in knowledge is called prior, as if prior simply. Of these, the things prior in account are other than those prior in sense. In account the universals are prior, in sense the particulars. And in account the accident is prior to the whole, as the musical to the musical man: for the account will not be whole without the part, though there cannot be musical unless someone is musical. Again, the affections of prior things are called prior, as straightness is prior to smoothness: the one is an affection of a line in itself, the other of a surface.
!note Prior in account and prior in sense: the distinction of Posterior Analytics I.2 (71b33-72a5), what is prior and better known by nature and what is prior and better known to us. Book Ζ (chapter 3, 1029b3-12) asks the learner to go from the second to the first, as Book α (chapter 1) says: our minds are to what is clearest by nature as the eyes of bats to daylight.

@p
@greek τὰ μὲν δὴ οὕτω λέγεται πρότερα καὶ ὕστερα, τὰ δὲ κατὰ φύσιν
Some things, then, are called prior and posterior in this way, and others in nature and substance: those that can be without other things, while these cannot be without them. This is the division Plato used. (Since being is said in many ways, first, the underlying thing is prior, and so substance is prior; next, in another way, things are prior in potency and in actuality: some things are prior in potency, others in actuality. In potency the half line is prior to the whole, the part to the whole and the matter to the substance; but in actuality they are posterior, for only when the whole is dissolved will they be in actuality.) In a way, then, all things called prior and posterior are called so in this last sense. For some can be without the others in coming to be, as the whole without the parts; and others in perishing, as the part without the whole. And likewise with the rest.
!flag a distinction which Plato used | Ross's words are right; his note sends the reader to the Timaeus (34b-c), where Plato says the soul is prior to the body in birth and in excellence, and is the elder; or to the Divisions of Plato that the school handed down. The Platonists made this sense of prior, what can be without the other but not the other without it, their own rule of the order of causes: what is higher can be without what is lower, not the lower without it (Proclus, Elements of Theology 7 and 57).

@ch Metaph.V.12 | Potency, the possible
"Potency" or "power" is a principle of change in another thing, or in the same thing as other; the power to do a thing well; and the state that keeps a thing from being changed for the worse. "Capable" is said in matching ways, and "incapacity" and "incapable" in the opposite ones. "Possible" and "impossible", what is not necessarily false and what is necessarily false, are said without reference to power, and in geometry "power" is said by a transfer of the word.

@p
"Potency" or "power" is said of a principle of motion or change that is in another thing, or in the same thing as other: as the art of building is a power that is not in what is being built, while the art of medicine, which is a power, might be in the one being healed, but not as being healed. Power in general, then, is said of the principle of change or motion in another thing or in the same thing as other; and also of the principle of being changed or moved by another thing or by itself as other. For it is by this principle that what is acted on is acted on, that we call it capable of being acted on: sometimes if it can be acted on in any way at all, sometimes not in every way, but only if it is changed for the better. Again, of the power to carry this out well, or as one chooses: for sometimes we say of those who could only walk or speak, but not well or not as they chose, that they cannot speak or walk; and likewise with being acted on. Again, the states by which things are not acted on at all, or are unchangeable, or are not easily moved for the worse, are called powers. For things are broken, crushed, bent and in general destroyed not by having a power but by not having one and by lacking something; and things are not acted on by such things if they are acted on hardly and slightly, through a power, and by being able, and by being in some state.
!flag Capacity is called, in the first place, the principle of motion | Taylor's "capacity" renders δύναμις, which the modern version says "power" in this chapter, where Aristotle speaks of what can act or be acted on, and "potency" where it is set against actuality, as everywhere in this work.
!note Book Θ (chapters 1 to 5) takes up power in this first sense, the principle of change in another thing, before it turns to potency as against actuality (Θ 6 to 9).

@p
@greek λεγομένης δὲ τῆς δυνάμεως τοσαυταχῶς
Since power is said in so many ways, "capable" too will be said, in one way, of what has a principle of motion or change (for what can bring a thing to rest is capable of something too) in another thing, or in itself as other; in one way, if something else has such a power over it; and in one way if it has the power to change into anything, whether for the worse or for the better. For even what perishes seems capable of perishing, or it would not have perished if it had been incapable; as it is, it has a certain disposition, cause and principle of such an affection. Sometimes it seems to be such by having something, sometimes by being deprived of something; but if privation is in a way a state, everything will be capable by having something, so that a thing is capable both by having some state and principle and by having the privation of this, if it is possible to have a privation; and if not, "capable" is said homonymously. And in one way a thing is capable because nothing else, nor itself as other, has a power or principle that can destroy it. Again, all these are said either because the thing might merely happen to come to be or not, or because it might do so well. For there is such a power even in lifeless things, as in instruments: we say that one lyre can speak, and another cannot at all, if it does not sound well.
!note The Greek of the sentence on privation is uncertain, and the 1924 text brackets some words; the modern version gives the sense Ross gives it.

@p
@greek ἀδυναμία δὲ ἐστὶ στέρησις
Incapacity is the privation of power, of such a principle as has been described: either altogether, or in what would naturally have it, or even when it would naturally have it already; for we would not call a boy, a man and a eunuch likewise incapable of begetting. Again, to each kind of power there is an opposite incapacity, both to the power that can only move and to the power that can move well.

@p
@greek καὶ ἀδύνατα δὴ τὰ μὲν κατὰ τὴν ἀδυναμίαν
Some things, then, are called incapable by this incapacity, and others in another way, as in "possible" and "impossible". The impossible is that whose contrary is necessarily true: it is impossible for the diagonal to be commensurate, because such a thing is false, and its contrary is not only true but necessary; so that its being commensurate is not only false but necessarily false. The contrary of this, the possible, is when the contrary is not necessarily false: it is possible for a man to be seated, for his not being seated is not necessarily false. The possible, then, in one way, as has been said, means what is not necessarily false; in another, what is true; in another, what can be true. In geometry "power" is said by a transfer of the word. These senses of "possible", then, are not by reference to power; but the senses said by reference to power are all said with reference to the first one: a principle of change in another thing or in the same thing as other. For the other things are called capable either because something else has such a power over them, or because it has not, or because it has it in a certain way; and likewise the incapable. So the strict definition of the first power will be: a principle of change in another thing, or in the same thing as other.
!flag in geometry is so called by a change of meaning | Ross's words are right; the note is on the Greek: in geometry δύναμις, "power", is the square of a line, the side "having the power" to make the square; so the English "power" of a number, from the Greek through the Latin. Plato's Theaetetus (147d-148b) uses the word so.

@ch Metaph.V.13 | Quantity
A quantity is what can be divided into parts within it, each of which is one and a this: a plurality if it can be counted, a magnitude if it can be measured. Some things are quantities in themselves, as the line, or as affections of such, as much and few, great and small; others by accident, as the musical, or as motion and time, which are quantities because what they belong to is divisible.

@p
"Quantity" is said of what can be divided into parts present in it, each or every one of which is by nature some one thing and a this. A plurality is a quantity if it can be counted, a magnitude if it can be measured. Plurality is said of what can be divided in potency into parts that are not continuous, magnitude of what can be divided into continuous parts. Of magnitudes, what is continuous in one direction is length, in two breadth, in three depth. Of these, a limited plurality is number, a limited length a line, breadth a surface, depth a body.
!note The definition of number as "a limited plurality" is Aristotle's; Book Ι (chapter 6) adds that it is a plurality measured by the one. The Platonists, after the Pythagoreans, put number before magnitude, as the point comes before the line (Proclus, on Euclid I, prologue).

@p
@greek ἔτι τὰ μὲν λέγεται καθʼ αὑτὰ ποσά
Again, some things are called quantities in themselves, others by accident: the line is a quantity in itself, the musical by accident. Of the things that are quantities in themselves, some are so as substances, as the line is a quantity (for "a certain quantity" is present in the account that says what it is); others are affections and states of such a substance, as much and few, long and short, broad and narrow, deep and shallow, heavy and light, and the others of the kind. And the great and the small, and greater and smaller, said both in themselves and relative to each other, are affections of quantity in themselves; but these names are carried over to other things too. Of the things called quantities by accident, some are said so in the way it was said that the musical and the pale are quantities, by the thing they belong to being a quantity; others as motion and time are. These too are called quantities of a sort, and continuous, because the things of which they are affections are divisible. I mean not what is moved but the distance it was moved over: because that is a quantity, the motion is a quantity too, and the time because the motion is.
!flag I mean not that which is moved, but the space through which it is moved | Ross's "the space through which it is moved" is right in sense, but the Greek says only "what it was moved" (ὃ ἐκινήθη), the magnitude passed over; the same argument, that motion is continuous because the magnitude is, and time because motion is, is in Physics IV.11 (219a10-14).
!note "The great and the small" were, for Plato as Aristotle reports him, the material principle of the Forms and the numbers, with the One as their form (Book Α, 987b20-27). Here Aristotle treats them as what they are in common speech: affections of quantity, carried over to other things by a transfer of the word.

@ch Metaph.V.14 | Quality
"Quality" is said of the difference of a substance (man is a two-footed animal); of what numbers have besides quantity; of the affections of things in motion, by which they are altered; and of virtue and vice, good and bad. In the main, quality is two things: the difference of the substance, and the affections of moving things as moving; good and bad are qualities above all in living things that choose.

@p
"Quality" is said, in one way, of the difference of the substance: man is an animal of a certain quality because he is two-footed, the horse because it is four-footed, and the circle is a figure of a certain quality because it has no angles; the difference in the substance being a quality. This, then, is one way quality is said, the difference of a substance. In another way, as of the unmoved things and the objects of mathematics: the numbers are of a certain quality, as the composite numbers that are not in one line only, but of which the plane and the solid are copies (these are the numbers that are so many times so many, or so many times so many times so many); and in general what is present in the substance besides the quantity. For the substance of each number is what it is once: of six, not what it is twice or three times, but what it is once; for six is once six.
!flag the differentia of the essence | Ross's "essence" here is οὐσία, "substance", which the modern version says throughout; his "differentia" is διαφορά, "difference", the word that marks off a species within its genus, as two-footed marks man off among the animals.
!note The plane and solid numbers are the products of two and of three factors, which the Greeks drew as rectangles and boxes of units: six is a plane number, two times three, eight a solid, two times two times two. Aristotle's point is that six is not "two threes" but six, once: its substance is not its factors.

@p
@greek ἔτι ὅσα πάθη τῶν κινουμένων
Again, the affections of substances in motion, such as heat and cold, whiteness and blackness, heaviness and lightness, and the like, by which bodies are said to be altered when they change. Again, quality as to virtue and vice, and in general the bad and the good.

@p
@greek σχεδὸν δὴ κατὰ δύο τρόπους
Quality, then, would be said in two ways, roughly, and of these one is the most strict. The first quality is the difference of the substance (and of this the quality in numbers is a part, for it is a difference of substances, but either of things not moved or not as moved). Next come the affections of things moved, as moved, and the differences of motions. Virtue and vice are among these affections: they show differences of the motion and of the activity by which things in motion act or are acted on well or badly. What can be moved or be active in this way is good, and what can in that way and the contrary way is bad. Good and bad signify quality most in living things, and among these most in those that have choice.
!flag that which can do so in another—the contrary—way is vicious | Ross's "vicious" renders μοχθηρόν, "bad", "wretched": the word is the general one for what is in a poor state, not only for vice of character.

@ch Metaph.V.15 | Relative
Things are relative as the double to the half and what exceeds to what is exceeded (by number); as what can heat to what can be heated (by power and its activity); and as the measurable to the measure, the knowable to knowledge. The first two are relative by being what they are of something else; the third because something else is related to them. Others are relative through their genus, or by having such a property, or by accident.

@p
Things are called relative, some as the double to the half and the triple to the third, and in general the multiple to the submultiple and what exceeds to what is exceeded; others as what can heat to what can be heated, what can cut to what can be cut, and in general what can act to what can be acted on; others as the measurable to the measure, the knowable to knowledge and the perceptible to perception. The first are said by number, either indefinitely or definitely, to numbers themselves or to one. The double is in a definite number relative to one; the multiple is in a number relative to one, but not a definite one, not this one or that; the one-and-a-half to its reciprocal is in a definite number relative to a number; the superparticular to the subsuperparticular in an indefinite one, as the multiple to one; and what exceeds to what is exceeded is altogether indefinite in number. For number is commensurable, and number is not said of what is not commensurable; and what exceeds, relative to what is exceeded, is so much and something more, and this something is indefinite, since it may be, as it happens, either equal or not equal. All these relatives, then, are said by number and are affections of number; and so, in another way, are the equal, the like and the same. For these are all said by reference to the one: things are the same whose substance is one, like whose quality is one, equal whose quantity is one; and the one is the principle and measure of number, so that all these are called relative by number, but not in the same way.
!flag but this relation may involve a ‘noncommensurate number’ | Ross's words follow another reading of the Greek: his text of 1924 says that number is not said of what is not commensurable, which the modern version follows. What exceeds may exceed by an amount that has no common measure with it, and then no number states the relation.
!note The "superparticular" (ἐπιμόριον) is the ratio of a number to the next below it, n+1 to n, as 3:2 and 4:3, the ratios of the fifth and the fourth in music; the one-and-a-half (ἡμιόλιον) is 3:2 itself. Plato builds the soul of the world from such ratios in the Timaeus (35b-36b).

@p
@greek τὰ δὲ ποιητικὰ καὶ παθητικὰ κατὰ δύναμιν
What can act and what can be acted on are relative by a power to act and a power to be acted on, and by the activities of the powers: what can heat is relative to what can be heated because it can heat it, and again what heats to what is heated, and what cuts to what is cut, as being active. Relatives by number have no activities, except in the way stated elsewhere; activities in the sense of motion they do not have. Of relatives by power, some are relative by times as well: what has made to what has been made, and what will make to what will be made. It is in this way that a father is called the father of a son: the one has acted, the other has been acted on. Again, some are relative by privation of power, as the incapable and things said so, such as the invisible.

@p
@greek τὰ μὲν οὖν κατʼ ἀριθμὸν καὶ δύναμιν λεγόμενα
Things called relative by number and by power, then, are all relative because just what they are is said to be of something else, not because something else is relative to them. But the measurable, the knowable and the thinkable are called relative because something else is said with reference to them. For the thinkable signifies that there is thought of it; but thought is not relative to that of which it is the thought (for the same thing would have been said twice). Likewise sight is sight of something, not of that of which it is the sight (though it is true to say so), but relative to colour or something else of the kind; said the other way, the same thing will be said twice: that sight is of that of which it is the sight.
!note Plato's Charmides (167c-169a) asks whether there can be a knowledge whose object is only knowledge, as sight would see only sight; and the Republic (438b-e) sets out the rule that a relative is of its own correlative, knowledge of the knowable. Aristotle's third kind of relative, the knowable and the measurable, is the one that the Platonists set highest: the intelligible is prior to the intellect that knows it.

@p
@greek τὰ μὲν οὖν καθʼ ἑαυτὰ λεγόμενα πρός τι
Things said to be relative in themselves are said so in these ways; others because their genera are such: medicine is a relative because its genus, knowledge, seems to be a relative. Again, the properties by which the things having them are called relative: equality because the equal is relative, and likeness because the like is. Others are relative by accident: a man is relative because it happens to him to be double, and the double is a relative; or the white, if it happens to the same thing to be double and white.

@ch Metaph.V.16 | Complete
"Complete" (τέλειον) is said of what has no part outside it; of what cannot be bettered in its kind, as a complete doctor (and, by a transfer, a complete thief), since virtue is a completion; and of what has reached its end, if the end is good (and by a transfer of what is completely ruined). Other things are called complete by reference to these.

@p
"Complete" is said, in one way, of that outside which not even one of its parts can be found: the complete time of each thing is the time outside which no time can be found that is a part of it. And of what, in excellence and in doing well, has nothing beyond it in its kind: as a doctor is complete and a flute-player complete, when they lack nothing in the form of their proper virtue. (So too, by a transfer of the word, we speak of bad things, and say a complete informer and a complete thief, since we even call them good, a good thief and a good informer. And virtue is a kind of completion: for each thing is complete, and every substance complete, when in the form of its proper virtue it lacks no part of its natural magnitude.)
!flag and speak of a complete scandal-monger and a complete thief | Ross's "scandal-monger" renders συκοφάντης, the "informer" of Athens, who brought prosecutions for gain or to blackmail; the word is the root of "sycophant", which has since come to mean a flatterer.
!flag The perfect is denominated that of which no part can be | Taylor's "perfect" and Ross's "complete" both render τέλειον, which is from τέλος, "end": what has reached its end, lacks nothing. The modern version says "complete", and keeps the link with "end" in the third sense.

@p
@greek ἔτι οἷς ὑπάρχει τὸ τέλος
Again, things that have reached their end, if it is good, are called complete; for things are complete by having their end. So, since the end is something last, we carry the word over to bad things too, and say that a thing has been completely ruined and completely destroyed, when nothing is lacking to its destruction and its badness, but it is at the last point. That is why death too is called the end by a transfer of the word, because both are last things; but that for the sake of which, the final end, is an end too. Things called complete in themselves, then, are called so in all these ways: some because, in doing well, they lack nothing and have nothing beyond them, and nothing of theirs can be found outside; others because, in general, they have nothing beyond them in each kind and nothing of theirs is outside. The others are now called complete by reference to these, because they make something of the kind, or have it, or fit it, or are in one way or another related to the things first called complete.
!note For the Platonists the complete is the mark of the first causes: what is complete gives of itself to what comes after it, as Proclus says (Elements of Theology 25), and the most complete is the cause of the most things. Plato's maker of the world makes it complete, a whole of wholes, lacking nothing (Timaeus 32c-34b).

@ch Metaph.V.17 | Limit
"Limit" is said of the last point of a thing, the first outside which there is nothing of it; of the form of a magnitude; of the end; and of the substance, what it is to be, which is the limit of knowledge and so of the thing. Limit is said in as many ways as principle, and more: every principle is a limit, but not every limit a principle.

@p
"Limit" is said of the last point of each thing, the first point outside which nothing of it can be found and the first within which all of it is; and of whatever is the form of a magnitude or of what has magnitude; and of the end of each thing (such is that to which the motion and the action go, and not that from which they go, though sometimes it is both, that from which and that to which, and that for the sake of which); and of the substance of each thing, what it is to be for each. For this is the limit of knowledge; and if of knowledge, of the thing too. So it is plain that limit is said in as many ways as principle, and in more: for the principle is a limit, but not every limit is a principle.
!note "Limit" (πέρας) is a word of the Philebus (23c-27c), where everything that is is a mixture of limit and the unlimited, and of the Pythagoreans before Plato. The Platonists after Plotinus made limit and the unlimited the first pair after the One (Proclus, Elements of Theology 89-92, and Platonic Theology III).

@ch Metaph.V.18 | That in virtue of which; in itself
"That in virtue of which" (καθ' ὅ) is said of the form, of the first thing a quality is in, of every kind of cause, and of position. So "in itself" (καθ' αὑτό) is said in many ways: of what it is to be, of what is in the what-it-is, of what is received first in the thing or a part of it, of what has no other cause, and of what belongs to a thing alone, as alone.

@p
"That in virtue of which" is said in many ways. In one way it is the form and the substance of each thing, as that in virtue of which one is good is the good itself; in another, the first thing in which something naturally comes to be, as colour in the surface. That in virtue of which, then, said in the first way, is the form; in the second way, the matter of each thing and the first underlying thing of each. In general "that in virtue of which" will be said in as many ways as "cause": we say "in virtue of what has he come?" or "for the sake of what has he come?", and "in virtue of what has he reasoned falsely, or reasoned?", or "what is the cause of the reasoning, or of the false reasoning?" Again, "that in virtue of which" is said of position: "that by which he stands" or "that along which he walks"; for all these signify place and position.
!flag that in virtue of which a man is good is the good itself | Ross's words follow his own correction of the Greek (his note says so), which the 1924 text keeps: "the good itself" (αὐτὸ ἀγαθόν). The Platonists heard in it the Form of the Good, by which good things are good (Republic 505a, 508e); Aristotle means the goodness in the man, his form.
!note Ross's note says that "no English word or phrase has quite the same ambiguity" as the Greek καθ' ὅ, which can mean "in virtue of which", "according to which" and, of place, "by which" or "along which".

@p
@greek ὥστε καὶ τὸ καθʼ αὑτὸ πολλαχῶς
So "in itself" too must be said in many ways. In one way, what it is to be for each thing: Callias is in himself Callias, and what it is to be Callias. In another, whatever is present in the what-it-is: Callias is in himself an animal, for animal is present in his account, since Callias is a certain animal. Again, if a thing has received something first in itself or in one of its parts: the surface is white in itself, and a man lives in himself, for the soul, in which life first is, is a part of the man. Again, what has no other cause: man has many causes, animal, two-footed, but still man is man in himself. Again, whatever belongs to a thing alone, and as alone; and so what is separated is in itself.
!flag the essence of each thing, e.g. Callias is in virtue of himself Callias and the essence of Callias | Ross's "essence" is τὸ τί ἦν εἶναι, "what it is to be", as everywhere in this work. "In itself" (καθ' αὑτό) is the Latin "per se"; the senses here are those of Posterior Analytics I.4 (73a34-b24).

@ch Metaph.V.19 | Disposition
A disposition is the order of what has parts, in place, in power or in form: a placing, as the word shows.

@p
"Disposition" is said of the order of what has parts, either in place, or in power, or in form. For there must be some position, as the word "disposition" itself shows.
!flag either according^ to place, or according to capacity | Taylor's "capacity" renders δύναμις, "power", which the modern version says here; and the word "disposition" (διάθεσις, "placing apart") contains θέσις, "position", as the Latin "dispositio" contains "positio".

@ch Metaph.V.20 | Having, state
"Having" (ἕξις) is an activity between the haver and the had, as making is between maker and made, and there can be no having of it. A state (ἕξις too) is a disposition by which a thing is well or badly disposed, as health; or a part of such a disposition, as the virtue of the parts.

@p
"Having" (ἕξις) is said, in one way, of a kind of activity of the haver and the had, as a kind of action or motion: when one thing makes and another is made, there is a making between them; so between the one who has a garment and the garment had there is a having. It is plain, then, that one cannot have this having: it would go on without end, if one could have the having of what is had. In another way, ἕξις is said of a disposition by which what is disposed is disposed well or badly, either in itself or relative to something else: health is a state, for it is such a disposition. Again, a part of such a disposition is called a state; and so the virtue of the parts is a state.
!flag means (1) a kind of activity of the haver and the had | Ross's "activity" is ἐνέργεια, "actuality", "being at work", here the having as it is going on. The one Greek word ἕξις does duty, as Ross's note says, for "having", "habit" and "state": the modern version says "having" in the first sense and "state" in the others. Virtue, the Ethics says (II.5), is a state in this second sense.

@ch Metaph.V.21 | Affection
"Affection" (πάθος) is said of a quality by which a thing can be altered; of the alterations themselves; above all of harmful alterations and painful harms; and of great misfortunes.

@p
"Affection" is said, in one way, of a quality by which a thing can be altered, as white and black, sweet and bitter, heaviness and lightness, and all the others of the kind. In another, of the activities of these, the alterations already taking place. Again, more than these, of the harmful alterations and motions, and above all of painful harms. Again, great misfortunes and pains are called affections.
!flag Passion is called in the first place quality | Taylor's "passion" renders πάθος, from πάσχειν, "to undergo", "to be acted on": what happens to a thing, not only a strong feeling. The modern version says "affection", Ross's word; the last senses, harms and misfortunes, are where the English "suffering" and "passion" come from.

@ch Metaph.V.22 | Privation
A thing is deprived when it lacks what some things naturally have, or what it or its genus naturally has, or what it naturally has and at the time when it should; and violent taking away is privation too. There are as many privations as words with the privative "un-" (in Greek, alpha): not having at all, having badly, having little, having not easily or not well. So not everyone is good or bad: there is a middle.

@p
"Privation" is said, in one way, if a thing does not have one of the attributes that things naturally have, even if it would not itself naturally have it: so a plant is said to be deprived of eyes. In another way, if it does not have what it, or its genus, would naturally have: so a blind man and a mole are deprived of sight in different ways, the one in respect of its genus, the other in respect of himself. Again, if it does not have it although it would naturally have it, and when it would naturally have it: blindness is a privation, but one is not blind at any age, only if one does not have sight at the age at which one would naturally have it. Likewise if it does not have it in the medium in which, by the organ by which, relative to the thing to which, and in the manner in which it would naturally have it. Again, the violent taking away of anything is called privation.
!note The mole, Aristotle says in On the Soul (III.1, 425a10-11) and the History of Animals (I.9, 491b28-34; IV.8, 533a3-12), has eyes beneath the skin, but cannot see: it lacks sight in respect of its genus, the animals, which naturally see.

@p
@greek καὶ ὁσαχῶς δὲ αἱ ἀπὸ τοῦ α ἀποφάσεις
Privations are said in as many ways as negations from the alpha (our "un-" or "in-"). A thing is called unequal by not having the equality it would naturally have; invisible either by having no colour at all or by having it poorly; footless either by having no feet at all or by having poor ones. Again, by having little of it, as the stoneless (of fruit): this is having it poorly in a way. Again, by not having it easily or not well: the uncuttable is not only what cannot be cut but what cannot be cut easily or well. Again, by not having it at all: it is not the one-eyed man who is called blind, but the one who has no sight in either eye. That is why not everyone is good or bad, just or unjust: there is also the middle.
!flag Again, a privative term may be used because the thing has little of the | Ross's page has lost a line here (at 1022b and 1023a): his "kernelless" is ἀπύρηνον, "stoneless", said of a fruit with a small stone, and the lost words say that this is having it poorly. Ross's page has lost the end of the paragraph too, from "one-eyed" to "That is why" (at 1023a); the modern version follows the Greek in both places.

@ch Metaph.V.23 | Having, holding
"To have" or "hold" (ἔχειν) is said of treating a thing by one's own nature or impulse, as a fever has a man; of what a thing is in as in its receiver; of the container holding what it contains; and of what holds a thing back from its own impulse, as pillars hold up a weight. "Being in" is said in the same ways.

@p
"To have" is said in many ways. In one way, to treat a thing according to one's own nature or one's own impulse: so a fever is said to have a man, and tyrants their cities, and those who wear clothes their clothes. In another way, that in which something is present, as in what can receive it, is said to have it: the bronze has the form of the statue, and the body the disease. In another way, as the container holds what is contained: what is in a container is said to be held by it, as we say that the vessel holds the liquid, the city men and the ship sailors; and so the whole holds the parts. Again, what prevents a thing from moving or acting by its own impulse is said to hold it: as pillars hold the weights placed on them, and as the poets make Atlas hold up the heaven, which would otherwise fall onto the earth, as some of the natural philosophers also say. In this way too what holds things together is said to hold the things it holds together, since they would otherwise separate, each by its own impulse. And "being in something" is said in like ways, following "having".
!flag as pillars hold the incumbent | Ross's page has lost words here (at 1023a), those on Atlas holding up the heaven; the modern version follows the Greek. "Some of the natural philosophers": On the Heavens (II.1, 284a18-26) says that some thought the heaven needs a support; Aristotle's own heaven moves by its nature and needs none.
!note Ross's page has lost words in the first and third senses as well (at 1023a), from "according to one's own impulse" to "tyrants", and from "what is in a container" to "the vessel"; the modern version follows the Greek.

@ch Metaph.V.24 | Being from something
"From something" (ἔκ τινος) is said of matter, of the first moving principle, of the compound (the parts from the whole, a line from the Iliad), of the form from its part, of one of these taken in part (the child from its parents), and of what comes after in time (night from day).

@p
"To be from something" is said, in one way, of that from which a thing is, as from matter; and this in two ways, either according to the first genus or according to the last species: in one way all things that can be melted are from water, in another the statue is from bronze. In another way, as from the first principle that moved it: "from what came the fight? From abuse", because this was the principle of the fight. In another way, from the compound of matter and shape, as the parts are from the whole, the verse from the Iliad and the stones from the house: for the shape is the end, and what has its end is complete. In another way, as the form is from its part: man from two-footed, and the syllable from the letter; for this is another way than the way the statue is from bronze, since the composite substance is from sensible matter, but the form too is from the matter of the form.
!note "The matter of the form": the parts of a definition, genus and difference, of which the form is composed as a statue of its bronze. Book Ζ (chapters 10 to 12) and Book Η (chapter 6) take this "intelligible matter" further.

@p
@greek τὰ μὲν οὖν οὕτω λέγεται, τὰ δʼ ἐὰν κατὰ μέρος
Some things, then, are said to be from something in these ways; others if one of these ways belongs to a part of it: as the child is from father and mother, and plants from the earth, because they are from some part of these. In another way, what comes after something in time: as night is from day, and storm from fair weather, because the one comes after the other. Of these, some are said so because they change into each other, as with the ones just named; others only because they follow one another in time, as "the voyage was from the equinox", because it took place after the equinox, and "the Thargelia are from the Dionysia", because they come after the Dionysia.
!flag the child comes from its father and mother and plants come from | Ross's page has lost the rest of the chapter (at 1023b), on what comes after in time; the modern version follows the Greek. The Dionysia, the great festival of Dionysus, came in spring; the Thargelia, the festival of Apollo, two months after.

@ch Metaph.V.25 | Part
"Part" is said of anything into which a quantity can be divided, or of what measures it exactly; of the species into which a kind is divided; of what a whole, the form or what has the form, is made of; and of what is in the account of a thing: so the genus is a part of the species, and the species, in another way, of the genus.

@p
"Part" is said, in one way, of that into which a quantity may be divided in any way: for what is taken away from a quantity, as a quantity, is always called a part of it, as two is called in a way a part of three. In another way, only of those that measure such a quantity exactly; so that two is in one way said to be a part of three, and in another not. Again, the things into which the kind might be divided, apart from quantity, are called its parts too: so people say the species are parts of the genus. Again, the things into which a whole is divided or of which it is composed, whether the whole is the form or what has the form: of the bronze sphere or the bronze cube, both the bronze is a part (this is the matter in which the form is) and the angle is a part. Again, the things in the account that shows each thing are parts of the whole too: so the genus is called a part of the species as well, though in another way the species is part of the genus.
!flag and the characteristic angle are parts | Ross's "characteristic" is his own addition: the Greek says only "the angle" (ἡ γωνία), which is part of the form of the cube, as the bronze is part of its matter.
!note Plato's Parmenides (137c-d, 142d-145a) and Theaetetus (204a-205e) ask how a whole is related to its parts, and whether the whole is something besides them; Aristotle's next chapter gives his answer for the word.

@ch Metaph.V.26 | Whole, total, all
A whole is what lacks none of the parts it has by nature, and what so contains its contents that they are one: the universal, which is one as each, and the continuous and limited, which is one from many. Where position makes no difference, we say "total" (πᾶν), where it does, "whole"; some things are both.

@p
"Whole" is said of that from which no part is absent of those of which it is called by nature a whole; and of what so contains the things it contains that they are one. This is in two ways: either as each is one, or as the one is made of them. The universal, and what is said generally as being a whole, is universal as containing many things, by being said of each and by all of them being one as each is, as man, horse and god are, because all are living beings. And the continuous and limited is a whole, when it is one thing made of many that are present in it, above all in potency, or if not, in actuality. Of these themselves, things that are such by nature are wholes more than those by art, as we said of the one too, wholeness being a kind of oneness.
!flag e.g. man, horse, god, are one, because all are living things | Ross's words are right; the Greek ζῷα is "animals", "living beings", and Aristotle counts god among them: god, Book Λ says (1072b28-30), is a living being, eternal and most good. For the Platonists the example is less strange: the world itself is a living being, with soul and intellect (Timaeus 30b).

@p
@greek ἔτι τοῦ ποσοῦ ἔχοντος δὲ ἀρχὴν
Again, of a quantity that has a beginning, a middle and an end, those in which the position makes no difference are called a total, and those in which it does, a whole. Those that admit of both are both wholes and totals: these are the things whose nature stays the same when they are rearranged, but whose shape does not, as wax or a cloak; they are called both whole and total, for they have both. Water, all liquids and number are called totals, but "the whole number" or "the whole water" is not said, except by a transfer of the word. Things are called "all", in the plural, of which "total" is said as of one, when they are taken as divided: "this total number", "all these units".
!flag those to which the position does not make a difference are called totals | Ross's "totals" render πᾶν, the Greek for "all", "every", said of what is one: "all the water", "the whole number". English cannot keep πᾶν and its plural πάντα apart in the same word, and the modern version says "total" and "all", as Ross does.

@ch Metaph.V.27 | Mutilated
Not every quantity can be mutilated: it must be divisible and a whole, keep its substance when the part is taken, have parts unlike and set in position, and be continuous; and the part taken must be neither a chief part nor any part at all, but an extremity that does not grow again. So the bald are not mutilated.

@p
"Mutilated" is said not of any quantity whatever: it must be divisible and a whole. Two is not mutilated if one of its ones is taken away (for the part taken off by mutilation is never equal to what is left), nor is any number at all; for the substance must remain: if a cup is mutilated, it must still be a cup, but the number is no longer the same. Besides, not even all things with unlike parts can be mutilated (for a number has in a way unlike parts, as two and three); but in general nothing whose position makes no difference is mutilated, as water or fire: things must be such as to have position in their substance. Again, they must be continuous: for a scale is made of unlike notes and has position, but does not become mutilated. Besides, not even things that are wholes are mutilated by the privation of any part whatever. The parts must be neither the chief parts of the substance nor parts anywhere at all: a cup is not mutilated if it is bored through, but only if the handle or some extremity is taken; and a man is not mutilated if he loses flesh or the spleen, but only some extremity, and not every one, but one which, once taken off altogether, does not grow again. That is why the bald are not mutilated.
!flag for a musical scale consists of unlike parts | Ross's "musical scale" renders ἁρμονία, the "attunement" or tuning of the strings, the order of unlike notes set in their places; the modern version says "scale". In Book Α he renders it so too (985b31).
!flag e. g.a cup is not mutilated if it is bored | Ross's page has lost the end of the chapter (at 1024a), on the handle of the cup, the extremities of a man and the bald; the modern version follows the Greek.

@ch Metaph.V.28 | Genus, race; other in genus
"Genus" (γένος) is said of the race, the continuous generation of things of the same form; of the stock from the first begetter; of the plane or the solid that underlies the differences of figures; and of the first part of an account, of which the qualities are the differences. Things are other in genus whose first underlying thing is other and cannot be reduced to one another, as form and matter, and the categories.

@p
"Genus" or "race" is said, in one way, if there is a continuous generation of things that have the same form: we say "as long as the race of men exists", meaning as long as their generation is continuous. In another way, of that from which things come as from the first that moved them into being: so some are called Hellenes by race and others Ionians, because the one come from Hellen and the others from Ion as their first begetter; and more from the begetter than from the matter (though people get a race-name from the female too, as the descendants of Pyrrha). Again, as the plane is the genus of plane figures and the solid of solids: each of the figures is either a plane of such a kind or a solid of such a kind, and this is what underlies the differences. Again, in accounts, the first part present, which is said in the what-it-is, is the genus, and the qualities are called its differences. "Genus", then, is said in all these ways: by continuous generation of the same form; by the first mover of the same form; and as matter, for that of which the difference and the quality are is the underlying thing, which we call matter.
!flag in reference to the first mover which is of the same | Ross's page has lost the end of this sentence (at 1024b), on genus as matter; the modern version follows the Greek. "Of the same form": the begetter is of the same kind as the begotten, a man from a man.
!note Hellen, son of Deucalion and Pyrrha, was the father of the Greeks in legend; Ion, his grandson, of the Ionians. Pyrrha and Deucalion alone survived the great flood, and peopled the earth again by throwing stones over their shoulders. The Greek γένος is both "race" and "genus", the logician's word.

@p
@greek ἕτερα δὲ τῷ γένει λέγεται
Things are called other in genus whose first underlying thing is other, and of which neither is reduced to the other nor both to the same thing: so form and matter are other in genus; and so are whatever are said according to another figure of predication of being (for some of the things that are signify what a thing is, others a quality, and others as has been divided before). For these too are not reduced either to one another or to some one thing.
!note "As has been divided before": the list of the categories in chapter 7. That the categories cannot be reduced to one genus is why being is not a genus (Book Β, 998b22-27): its unity is "with reference to one thing", substance (Book Γ, chapter 2).

@ch Metaph.V.29 | False
"False" is said of things that are not, or cannot be, put together (the diagonal commensurate), or that make an appearance of what is not (a shadow-painting, a dream); of an account of what is not, so that every account is false of anything but its own (against Antisthenes, who said nothing can be said but by its own account); and of a man who likes such accounts for their own sake. The argument in the Hippias Minor is misleading.

@p
"False" is said, in one way, of a thing as false: this either because it is not put together or cannot be put together, as is said of the diagonal being commensurate, or of your being seated; for of these one is false always, the other at times, and in this way they are not things that are. Or of things that are, but are by nature such as to appear either not such as they are or as things that are not, as shadow-painting and dreams: these are something, but not the things of which they produce the appearance. Things, then, are called false in this way, either because they themselves are not, or because the appearance that comes from them is of something that is not.
!flag e.g. a sketch or a dream | Ross's "sketch" renders σκιαγραφία, "shadow-painting", painting with light and shade that looks like the thing from far off, but not close by. Plato uses the word for the images of the senses and of opinion (Republic 365c, 523b, 583b; Theaetetus 208e; Phaedo 69b).

@p
@greek λόγος δὲ ψευδὴς ὁ τῶν μὴ ὄντων
A false account is the account of things that are not, in so far as it is false. So every account is false of anything other than that of which it is true: the account of the circle is false of the triangle. Of each thing there is in a way one account, that of what it is to be, and in a way many, since the thing itself and the thing with an affection are in a way the same, as Socrates and musical Socrates. (A false account is not an account of anything, simply.) That is why Antisthenes foolishly thought that nothing could be said except by its own account, one of one; from which it followed that there could be no contradiction, and almost no saying anything false. But it is possible to say each thing not only by its own account but by another's, altogether falsely, but also in a way truly: as eight is double by the account of two.
!flag A false conception is the conception of non-existent objects | Ross's "conception" renders λόγος, "account", "statement", what is said of a thing; the modern version says "account", as everywhere in this work. Antisthenes' claim is about speech: one name, one account, for one thing.
!note Antisthenes, a follower of Socrates, held that a thing can be named but not defined, and that one cannot contradict, since two who speak of the same thing say the same, and two who say different things speak of different things. Plato's Euthydemus (285e-286c), Theaetetus (201e-202c) and Sophist (251b-c) answer views of this kind.

@p
@greek τὰ μὲν οὖν οὕτω λέγεται ψευδῆ
Things, then, are called false in these ways; and a false man is one who readily and by choice deals in such accounts, not for anything else but for their own sake, and one who produces such accounts in others, as we say things are false that produce a false appearance. That is why the argument in the Hippias is misleading, that the same man is false and true. For it takes the false man to be the one who can deceive (and this is the one who knows and is wise); and further, that the man who is bad willingly is better. It takes this falsehood through induction: for the man who limps willingly is better than the one who limps unwillingly, taking "limping" to mean mimicking a limp; since if he were lame willingly, he would perhaps be worse, as with character, and so in this case.
!flag This is a false result of zduction | Ross's words are right, the OCR aside: "induction" (ἐπαγωγή) is the leading on from examples, the runner, the wrestler, the archer, by which Socrates in the Hippias Minor (373c-376b) reaches the conclusion that he who errs willingly is better than he who errs unwillingly. The Platonists read that conclusion as Plato's irony: no one who knows the good does wrong willingly.
!note "Wise" here is φρόνιμος, "sensible", "of practical wisdom". The Hippias Minor (365b-369b) argues that the false man and the true are the same, since the one who knows can lie best.

@ch Metaph.V.30 | Accident
An accident is what belongs to a thing and can be truly said of it, but neither of necessity nor for the most part, as finding treasure when digging for a plant; it has no definite cause, only chance. In another sense, what belongs to a thing in itself but is not in its substance is an accident too, as having angles equal to two right angles belongs to the triangle; these can be eternal.

@p
"Accident" is said of what belongs to something and can be truly said of it, but neither of necessity nor for the most part: as if someone, digging a hole for a plant, found treasure. The finding of treasure is an accident to the one digging the hole: for the one does not come of necessity from the other or after it, nor, for the most part, if one plants, does one find treasure. And a musical man might be pale; but since this does not come about of necessity or for the most part, we call it an accident. So, since there are things that belong, and belong to things, and some of them belong somewhere and at some time, whatever belongs, but not because it was this, or now, or here, will be an accident. Nor is there any definite cause of the accident, but only chance, and this is indefinite. Going to Aegina was an accident to someone, if he did not arrive there in order to go there, but because he was driven off course by a storm or captured by pirates. The accident has come about, or is, but not as itself, but as something else: for the storm was the cause of his coming to a place he was not sailing for, and this was Aegina.
!flag Accident is called that which is inherent in something | Taylor's "inherent" renders ὑπάρχει, "belongs to"; the modern version says "belongs". The Greek συμβεβηκός, from συμβαίνειν, "to go along with", "to happen", is what happens to go with a thing, not what is in it by its nature.
!note Book Ε (chapters 2 and 3) returns to the accident: there can be no science of it, since every science is of what is always or for the most part; and Physics II (chapters 4 to 6) on chance and luck.

@p
@greek λέγεται δὲ καὶ ἄλλως συμβεβηκός
"Accident" is said in another way too: of whatever belongs to each thing in itself without being in its substance, as having its angles equal to two right angles belongs to the triangle. These can be eternal, but none of the others can. The account of this is given elsewhere.
!flag This is explained elsewhere | Ross's words are right; "elsewhere" is the Posterior Analytics (I.6, 75a18-22, and I.7), where such attributes, the "accidents in themselves" (καθ' αὑτὰ συμβεβηκότα), are what a science demonstrates of its subject. The later schools called them "properties".

@treatise Metaph.VI | Metaphysics, Book Ε (VI)
Book Ε is short, and a hinge of the work. It begins again from the science of being as being (Book Γ) and sets it among the sciences: every thought is practical, productive or theoretical, and there are three theoretical philosophies, physics, mathematics and theology, of which the first, the science of what is eternal, unmoved and separate, is first philosophy, and universal because it is first (chapter 1). Then it sets aside two of the ways being is said: being by accident, of which there is no science, since every science is of what is always or for the most part (chapters 2 and 3), and being as true, which is in thought, not in things (chapter 4). What remains is being in the ways of the categories, and above all substance, the subject of Book Ζ.

@ch Metaph.VI.1 | The three theoretical sciences, and first philosophy
We are looking for the principles and causes of the things that are, as things that are. The other sciences each mark off one kind of being and do not ask what it is, or whether it is. Physics is about substances that have the principle of motion in themselves; so it is neither practical nor productive but theoretical, and it defines its objects, like "snub", with their matter. Mathematics is theoretical too. If there is something eternal, unmoved and separate, knowing it belongs to a theoretical science prior to both: theology. So there are three theoretical philosophies, and theology is the highest; it is universal because it is first, and it studies being as being.

@p
We are looking for the principles and causes of the things that are, and clearly of them as things that are. There is a cause of health and of good condition, and the objects of mathematics have principles, elements and causes; and in general every science that uses thought, or has any share in thought, deals with causes and principles, more exact or more simple. But all these sciences mark off some one being, some one kind, and busy themselves with that, not with being simply, nor as being; and they give no account of what that thing is. They start from it, some making it plain to the senses, others taking what it is as a hypothesis, and so they demonstrate, more strictly or more loosely, the attributes that belong in themselves to the kind they are about. So it is clear that from such a survey there is no demonstration of substance, or of what a thing is, but some other way of making it known. Likewise they say nothing of whether the kind they deal with is or is not; for it belongs to the same thinking to make plain what a thing is and that it is.
!flag every dianoetic science, or which participates any thing of the dianoetic power | Taylor's "dianoetic" is the Greek διανοητική carried over: the science that works by thought, by reasoning step by step (διάνοια), as against the senses. In the Platonists' scheme of the Republic (511d-e) διάνοια is the reasoning of the mathematician, below intellect (νοῦς); Aristotle uses the word more widely, of any science that reasons. The modern version says "uses thought".
!note "No demonstration of substance": a definition is not proved but grasped or shown; the Posterior Analytics (II.3-10) argues it at length. The sciences take what their subject is, and that it is, as given; the science of being as being examines both.

@p
@greek ἐπεὶ δὲ καὶ ἡ φυσικὴ ἐπιστήμη τυγχάνει οὖσα περὶ γένος τι τοῦ ὄντος
Now natural science too happens to be about one kind of being: it is about the kind of substance that has the principle of its motion and rest in itself. So clearly it is neither practical nor productive. In things made, the principle is in the maker, either intellect, or art, or some power; in things done, it is in the doer, the choice: for what is done and what is chosen are the same. So if every thought is either practical, or productive, or theoretical, natural science must be some theoretical science; but theoretical about the kind of being that can be moved, and about substance that, by its account, is for the most part not separable on its own.
!flag viz. will, for that which is done and that | Ross's "will" renders προαίρεσις, "choice", the deliberate choice that Aristotle sets at the head of action (Nicomachean Ethics III.2-3); Taylor says "free-will". Both read a later idea of the will into the word; the modern version says "choice", as in Book Δ (V.1).
!flag only about that kind of substance which in respect of its definition is for the most part not separable from matter | The Greek is compressed: "about substance that is according to its account, for the most part, as not separable only". Ross supplies "from matter", which the sense needs: the natural scientist defines things, for the most part, together with their matter, as the next lines show with "snub". Taylor reads the words otherwise ("known through definition, and has not alone a separate subsistence").

@p
@greek δεῖ δὲ τὸ τί ἦν εἶναι καὶ τὸν λόγον πῶς ἐστὶ μὴ λανθάνειν
We must not fail to notice how what it is to be, and the account, stand: without this, inquiry does nothing. Of things defined, and of the "what is it" of things, some are like the snub and some like the concave. These differ in that the snub is taken together with its matter (the snub is a concave nose), while concavity is without perceptible matter. If, then, all natural things are said in the way the snub is (nose, eye, face, flesh, bone, and animal in general; leaf, root, bark, and plant in general; for the account of none of them is without motion, but always has matter), it is clear how we must look for and define what a thing is in natural things, and why it belongs to the student of nature to study some of the soul too: as much of it as is not without matter. That natural science is theoretical, then, is plain from this.
!flag understand in what manner the formal cause and definition | Taylor's "formal cause" renders τὸ τί ἦν εἶναι, "what it is to be", which the modern version says throughout this work (Ross: "essence"). The name "formal cause" is the later schools'.
!note The snub (σιμόν) is Aristotle's standing example of a thing whose definition must name its matter: snubness is concavity in a nose. Book Ζ (chapters 5 and 10 to 11) returns to it. On the Soul (I.1, 403a-b) draws the same line: the student of nature defines anger as a boiling of the blood about the heart, the dialectician as a desire to hurt back.

@p
@greek ὅτι μὲν οὖν ἡ φυσικὴ θεωρητική ἐστι
But mathematics is theoretical too. Whether its objects are unmoved and separate is not clear at present; but it is clear that some parts of mathematics study their objects as unmoved and as separate. If there is something eternal, unmoved and separate, plainly knowing it belongs to a theoretical science: not, however, to natural science (for natural science is about certain things that move), nor to mathematics, but to a science prior to both. Natural science is about things that are separate but not unmoved; some parts of mathematics are about things unmoved but probably not separate, rather as in matter; the first science is about things both separate and unmoved. All causes must be eternal, but these above all: for they are the causes of the divine things that are manifest to us. So there will be three theoretical philosophies, mathematics, natural science and theology; for it is not unclear that if the divine is present anywhere, it is present in a nature of this kind, and the most honourable science must be about the most honourable kind. The theoretical sciences, then, are more to be chosen than the others, and this one more than the other theoretical sciences.
!flag what we may call theology | The Greek says simply "theology" (θεολογική), with no "what we may call". Ross's hedge is his own. Aristotle names the first philosophy theology here and again in Book Κ (XI.7); for the Platonists it was the name of the whole summit of the course, and Proclus called his own work on the gods the Elements of Theology and the Platonic Theology.
!flag causes of so much of the divine as appears to us | Ross's footnote takes "the divine things that are manifest to us" to be the movements of the heavenly bodies, which the unmoved causes of Book Λ (XII.8) move; Taylor keeps the Greek's "the phaenomena of things divine". The Platonists read the line of the visible gods, the stars, whose causes are the intelligible gods.
!note "Separate" (χωριστόν): able to be on its own, not in something else. Natural things are separate but move; mathematical objects are unmoved but are not on their own, only in thought apart from the bodies they are in; the objects of first philosophy are both. Whether there are such, Book Λ (XII.6-8) argues.

@p
@greek ἀπορήσειε γὰρ ἄν τις πότερόν ποθʼ ἡ πρώτη φιλοσοφία καθόλου ἐστὶν
One might raise the puzzle whether first philosophy is universal, or about some one kind and some one nature. Not even the mathematical sciences are all alike in this: geometry and astronomy are about a certain nature, while universal mathematics is common to all. If there is no other substance besides those formed by nature, natural science would be the first science. But if there is some unmoved substance, the science of it is prior, and is first philosophy, and universal in this way, because it is first. And it would belong to this science to study being as being, both what it is and what belongs to it as being.
!note The answer joins the science of being as being of Book Γ with the theology of Book Λ: the science of the first substance is universal because it is first, since all other things are called beings by their relation to substance, and substances to the first. The Platonists made the same move, from the One that is first to the One that is the cause of all.

@ch Metaph.VI.2 | Being by accident: no science of it
Being is said in many ways: by accident, as true, in the figures of the categories, potentially and actually. First, being by accident. No science deals with it, for it is endless and almost a mere name: so Plato was right that sophistry deals with what is not. Its cause is that some things are always or of necessity, and others only for the most part: what is neither always nor for the most part is an accident, and its cause is matter, which can be otherwise. Every science is of what is always or for the most part; so there is no science of the accident.

@p
Being, said simply, is said in many ways. One of them, we saw, was being by accident; another, being as true, with not being as the false. Besides these there are the figures of the categories, as the what, the of-what-kind, the how-much, the where, the when, and anything else that signifies in this way; and again, besides all these, being potentially and actually. Since, then, being is said in many ways, we must first say of being by accident that there is no study of it. A sign of this is that no science troubles itself about it, practical, productive or theoretical. The one who makes a house does not make all the things that happen to go with the house as it comes to be: they are endless. Nothing prevents the house once made from being pleasant to some, harmful to others, useful to others, and different, one might say, from everything there is; and the art of building produces none of these. In the same way the geometer does not study the attributes that belong to figures in this way, nor whether "triangle" and "triangle with angles equal to two right angles" are different.
!flag nor whether a wooden triangle | Taylor's "wooden" is not in the Greek, which asks only whether "triangle" and "triangle having two right angles" are different: whether a thing and that thing with an attribute are the same, a sophist's question. His text, or his reading of it, had something else.
!note "We saw": in Book Δ (V.7), on the ways being is said. The four ways named here are the plan of the books that follow: being by accident and as true are set aside in this book; being in the categories, above all substance, is Books Ζ and Η; potency and actuality Book Θ.

@p
@greek καὶ τοῦτʼ εὐλόγως συμπίπτει
And this happens with good reason: the accident is, as it were, a mere name. That is why Plato, in a way, was not wrong to set sophistry in the region of what is not. For the sophists' arguments are about the accident, one might say, more than about anything: whether the musical and the literate are different or the same, and the musical Coriscus and Coriscus; and whether everything that is, but not always, has come to be, so that if someone who was musical has become literate, he has also, being literate, become musical; and all the other arguments of this kind. For the accident appears to be something close to what is not. This is clear also from such arguments as these: of things that are in another way there is coming to be and passing away, but of things that are by accident there is not. Still, we must say further, as far as we can, what the nature of the accident is and from what cause it is; for at the same time it will perhaps be clear why there is no science of it.
!flag whether ‘ musical ’ and ‘lettered’ are different | Ross's "lettered" is γραμματικός, the one who knows his letters, who can read and write: "literate". The modern version says "literate".
!note Plato's Sophist (254a) places the sophist "in the darkness of what is not", where he is hard to see; the Stranger's hunt for him ends with the account of not being as difference. Coriscus is Aristotle's stock example of a particular man, perhaps the Coriscus of Scepsis who was with him at Assos.

@p
@greek ἐπεὶ οὖν ἐστὶν ἐν τοῖς οὖσι τὰ μὲν ἀεὶ ὡσαύτως ἔχοντα
Since among the things that are, some are always in the same state and of necessity (not necessity in the sense of force, but the necessity we mean when we say a thing cannot be otherwise), and others are not of necessity nor always, but for the most part, this is the principle and this the cause of there being an accident: for what is neither always nor for the most part, this we call an accident. If there is wintry, cold weather in the dog days, we say that this happened by accident, but not if there is stifling heat; for the one is always or for the most part, the other not. It is an accident that a man is pale (for this is neither always nor for the most part), but it is not by accident that he is an animal. It is an accident that the builder makes someone healthy, because it is not the builder's nature to do this but the doctor's: the builder happened to be a doctor. And a cook, aiming at pleasure, might make something healthy, but not by the art of cooking: so we say it happened by accident; and there is a way in which he makes it, but simply he does not. For of other things there are sometimes powers that produce them, but of these there is no art and no definite power; for of things that are or come to be by accident, the cause too is by accident.
!flag a confectioner, aiming at giving pleasure | Ross's "confectioner" is ὀψοποιός, the cook who prepares savoury dishes; Plato's Gorgias (464d-465b) sets the cook's knack, aimed at pleasure, against medicine, aimed at health. The modern version says "cook", as Taylor does.
!flag sometimes tend to be produced by alien potencies | The Greek, which the 1924 text brackets in part ("sometimes"), says only that "of other things there are powers that produce them": of what is not by accident there is a definite power or art that makes it, of the accident none. Ross's "alien potencies" reads the line otherwise.
