!note "The dog days" (ἐπὶ κυνί): the hottest weeks of summer, when the Dog Star rises with the sun; Taylor has "when the sun is in Canis".

@p
@greek ὥστʼ ἐπεὶ οὐ πάντα ἐστὶν ἐξ ἀνάγκης
So, since not everything is or comes to be of necessity and always, but most things for the most part, there must be being by accident. The pale man, for instance, is musical neither always nor for the most part; but since this sometimes happens, it will be by accident (otherwise everything will be of necessity). So the cause of the accident will be the matter, which can be otherwise than it is for the most part. We must take as our starting point this question: is there nothing that is neither always nor for the most part? Or is that impossible? There is, then, something besides these, which falls out either way, and by accident. But is there the for-the-most-part, and does the always belong to nothing, or are there some eternal things?
!note Matter as the cause of the accident: what is for the most part fails sometimes because the matter can be otherwise; in what has no matter, or none that can change, nothing happens by accident. The Platonists said the same of the receptacle of the Timaeus (52a-53a), the "wandering cause".

@p
@greek περὶ μὲν οὖν τούτων ὕστερον σκεπτέον
We must look into these questions later; but that there is no science of the accident is plain. For every science is either of what is always or of what is for the most part: how else will one learn it or teach it to another? The thing must be determined by the always or the for-the-most-part: for instance, that honey-water does good to someone with a fever for the most part. But science will not be able to say what falls outside this, when it does not, for instance "at the new moon": for that too, what happens at the new moon, is either always or for the most part; but the accident is outside these. What the accident is, then, from what cause it is, and that there is no science of it, has been said.
!flag This must be considered later | Ross's words are right; "later" is Book Λ (XII.6-8), where Aristotle argues that there are eternal things, the unmoved movers and the heavens, and so that not everything is only for the most part.
!note The physician's rule is a rule for the most part, and the exception is not a further rule: if it were ("at the new moon"), it too would be a rule, and the accident is what no rule covers.

@ch Metaph.VI.3 | The causes of accidents
There are principles and causes that come to be and pass away without a process of coming to be and passing away. Otherwise everything would be of necessity: trace any future event back through its conditions ("he will die by violence if he goes out, and he will go out if he is thirsty ...") and you reach something present or past, which is already so. The chain stops at a starting point that has no further cause; this is the principle of what falls out either way. What kind of cause it is must be looked into.

@p
That there are principles and causes that can come to be and pass away without being in process of coming to be and passing away is plain. For if this were not so, everything would be of necessity, if what comes to be and passes away must have some cause that is not its cause by accident. Will this be, or not? It will, if this other thing happens; if not, not. And that other thing will, if something else does. In this way, plainly, as time is always taken away from a limited time, one will arrive at the present. So this man will die by violence if he goes out; and he will go out if he is thirsty; and he will be thirsty if something else happens; and so one will come to what is now present, or to something that has already happened. For instance, he will go out if he is thirsty; and he will be thirsty if he is eating something sharp; and this either is so or is not. So he will die, or not die, of necessity. Likewise, if one jumps back to the past, the argument is the same: for this, I mean what has happened, is already present in something. Everything that will be, then, will be of necessity: the living, for instance, will die, since something has already come about, such as contraries in the same body.
!flag if he has eaten fast meat | The Greek is δριμέα, "sharp" or "pungent" food, which makes one thirsty; Ross says "something pungent". Taylor's words, as the scan has them, say something else.
!note The argument is close to On Interpretation, chapter 9, on tomorrow's sea battle (the Workshop's modern English of it is on the same shelf): if every event had a cause that made it necessary, all would be of necessity, and deliberating would be idle.

@p
@greek ἀλλʼ εἰ νόσῳ ἢ βίᾳ, οὔπω
But whether by disease or by violence is not yet so, but only if this other thing happens. Clearly, then, the process goes back to a certain starting point, and this does not go back to anything further. This, then, will be the principle of what falls out either way, and nothing else is the cause of its coming to be. But to what kind of principle and what kind of cause such a tracing back leads, whether to matter, or to that for the sake of which, or to what moved it, must be looked into above all.
!flag The events A. seems to be thinking of are those which he would ascribe to free will | Ross's note reads the starting points that have no cause as the acts of a free will, such as eating something sharp. The Greek does not say so here; it leaves open whether the starting point is matter, an end or a mover. The Platonists, after Plotinus (III.1-3), placed such starting points in the soul's own choice, within providence.
!note Ross's 1924 text brackets "by disease or" in the first paragraph, where the Greek, as Perseus gives it, still has the words in brackets; this line shows why: what is settled is that the living will die, but not yet how.

@ch Metaph.VI.4 | Being as true, and what remains
Being as true and not being as false lie in combining and dividing: the true affirms what is combined and denies what is divided. Truth and falsity are not in things but in thought, and about simple things and what a thing is not even in thought. So being as true, like being by accident, is set aside: the one's cause is indefinite, the other's an affection of thought, and neither shows a nature of being outside the rest. We must look for the causes and principles of being itself, as being.

@taylor 3
@p
About being by accident, then, let this be enough: it has been sufficiently marked off. Being as true, and not being as false, depend on combining and dividing, and the two together on the division of a contradiction. For the true has the affirmation where the thing is combined and the denial where it is divided, and the false has the contradictory of this division. How it comes about that we think things together or apart is another question (by "together" and "apart" I mean so that there is not a succession but one thing comes to be). For falsity and truth are not in things, as though the good were true and the bad false straight off, but in thought; and about simple things and the what-it-is of things they are not even in thought.
!flag while with regard to simple concepts and essences falsity and truth do not exist even in thought | Ross's "simple concepts and essences" renders τὰ ἁπλᾶ καὶ τὰ τί ἐστιν, "simple things and the what-it-is": things without parts, which thought does not combine but simply touches or misses. Book Θ (IX.10) explains how there is truth of these without falsehood, by contact; the modern version says "simple things".
!note "The good were true and the bad false": it is not that some things are true and others false in themselves, as some are good and others bad; truth is in the statement that combines or divides as the things are combined or divided.

@p
@greek ἐπεὶ δὲ ἡ συμπλοκή ἐστιν καὶ ἡ διαίρεσις ἐν διανοίᾳ
What we must study about being and not being in this sense must be looked at later. But since the combining and the dividing are in thought, not in the things, and being in this sense is a different being from the beings in the proper sense (for thought joins or separates either what a thing is, or that it is of a kind, or of a quantity, or something else), being by accident and being as true must be set aside. The cause of the one is indefinite, and of the other an affection of thought; and both concern the remaining kind of being, and do not show any nature of being that is outside it. So let these be set aside, and let us look into the causes and principles of being itself, as being. (It was clear, where we marked off how many ways each thing is said, that being is said in many ways.)
!note The last sentence, which the 1924 text brackets, sends the reader back to Book Δ (V.7), and leads into the first words of Book Ζ, "Being is said in many ways". "Later": Book Θ, chapter 10.

@treatise Metaph.VII | Metaphysics, Book Ζ (VII)
Book Ζ is the centre of the Metaphysics, and the hardest book in it: the inquiry into substance (οὐσία). Being is said in many ways, but first of all of substance, so that the old question "what is being?" is the question "what is substance?" (chapter 1). Bodies are agreed to be substances; whether there are others, as Plato's Forms and the objects of mathematics, is the question (2). Substance is said of four things above all: the underlying thing, what it is to be, the universal and the genus. As the underlying thing, matter would seem to be substance, which cannot be (3). The book then looks at what it is to be, and finds it belongs first to substances, and that each primary thing is one with what it is to be (4 to 6); it turns to coming to be, by nature, art and chance, and shows that the form is not made, nor the matter, but the compound (7 to 9); it asks how the parts of a definition stand to the parts of the thing, and which parts are prior (10 and 11), and what makes a definition one (12). Then it shows that no universal is substance, and so that the Forms, taken as separate universals, cannot be substances, nor can anything be made of substances present in it actually (13 to 16). Last, beginning again, it finds substance to be the cause: the form, by which the matter is some definite thing (17).

@ch Metaph.VII.1 | Being is said first of substance
Being is said in many ways, but first of what a thing is, which signifies substance; other things are called beings because they are quantities, qualities or affections of what is in this way. Only substance is separate; it is first in account, in knowledge and in time. So the question that has always been asked, what being is, is the question what substance is.

@p
Being is said in many ways, as we marked off earlier in the work on how many ways things are said. It signifies, on the one hand, what a thing is and this something; on the other, of what kind, or how much, or each of the other things predicated in this way. While being is said in so many ways, it is plain that the first of them is what a thing is, which signifies the substance. When we say of what kind a thing is, we say it is good or bad, not three cubits long or a man; but when we say what it is, we do not say pale or hot or three cubits long, but a man or a god. The other things are called beings because they are, some, quantities of what is in this way, others qualities of it, others affections of it, and others something else. So one might even raise the puzzle whether walking, being healthy and sitting each signify a being, and likewise with any other such thing. For none of them is by nature in itself, or able to be separated from the substance; rather, if anything, it is what walks, what sits and what is healthy that is among the beings. These appear more to be, because there is something definite underlying them (this is the substance and the particular), which shows in such a predicate: "good" or "sitting" is not said without it. Clearly, then, it is because of the substance that each of those others is. So what is primarily, and is not something but is simply, would be the substance.
!flag the individual thing, and in another sense | Ross's "the individual" renders τόδε τι, "this something", "a this": what can be pointed at as one definite thing, a man or a horse, the mark of substance in the Categories (3b10-23). Taylor says "this particular thing". The modern version keeps the Greek's "this something".
!flag But this is essence, and that which is particular | Taylor's "essence" is οὐσία, "substance", throughout his Metaphysics; Ross and the modern version say "substance", and keep "what it is to be" for τὸ τί ἦν εἶναι, which Ross calls "essence". Reading Taylor beside this book, put "substance" for his "essence".
!note "Earlier", "in the work on how many ways things are said": Book Δ, chapter 7, on being. "A man or a god": the answer to "what is it?" names a substance.

@p
@greek πολλαχῶς μὲν οὖν λέγεται τὸ πρῶτον
"First" is said in many ways; yet substance is first in every way, in account, in knowledge and in time. Of the other things predicated none is separate; substance alone is. And in account it is first: for in the account of each thing the account of its substance must be present. And we think we know each thing most of all when we know what it is, what man is or fire, rather than of what kind it is, or how much, or where; since we know each of these too only when we know what the how-much or the of-what-kind is. Indeed, the question that was asked long ago, is asked now and always, and is always a puzzle, what being is, is the question what substance is. It is this that some say is one, others more than one, and some limited in number, others unlimited. So we too must study, above all and first and, one might say, only, what that which is in this way is.
!flag in definition, knowledge, time, and nature | Taylor adds "nature" to the three ways in which substance is first: the Greek has three, "in account, in knowledge and in time". "First by nature" is another way of being prior (Book Δ, V.11), but it is not named here.
!note "Some say it is one, others more": the old thinkers of Book Α, the Milesians and Parmenides who said being is one, Empedocles and Anaxagoras who said many; their question, Aristotle says, was always the question of substance.

@ch Metaph.VII.2 | Which things are substances: the opinions
Bodies are thought to be substances most plainly: animals, plants, the elements, the heaven and the stars. Some think the limits of bodies, surfaces, lines, points and units, are substances more than bodies; others posit eternal substances besides the sensible: Plato the Forms and the objects of mathematics, Speusippus more kinds, each from its own principle, others the Forms and the numbers as one nature. We must look into which is right, after first sketching what substance is.

@p
Substance is thought to belong most plainly to bodies. So we say that animals and plants and their parts are substances, and the natural bodies, such as fire, water, earth and each such thing, and all that are either parts of these or made of these, either of some of them or of all, such as the heaven and its parts, stars, moon and sun. But whether these alone are substances, or others also; or some of these, and others also; or none of these but some others, we must look into. Some think the limits of body, surface, line, point and unit, are substances, and more so than the body and the solid.
!note The limits of body as substances: the view of some Pythagoreans and Platonists, which Book Β (III.5) set out as one of its puzzles, and Book Μ (XIII.2) answers.

@p
@greek ἔτι παρὰ τὰ αἰσθητὰ οἱ μὲν οὐκ οἴονται εἶναι οὐδὲν τοιοῦτον
Again, some think there is nothing of this kind besides the sensible things; others think there are more, and that they are more fully, being eternal. So Plato posits the Forms and the objects of mathematics as two kinds of substance, and as a third the substance of sensible bodies; and Speusippus makes even more kinds of substance, beginning from the One, and principles for each kind, one for numbers, another for magnitudes, then another for soul; and in this way he extends the kinds of substance. Some say that the Forms and the numbers have the same nature, and that the other things follow on them, lines and planes, down to the substance of the heaven and the sensible things. About these matters, then, we must look into what is said well and what not, and which things are substances; whether there are any besides the sensible substances or not, and how these are; and whether there is any separate substance, and why and how, or none, besides the sensible ones. But first we must sketch what substance is.
!flag until we come to the substance of the material universe | Ross's "material universe" renders τοῦ οὐρανοῦ, "the heaven": the visible heaven, its spheres and stars, which these thinkers set at the bottom of their order of substances, above the sensible things. The modern version says "the heaven".
!note Speusippus, Plato's nephew, who led the Academy after him; Book Λ (XII.10) calls his world "episodic", a series of kinds with no link between them. "Some say that the Forms and the numbers have the same nature": Xenocrates, who led the Academy after Speusippus. The Platonists of late antiquity, Syrianus and Proclus, answered these chapters in their commentaries on Books Μ and Ν.

@ch Metaph.VII.3 | Substance as the underlying thing; matter
Substance is said of four things above all: what it is to be, the universal, the genus, and the underlying thing. The underlying thing is what other things are said of, while it is not said of anything. In one way the matter is this, in another the form, in a third the compound. But if substance were only the underlying thing, matter would be substance, since when all else is stripped away it alone is left; and that is impossible, since being separate and a this belong to substance above all. The compound is posterior and plain; we must look at the form, the hardest, beginning from the sensible substances, since we learn by going from what is better known to us to what is better known by nature.

@p
Substance is said, if not in more ways, at least in four above all: what it is to be, the universal and the genus are thought to be the substance of each thing, and fourth of these, the underlying thing. The underlying thing is that of which the other things are said, while it is not itself said of anything else. So we must first mark off what this is; for the first underlying thing is thought to be substance most of all. In one way the matter is said to be such a thing, in another the shape, and in a third what is made of these. By the matter I mean, for instance, the bronze; by the shape, the figure of its form; and by what is made of these, the statue, the whole. So if the form is prior to the matter, and more a being, it will be prior to what is made of both too, for the same reason.
!flag For the formal cause, universal, and genus | Taylor's "formal cause" renders τὸ τί ἦν εἶναι, "what it is to be", as in Book Ε (VI.1). The four candidates are the plan of the book: the underlying thing (chapter 3), what it is to be (4 to 12), the universal and the genus (13 to 16).
!flag by the shape the plan of its form | Ross's "plan of its form" renders τὸ σχῆμα τῆς ἰδέας, "the figure of its form", the outline the bronze takes. Here μορφή, "shape", and ἰδέα are used of the form in the thing, not of a Platonic Form; the modern version says "form" in lower case.

@p
@greek νῦν μὲν οὖν τύπῳ εἴρηται τί ποτʼ ἐστὶν ἡ οὐσία
We have now said in outline what substance is: that it is what is not said of an underlying thing, but what the other things are said of. But we must not leave it at this, for it is not enough. The statement itself is unclear, and further, the matter becomes substance. For if this is not substance, what else is escapes us: when the other things are stripped away, nothing appears to remain. The rest are affections, products and powers of bodies, while length, breadth and depth are quantities, not substances (for the quantity is not a substance); rather, what these belong to first, that is substance. But when length, breadth and depth are taken away, we see nothing left, unless there is something that is bounded by them; so that to those who look at it in this way the matter must appear to be the only substance. By matter I mean what in itself is said to be neither something, nor of a quantity, nor any of the other things by which being is marked off. For there is something of which each of these is predicated, whose being is different from that of each of the categories (for the others are predicated of the substance, and this of the matter); so that the last thing is in itself neither something nor of a quantity nor anything else. Nor is it the denials of these, for these too will belong to it by accident.
!flag matter is only to be perceived by an adulterated reason | These words are not Aristotle's, nor is the long passage around them, on matter as darkness to the soul, "a flying mockery", "an image in an image", and on bodies' falling as a defect of being: they belong to a note of Taylor's, drawn from Plotinus (II.4.10-12 and III.6.7-12) and from Simplicius on the Physics, which the scan of his page reads into the middle of the chapter. "An adulterated reason" is the "bastard reasoning" of Plato's Timaeus (52b), by which the receptacle is grasped.
!note Stripping away (περιαίρεσις): take from a body its affections, then its dimensions, and what is left, if anything, is the matter, which in itself is nothing definite. The Platonists found here the matter of the Timaeus, the receptacle that has no form of its own (50b-51b).

@p
@greek ἐκ μὲν οὖν τούτων θεωροῦσι συμβαίνει οὐσίαν εἶναι τὴν ὕλην
For those who look at it from this point of view, then, it follows that matter is substance. But that is impossible: for being separate and being a this are thought to belong to substance most of all. So the form, and what is made of both, would be thought to be substance more than the matter. The substance made of both, I mean of the matter and the shape, may be set aside: it is posterior, and plain. The matter too is plain in a way. We must look into the third, for it is the most puzzling.
!note "The third": the form. The compound (the statue, Socrates) is plainly a substance, and posterior to its form and matter; the matter is known by analogy (Physics I.7). The inquiry into the form, "what it is to be", fills the rest of the book.

@p
@greek ὁμολογοῦνται δʼ οὐσίαι εἶναι τῶν αἰσθητῶν τινές
It is agreed that some of the sensible things are substances, so we must look among these first. For it is useful to pass on to what is better known. Learning comes about for everyone in this way, through what is less knowable by nature to what is more knowable. And just as in actions the task is to start from what is good for each and make what is good without qualification good for each, so here the task is to start from what is better known to oneself and make what is knowable by nature known to oneself. What is known and first to each of us is often known only faintly, and has little or nothing of being; but still, from what is poorly known, yet known to oneself, one must try to know what is knowable without qualification, passing, as has been said, through these very things.
!flag it is an advantage to advance to that which is more intelligible | Ross's "intelligible" renders γνώριμον, "known", "knowable": what is better known by nature against what is better known to us. "Intelligible" would be νοητόν, the object of intellect, a Platonic word with another sense; the modern version says "better known".
!note The lines from "For it is useful to pass on to what is better known" to the end of the chapter (1029b3-12) stand in Bekker's text inside chapter 4, after its first sentence, where they break the thread; Ross's 1924 text, which the Workshop gives, moves them here, where they explain why we begin from the sensible substances. Taylor has them at the head of his chapter 4.

@ch Metaph.VII.4 | What it is to be: first of substances
What it is to be, for each thing, is what it is said to be in itself: being you is not being musical. Not everything in itself, either: the account of what it is to be is the one in which the thing itself is not present. Is there a what-it-is-to-be of a compound such as "pale man"? Strictly, only of what is a this, and so a definition only of a primary thing, the species of a genus; the others have an account but not a definition. Or rather, definition and what it is to be are said in many ways, like being: primarily and simply of substances, and in a secondary way of the others.

@p
Since at the beginning we marked off in how many ways we determine substance, and one of these was thought to be what it is to be, we must study it. First, let us say some things about it in the manner of argument about words. What it is to be, for each thing, is what it is said to be in itself. Being you is not being musical: for you are not musical in virtue of yourself. What it is to be you, then, is what you are in virtue of yourself. Nor is it all of this: not what is in itself in the way that pale belongs to a surface, because being a surface is not being pale. Nor yet is it what is made of both, being a pale surface, because "surface" is added. The account, then, in which the thing itself is not present, but which says it, this is the account of what it is to be, for each thing. So if being a pale surface is being a smooth surface, being pale and being smooth are one and the same.
!flag let us make some abstract linguistic remarks | Ross's "abstract linguistic" renders λογικῶς, "logically", in the manner of the arguments about words and accounts that the dialectician uses, as against arguments from the nature of the thing (φυσικῶς). Taylor says "logically". The modern version says "in the manner of argument about words".
!flag The very nature of a thing, therefore, is that which a thing is said to be | Taylor's "the very nature of a thing" renders τὸ τί ἦν εἶναι, "what it is to be", the phrase this book turns on (Ross: "essence"). Literally "the what-it-was-to-be": what it is for this thing to be at all, which its definition states.
!note "Being you", "being musical": Aristotle's idiom is "the being for you" (τὸ σοὶ εἶναι), "the being for a musical thing". The pale surface example: if "pale" were defined as "smooth" (as Democritus said colour is), the account of being a pale surface would give what it is to be pale, not what it is to be a surface, since "surface" is repeated in it.

@p
@greek ἐπεὶ δʼ ἔστι καὶ κατὰ τὰς ἄλλας κατηγορίας σύνθετα
But since there are compounds in the other categories too (for there is something underlying each of them, as for quality, quantity, the when, the where and motion), we must look into whether there is an account of what it is to be for each of them, and whether these too have a what-it-is-to-be: for a pale man, for instance. Let its name be "cloak". What is being a cloak? But this, someone will say, is not among the things said in themselves either. Or rather, "not in itself" is said in two ways, and one of them is by addition and the other not. One is said because the thing being defined is added to something else, as if someone defining being pale gave the account of pale man; the other because something else is added to it, as if "cloak" meant pale man, and someone defined the cloak as pale. A pale man is pale indeed, but what it is to be him is not being pale.
!note "Cloak" (ἱμάτιον) is a made-up name for the compound "pale man", to test whether a compound with a name of its own has a definition; Ross's English puts "X" in its place. A name alone does not make a definition, as the next paragraph says.

@p
@greek ἀλλὰ τὸ ἱματίῳ εἶναι ἆρά ἐστι τί ἦν εἶναί τι
But is being a cloak a what-it-is-to-be at all? Or not? For what it is to be is just what something is; but when one thing is said of another, it is not just what a this is: the pale man, for instance, is not just what a this is, since being a this belongs to substances only. So there is a what-it-is-to-be of those things whose account is a definition. And there is a definition not wherever a name means the same as an account (for then all accounts would be definitions, since there could be a name for any account whatever, so that even the Iliad would be a definition), but where it is an account of something primary; and primary things are those that are said without one thing being said of another. So nothing that is not a species of a genus will have a what-it-is-to-be, but only these; for these are thought to be said not by participation, nor as an affection, nor as an accident. Of everything else too there will be an account of what it means, if it has a name, that this belongs to that, or instead of a simple account a more exact one; but there will be no definition, nor what it is to be.
!flag for in these the subject is not thought to participate in the attribute | The Greek says only that species "are not said by participation", not that "the subject" participates "in the attribute"; Ross's footnote sends the reader to 1037b14-21, where Aristotle argues that a genus is not in its differentiae by participation. Taylor keeps the bare word: "not through participation".
!note "Species of a genus": a species, man or horse, is one thing, not one thing said of another; its genus and differentia are not two things combined, as "pale" and "man" are. The Iliad is one account by being continuous, one poem; but it is not one definition.

@p
@greek ἢ καὶ ὁ ὁρισμὸς ὥσπερ καὶ τὸ τί ἐστι πλεοναχῶς λέγεται
Or rather, definition, like the what-it-is, is said in several ways. For the what-it-is signifies, in one way, the substance and this something, and in another each of the things predicated, quantity, quality and the rest. Just as "is" belongs to all things, but not in like manner, but to the one primarily and to the others in a following way, so too the what-it-is belongs simply to the substance, and in a way to the others. For we might ask of a quality too what it is, so that quality too is among the things that have a what-it-is, but not simply; just as some say, in the manner of argument about words, that not being is, not simply but as not being, so too with quality.
!note "Some say that not being is": the Stranger of Plato's Sophist (256d-259b), who shows that not being is, as difference; Aristotle grants it, "in the manner of argument about words", not simply.

@p
@greek δεῖ μὲν οὖν σκοπεῖν καὶ τὸ πῶς δεῖ λέγειν περὶ ἕκαστον
We must look into how one ought to speak about each thing, but not more than into how things are. So now, since what is being said is clear, what it is to be too will likewise belong primarily and simply to substance, and then to the others, as the what-it-is does: not simply what it is to be, but what it is to be for a quality or a quantity. For we must say either that these are beings by an ambiguity, or by adding and taking away, as we say that the unknowable is known; since the right way is to say neither by an ambiguity nor in the same sense, but as "medical" is said, by reference to one and the same thing, without meaning one and the same thing, and yet not by an ambiguity either. A body, an operation and an instrument are called medical neither by an ambiguity nor in one sense, but by reference to one thing. But it makes no difference in which of these ways one wishes to speak of them. This is plain: that definition and what it is to be, primarily and simply, belong to substances. Not that they do not belong likewise to the others, but not primarily. For if we posit this, it is not necessary that there be a definition of whatever means the same as some account, but as some kind of account; and this is so if it is the account of one thing, not one by being continuous, like the Iliad, or by being bound together, but one in the ways "one" is said; and "one" is said as "being" is, and being signifies now a this, now a quantity, now a quality. So there will be an account and a definition even of pale man, but in another way than of pale and of substance.
!flag by making qualifications and abstractions | Ross's "abstractions" renders ἀφαιροῦντας, "taking away", as the mathematician's abstraction is called in Greek; here it is the opposite of "adding": a word is used of a quality with something added or taken away from its sense. The modern version says "adding and taking away".
!note "Medical" is the example of Book Γ (IV.2) for what is said "by reference to one thing" (πρὸς ἕν): neither the same in sense nor merely sharing a name. So "being" is said of all the categories by reference to substance, and so too definition and what it is to be.

@ch Metaph.VII.5 | No definition of things said with another
Things such as the snub, which is concavity in a nose, cannot be defined without what they belong to; their definitions are by addition, and say the same thing twice ("a snub nose" is a "concave nose nose"). So, strictly, only substance has a definition and a what-it-is-to-be; the others have them only in another way.

@p
There is a puzzle, if one denies that an account by addition is a definition: of what things that are not simple but coupled will there be a definition? For they must be made plain by addition. I mean, for instance, there is nose and concavity, and snubness, which is said of the two by one being in the other; and it is not by accident that either the concavity or the snubness is an affection of the nose, but in itself; nor as pale belongs to Callias or to man, because Callias, who happens to be a man, is pale, but as male belongs to animal and equal to quantity, and all the things said to belong in themselves. These are the things in which there is either the account or the name of the thing whose affection this is, and which cannot be made plain apart from it, as pale can be without man but female cannot without animal. So of these there is either no what-it-is-to-be or definition of any of them, or, if there is, it is in another way, as we have said.
!note "Coupled" (συνδεδυασμένα): things whose account must name another thing they are in, as female names animal, odd names number, snub names nose. "In itself" here is the second sense of Posterior Analytics I.4: the subject is in the account of the attribute.

@p
@greek ἔστι δὲ ἀπορία καὶ ἑτέρα περὶ αὐτῶν
There is another puzzle about them as well. If a snub nose and a concave nose are the same, the snub and the concave will be the same. But if not, because one cannot say "snub" without the thing of which it is an affection in itself (for the snub is concavity in a nose), then either one cannot say "snub nose", or the same thing will be said twice, "nose nose concave": for the snub nose will be a concave nose nose. So it is absurd that such things should have a what-it-is-to-be; otherwise it goes on without end, for in "snub nose nose" there will be yet another nose. Clearly, then, there is definition only of substance. For if there were also of the other categories, it would have to be by addition, as of the odd: for it is not without number, nor the female without animal. (By "by addition" I mean those in which it happens that the same thing is said twice, as in these.) And if this is true, there will be none of coupled things either, such as odd number; but it escapes notice that the accounts are not given exactly. If there are definitions of these too, either they are in another way, or, as was said, definition and what it is to be must be said in many ways; so that in one way there will be no definition of anything, and what it is to be will belong to nothing except substances, and in another way there will be. That definition, then, is the account of what it is to be, and that what it is to be belongs either to substances alone, or to them most of all, primarily and simply, is clear.
!flag in the definitions of quality and the odd | The Greek here is corrupt, and the 1924 text marks the word "of the quality" (ποιοῦ) with daggers; some editors read "of the odd" alone, as the next words, "it is not without number", require. Taylor translates the word as it stood.
!note The infinite regress: if "snub" means "concave nose", then "snub nose" means "concave nose nose", and the "snub" in it again unfolds into "concave nose", without end. So the snub has no definition in the strict sense; Book Ζ (chapters 10 and 11) and Book Η use it again, as the type of a form that cannot be defined without its matter.

@ch Metaph.VII.6 | Each thing and what it is to be: the same?
Is each thing the same as what it is to be? For things said by accident, no: the pale man is not the same as being a pale man. For things said in themselves, and primary, yes: if the good itself were other than being good, and so on, as some say of the Forms, there would be no knowledge of the one and no being of the other, and an endless series. So in primary things each thing and what it is to be are one and the same; and the sophists' puzzles about Socrates and being Socrates are solved the same way.

@taylor 5
@p
We must look into whether each thing and what it is to be are the same or different. This is of use for the inquiry into substance: each thing is thought to be nothing other than its own substance, and what it is to be is said to be the substance of each thing. In things said by accident, they would seem to be different: a pale man, for instance, is different from being a pale man. (For if they are the same, being a man and being a pale man are the same too; for a man and a pale man are the same, as they say, so that being a pale man and being a man are the same too. Or is it not necessary that things that are the same by accident be the same, since the extremes do not become the same in the same way? But perhaps this at least would seem to follow, that the extremes become the same by accident, as being pale and being musical; but it is thought not.)
!note The argument in parentheses: if a man is the same as a pale man, and each is the same as what it is to be it, then being a man would be the same as being a pale man. Aristotle answers that identity by accident does not carry over in this way; Alexander and Taylor supply words to fill out the sentence.

@p
@greek ἐπὶ δὲ τῶν καθʼ αὑτὰ λεγομένων ἆρʼ ἀνάγκη ταὐτὸ εἶναι
But in things said in themselves, must they be the same? As, for instance, if there are substances to which no other substances or natures are prior, such as some say the Forms are. For if the good itself and being good are different, and animal and being animal, and being and being a being, there will be other substances and natures and Forms besides those spoken of, and those others will be prior substances, if what it is to be is substance. And if they are cut off from one another, there will be no knowledge of the ones, and the others will not be beings (by "cut off" I mean if neither the good itself has being good, nor this has being good). For there is knowledge of each thing when we know what it is to be that thing; and with the good and the others it is likewise; so if being good is not good, neither is being a being a being, nor being one one. And either all what-it-is-to-be's are, or none are, likewise; so if being a being is not a being, neither is any of the others. Again, what does not have being good is not good. So the good and being good must be one, and the beautiful and being beautiful, and all things that are not said by virtue of something else, but in themselves and primary. This is enough if they are so, even if there are no Forms; and perhaps more so if there are Forms. (At the same time it is clear too that if there are Forms such as some say, the underlying thing will not be substance: for these must be substances, but not said of an underlying thing; for then they would be by participation.)
!flag If the essence of good is to be different from the Idea of good | Ross's "the Idea of good" renders αὐτὸ τὸ ἀγαθόν, "the good itself", Plato's phrase for the Form of the Good (Republic 507b, 532a-b); the Greek here does not say "Idea". Ross's "essence of good" is τὸ ἀγαθῷ εἶναι, "being good". The modern version says "the good itself" and "being good", and "Form" for ἰδέα.
!note The argument turns Plato's own principle against a separation: the Form, the good itself, must be what it is to be good, not something that has it; otherwise there would be a further "being good" prior to it. The Platonists agreed: for Plotinus (V.9.7-8) and Proclus (Elements of Theology, props. 167-169) each Form in intellect is one with its being, and intellect with its objects.

@p
@greek ἔκ τε δὴ τούτων τῶν λόγων ἓν καὶ ταὐτὸ
From these arguments, then, each thing itself and what it is to be are one and the same, and not by accident; and also because to know each thing is just to know what it is to be it; so that by setting out examples too both must be one. (But of what is said by accident, such as "the musical" or "the pale", it is not true to say that what it is to be and the thing itself are the same, because it signifies two things: both that to which pale belongs and the accident. So in a way what it is to be and the thing itself are the same, and in a way not: not the same as man or as pale man, but the same as the affection.) It would appear absurd, too, if one gave a name to each of the what-it-is-to-be's: for there would be yet another besides it, as besides what it is to be a horse there would be another what it is to be. And yet what prevents some things from being what-it-is-to-be's at once, if what it is to be is substance? But indeed not only are they one, but their account too is the same, as is clear from what has been said; for it is not by accident that being one and the one are one. Again, if it is something else, it will go on without end: for there will be the what-it-is-to-be of the one, and the one, so that the same argument will hold of those too. That in primary things and things said in themselves, being each thing and each thing are the same and one, is clear. And the sophists' refutations against this position are plainly solved by the same solution as the question whether Socrates and being Socrates are the same: for there is no difference either in the premises from which one would ask the question or in those from which one would succeed in solving it. How, then, what it is to be is the same as each thing, and how it is not, has been said.
!flag So that, from induction it | Taylor's "induction" renders ἔκθεσις, "setting out", the setting out of a case before one's eyes; it is not ἐπαγωγή, the leading on from examples. Ross says "the exhibition of instances", which is right. The modern version says "setting out examples".
!note "Being Socrates": Socrates himself is a compound of form and matter, not a primary thing; the sophists' question whether he is the same as "being Socrates" is answered as the question about "pale man" was: in one way yes, in another no.

@ch Metaph.VII.7 | Coming to be by nature, by art and by chance
Whatever comes to be comes to be by something, from something, and becomes something. Natural things come to be from matter, by a nature of the same form ("man begets man"). Things made come from art, power or thought: the form is in the soul of the maker, and the doctor thinks back from health to what he can do first, then makes forward; so in a way health comes from health, and the house from the house without matter. Something must be there first, the matter, which is a part; and we call a thing not "wood" but "wooden", because it comes from the matter as changed.

@taylor 6
@p
Of things that come to be, some come to be by nature, some by art, and some by chance; and everything that comes to be comes to be by something, from something, and becomes something. By "something" I mean in each of the categories: a this, or of a quantity, or of a quality, or somewhere. Natural comings to be are those of things whose coming to be is from nature: that from which they come is what we call matter; that by which, something that is by nature; and what they become, a man or a plant or something else of this kind, which we call substances most of all. All things that come to be, by nature or by art, have matter: for each of them can both be and not be, and this is the matter in each. In general, both that from which is nature, and that according to which is nature (for what comes to be has a nature, as a plant or an animal), and that by which is the nature said by way of the form, the nature of the same form; and this is in another. For man begets man.
!note "Man begets man" (ἄνθρωπος ἄνθρωπον γεννᾷ) is Aristotle's watchword for natural coming to be: the cause that moves is a thing of the same form as what comes to be, already actual. Book Λ (XII.3, 1070a27-30) repeats it against the Forms.

@p
@greek οὕτω μὲν οὖν γίγνεται τὰ γιγνόμενα διὰ τὴν φύσιν
This, then, is how things come to be through nature; the other comings to be are called makings. All makings are from art, or from a power, or from thought. Some of them come to be also by chance and by luck, much as among the things that come to be by nature: for there too the same things come to be both from seed and without seed. These we must look into later. From art come the things whose form is in the soul (by form I mean what it is to be each thing, and its first substance). For in a way even contraries have the same form: the substance of the privation is the opposite substance, as health of disease, for disease is the absence of health; and health is the account in the soul, the knowledge. The healthy thing comes to be when the doctor thinks thus: since health is this, if the body is to be healthy, this must be present, say evenness; and if this, heat; and he goes on thinking in this way until he brings it to what he himself can make last. Then the motion from this point, toward becoming healthy, is called making. So it turns out that in a way health comes from health, and the house from the house, the one with matter from the one without matter: for the art of medicine and the art of building are the form of health and of the house. By substance without matter I mean what it is to be.
!flag But health is reason in the soul and in science | Taylor's "reason" renders λόγος, the "account" of health in the doctor's soul, which is his knowledge of it; Ross says "formula". The modern version says "account", as in Book Δ.
!note "From seed and without seed": the old view, which Aristotle shares, that some small animals and plants come to be of themselves from putrefying matter. "Later": chapter 9.

@p
@greek τῶν δὴ γενέσεων καὶ κινήσεων ἡ μὲν νόησις καλεῖται
Of the comings to be and motions, one part is called thinking and the other making: what proceeds from the principle and the form is thinking, and what proceeds from the last step of the thinking is making. Each of the other, intermediate things comes to be likewise. I mean, for instance: if he is to be healthy, he must be made even. What, then, is being made even? This; and this will be if he is warmed. And what is this? This. And this is present potentially; and this is now in the doctor's power. What makes, then, and where the motion toward health starts, if it is from art, is the form in the soul; if by chance, it starts from whatever is the starting point of the making for the one who makes by art: as in healing perhaps the start is from warming (and this the doctor produces by rubbing). The heat in the body, then, is either a part of health, or something follows on it that is a part of health, either directly or through several steps; and this last is what makes the part of health. And so with the house (the stones, for instance) and the other things. So, as the saying is, it is impossible for anything to come to be if nothing is there before.
!note The doctor's reasoning runs back from the end to the first thing in his power, as deliberation does in the Nicomachean Ethics (III.3, 1112b11-24), like the analysis of a geometer; making then runs forward in the opposite order.

@p
@greek ὅτι μὲν οὖν τι μέρος ἐξ ἀνάγκης ὑπάρξει φανερόν
That some part must be there before, then, is plain: for the matter is a part (it is present in the thing, and it is what comes to be something). But is something in the account a part too? We say what bronze circles are in both ways: naming the matter, that it is bronze, and the form, that it is such a figure; and this is the genus in which it is first placed. The bronze circle, then, has its matter in its account. As for what things come to be from as matter, some, when they have come to be, are called not "that" but "that-en": the statue is not "stone" but "stony"; while the man who becomes healthy is not called by what he came from. The reason is that he comes to be from the privation and from the underlying thing, which we call the matter (as both the man and the sick one become healthy); but he is said to come to be from the privation more, as healthy from sick rather than from man. So the healthy one is not called sick, but man, and the man healthy. But where the privation is unclear and has no name, as in bronze of any shape, or in bricks and timber of a house, the thing seems to come to be from these as there from the sick. So, as there the thing is not called by what it came from, here too the statue is not called "wood" but by a change of word "wooden", and "brazen", not "bronze", and "stony", not "stone", and the house "bricken", not "bricks". For if one looks closely, one would not say simply either that the statue comes to be from wood or the house from bricks, because what a thing comes from must change, not remain. That is why it is said in this way.
!note "That-en" (ἐκείνινον): Greek, like English, makes adjectives of matter ("wooden", "stony"). Aristotle uses the idiom again in Book Θ (IX.7) for the relation of a thing to its matter: the box is not wood but wooden.

@ch Metaph.VII.8 | The form is not made; against the Forms as causes of coming to be
What the maker makes is not the matter, nor the form (the sphere), but the bronze sphere, the form in this matter; otherwise there would be an endless series of makings. So the form, or what it is to be, does not come to be; the compound does. There is no need of a Form apart, a sphere besides the spheres: the form signifies a "such", not a "this", and the begetter makes a "such" from a "this". In natural things man begets man, and the begetter is enough to cause the form in the matter; the whole, this form in this flesh and these bones, is Callias or Socrates, different by matter, the same in form.

@taylor 7
@p
Since what comes to be comes to be by something (by this I mean the source from which the coming to be starts), and from something (let this be not the privation but the matter: we have already marked off in what way we mean this), and becomes something (a sphere, a circle, or whatever it may be), then just as one does not make the underlying thing, the bronze, so neither does one make the sphere, except by accident, because the bronze sphere is a sphere and one makes that. For to make a this is to make a this out of the underlying thing in the full sense. I mean that to make the bronze round is not to make the round or the sphere, but something else, this form in another thing. For if one makes it, one would make it out of something else, for this was assumed: as one makes a bronze sphere, and does so in this way, that out of this, which is bronze, one makes this, which is a sphere. If, then, one makes this too itself, clearly one will make it in the same way, and the comings to be will go on without end. It is plain, then, that the form too, or whatever one should call the shape in the perceptible thing, does not come to be, and there is no coming to be of it, nor of what it is to be: for this is what comes to be in another thing, either by art or by nature or by a power. What one makes is a bronze sphere: one makes it out of bronze and sphere; one makes the form into this, and the result is a bronze sphere.
!note If the form were made, it would have to be made out of something, a matter of its own, and that compound's form would have to be made too, without end. So the form is not made, nor does it perish; it is in the thing, or not. Book Η (VIII.3, 5) and Λ (XII.3) return to this: forms are and are not without coming to be and passing away.

@p
@greek τοῦ δὲ σφαίρᾳ εἶναι ὅλως εἰ ἔσται γένεσις
If there is to be a coming to be of being a sphere in general, it will be something out of something. For what comes to be must always be divisible, and one part be this and another that: I mean one the matter and the other the form. If, then, a sphere is the figure whose surface is everywhere equally far from the centre, of this one part will be that in which what is made will be, and the other what is in that, and the whole will be what has come to be, as the bronze sphere. It is plain, then, from what has been said, that what is said as form or substance does not come to be, but the compound that is called after it does; and that in everything that comes to be there is matter, and one part is this and the other that.

@p
@greek πότερον οὖν ἔστι τις σφαῖρα παρὰ τάσδε
Is there, then, a sphere besides these spheres, or a house besides the bricks? Or rather, nothing would ever come to be, if it were so, as a this; but the form signifies a "such", and is not a this and definite; and the maker makes, and the begetter begets, a such out of a this; and when it has been begotten, it is a "this such". The whole, the this, Callias or Socrates, is like this bronze sphere here; and man and animal are like bronze sphere in general. It is plain, then, that the cause of the Forms, in the way some are accustomed to speak of the Forms, if they are some things besides the particulars, is of no use at least for comings to be and for substances; nor would they be, for these reasons at least, substances in themselves.
!flag It is evident, therefore, that forms themselves | The Greek says "the cause of the Forms" (ἡ τῶν εἰδῶν αἰτία), that is, the Forms as a cause, the cause the Platonists say the Forms are; Taylor makes it "forms themselves". Aristotle's point is that this cause is idle for coming to be.
!note "A such, not a this": the form is a kind, said of many, not a particular. Chapters 13 to 16 build on it: no universal is a substance. The Platonists answered that the Forms are causes not as particulars beside particulars, nor as universals in them, but as the patterns in the divine intellect by which the begetter itself is what it is (Syrianus on Book Μ, Proclus on the Parmenides).

@p
@greek ἐπὶ μὲν δή τινων καὶ φανερὸν ὅτι τὸ γεννῶν τοιοῦτον μὲν οἷον τὸ γεννώμενον
In some cases it is even plain that the begetter is of the same kind as what is begotten, though not the same, nor one in number, but one in form, as in natural things (for man begets man), unless something comes about against nature, as a horse begets a mule. (And these too are likewise: for what would be common to horse and ass, the nearest genus, has no name, but it would perhaps be both, like the mule.) So it is plain that there is no need to set up a Form as a pattern (for Forms would have been sought in these cases most of all, since these are substances most of all); the begetter is enough to make the thing and to be the cause of the form in the matter. And the whole, such a form in this flesh and in these bones, is Callias and Socrates; and they are different because of their matter (for that is different), but the same in form; for the form is indivisible.
!flag to introduce form as a paradigm | Taylor's "paradigm" is the Greek παράδειγμα carried over, "pattern", Plato's word for the Forms as the models that the maker looks to (Timaeus 28a-29b). Ross says "pattern", as the modern version does. Aristotle's point is that in nature the begetter is the pattern, already actual: man begets man.
!note "The form is indivisible" (ἄτομον τὸ εἶδος): the lowest species, man, cannot be divided into further species; Callias and Socrates differ only by matter. The later schools took this line as the root of the doctrine that matter is the principle of individuation.

@ch Metaph.VII.9 | Why some things come by chance; no form comes to be
Health can come by art or by chance, a house only by art, because in some things the matter can start the motion of itself and in others not. Everything comes to be, in a way, from something of the same name: natural things from their like, made things from the form in the art. Substance is the starting point of all coming to be, as of syllogisms. Not only the form of substance but those of the other categories do not come to be; but substance alone needs another substance, actual, before it.

@taylor 8
@p
One might raise the puzzle why some things come to be both by art and by chance, as health, and others not, as a house. The reason is that in some things the matter, which governs the coming to be in the making and in the coming to be of things from art, and in which some part of the thing is present, is such as to be moved by itself, and in others not; and of the former, some can be moved in this particular way and some cannot. For many things can be moved by themselves, but not in this particular way: dancing, for instance. Things whose matter is of this kind, such as stones, cannot be moved in this particular way except by something else; in that other way, yes. And so with fire. So some things will not exist without the one who has the art, and others will: for they will be moved by things that do not have the art, and that themselves can be moved by other things that do not have the art, or by a part of the thing.
!note A heap of stones cannot arrange itself into a house; but a body can warm itself, by rubbing or fever, and so come to health without a doctor. Chance is the art's work done by a cause that has not the art.

@p
@greek δῆλον δʼ ἐκ τῶν εἰρημένων καὶ ὅτι τρόπον τινὰ πάντα γίγνεται ἐξ ὁμωνύμου
It is clear from what has been said, too, that in a way all things come to be from something of the same name, as natural things do, or from a part of the same name (as the house from a house, as by intellect: for the art is the form), or from something that has a part, unless they come to be by accident; for the cause of the making, first and in itself, is a part. The heat in the motion produces heat in the body; and this is either health or a part of it, or a part of health follows on it, or health itself. That is why it is said to make health, because that makes health on which heat follows and to which it belongs. So, as in syllogisms, substance is the starting point of everything: syllogisms start from the what-it-is, and here comings to be do.
!flag in a sense everything is produced from (1) another individual which shares its name | Ross's words are right; Taylor's "from equivocals" renders ἐξ ὁμωνύμου, "from what has the same name", in the old logical sense of "homonym" (Categories 1a1): here a thing of the same name and form, as man from man. "Equivocal" now means ambiguous, the opposite of the point.
!note "The heat in the motion": the heat of the doctor's rubbing hand, Ross's note says, which makes heat in the body.

@p
@greek ὁμοίως δὲ καὶ τὰ φύσει συνιστάμενα τούτοις ἔχει
Things formed by nature are likewise. The seed makes as the things from art are made: for it has the form potentially, and that from which the seed comes is in a way of the same name (for we must not look for all cases to be like man from man: a woman too comes from a man), unless the offspring is a deformity; that is why a mule does not come from a mule. The things that come to be by chance, as there, are those whose matter can be moved also by itself with the motion that the seed gives it; those whose matter cannot be so moved cannot come to be in any other way than from parents of their own kind.
!flag only if the offspring is not an imperfect form | Ross's "imperfect form" renders πήρωμα, a deformity, a thing maimed or incomplete by nature: the mule, which cannot beget. The Generation of Animals (II.3, 737a27-28) calls even the female "as it were a deformed male", a view of Aristotle's that the modern version gives as he states it.

@p
@greek οὐ μόνον δὲ περὶ τῆς οὐσίας ὁ λόγος δηλοῖ τὸ μὴ γίγνεσθαι τὸ εἶδος
Not only about substance does the argument show that the form does not come to be; the argument is common to all the primary things likewise, quantity, quality and the other categories. As the bronze sphere comes to be, but not the sphere nor the bronze, and with bronze too, if it comes to be (for the matter and the form must always be there before), so it is with the what-it-is, and the quality and quantity and the other categories likewise. For it is not the quality that comes to be, but wood of that quality, nor the quantity, but wood or an animal of that size. But a mark peculiar to substance may be taken from this: there must be another substance already there, being in actuality, that makes it, as an animal if an animal comes to be; but a quality or a quantity need not be there before except potentially.
!flag having a subsistence in energy | Taylor's "energy" renders ἐντελεχείᾳ, "in actuality", "in its complete state" (Ross: "actual"); the word is Aristotle's coinage, from "having its end within". Taylor carries over the Greek ἐνέργεια for it, which means "being at work", not force. The modern version says "in actuality", as everywhere in this work.

@ch Metaph.VII.10 | The parts of the definition and the parts of the thing
Does the account of the whole contain the accounts of the parts? The circle's does not contain its segments, the syllable's contains its letters. "Part" is said in many ways: the matter is a part of the compound, not of the form. Parts of the account are prior to the whole; parts as matter are posterior. The soul is the substance by the account, prior to the body and its parts; man and horse, said universally, are compounds of this form and this matter taken universally. Particular things, sensible or intelligible, have no definition, but are known by thought or perception; matter in itself is unknowable, and is either sensible or intelligible.

@taylor 9
@p
Since a definition is an account, and every account has parts, and as the account is to the thing, so likewise the part of the account is to the part of the thing, the puzzle now arises whether the account of the parts must be present in the account of the whole or not. In some cases they appear to be present, and in others not. The account of the circle does not contain that of the segments, but the account of the syllable contains that of the letters; and yet the circle is divided into its segments just as the syllable is into its letters. Further, if the parts are prior to the whole, and the acute angle is a part of the right angle and the finger of the animal, the acute angle would be prior to the right and the finger to the man. But the latter are thought to be prior: for in account the former are said from them, and in being able to be without each other the latter are prior.
!note The puzzle sets two cases side by side: in a syllable the letters are parts of what it is ("ba" is b and a); in a circle the segments are not, since a circle is not defined by its halves. The answer will be that "part" is said of the form's parts and of the matter's.

@p
@greek ἢ πολλαχῶς λέγεται τὸ μέρος
Or rather, "part" is said in many ways, one of them being what measures a thing in quantity. But let that be set aside: what we must look into is the things of which the substance is made, as of parts. If, then, there is the matter, the form, and what is made of these, and the matter, the form and what is made of them are each substance, the matter is in one way called a part of something, and in another not, but only those things of which the account of the form is made. Flesh, for instance, is not a part of concavity (for it is the matter in which concavity comes to be), but it is a part of snubness. And the bronze is a part of the statue as compound, but not of the statue said as form (for one must name the form, and each thing as having form; what is material should never be named by itself). That is why the account of the circle does not contain that of the segments, while that of the syllable contains that of the letters: for the letters of the account are parts of the form and not matter, while the segments are parts as the matter on which the form comes; yet they are nearer to the form than the bronze is, when roundness comes to be in bronze. In a way not all the letters will be in the account of the syllable either, such as these letters in wax, or those in the air: for these too are already parts of the syllable as perceptible matter.
!note "Letters in the air": the sounds of the spoken syllable; "in wax": the letters scratched on a writing tablet. The syllable as a form is the pattern of its letters, not these particular marks or sounds.

@p
@greek καὶ γὰρ ἡ γραμμὴ οὐκ εἰ διαιρουμένη εἰς τὰ ἡμίση φθείρεται
For the line, if it perishes by being divided into halves, or the man into bones, sinews and flesh, is not for that reason made of these as of parts of its substance, but as of matter; they are parts of the compound, but no longer of the form and of what the account is of. That is why they are not in the accounts either. In some accounts, then, the account of such parts will be present, and in others it must not be, unless the account is of the thing taken together with its matter. For this is why some things are made of these as of principles into which they perish, and others are not. Those that are the form and the matter taken together, such as the snub or the bronze circle, perish into these, and the matter is a part of them; those not taken together with the matter, but without matter, whose accounts are of the form only, do not perish, either not at all or at least not in this way. So these are principles and parts of the former, but neither parts nor principles of the form. And that is why the clay statue perishes into clay, the sphere into bronze and Callias into flesh and bones, and further the circle into its segments: for there is a circle taken together with its matter. "Circle" is said by an ambiguity both of the circle simply and of the particular circles, because the particular ones have no name of their own.

@p
@greek εἴρηται μὲν οὖν καὶ νῦν τὸ ἀληθές
The truth has now been told; still, let us state it more clearly, taking it up again. The parts of the account, into which the account is divided, are prior, all or some of them. But the account of the right angle is not divided into the account of the acute; rather that of the acute into the right: for whoever defines the acute angle uses the right, since the acute is less than a right angle. The circle and the semicircle are likewise: the semicircle is defined by the circle, and the finger by the whole, for a finger is such a part of a man. So the parts that are as matter, into which a thing is divided as into matter, are posterior; those that are parts of the account and of the substance by the account are prior, all or some.

@p
@greek ἐπεὶ δὲ ἡ τῶν ζῴων ψυχή
And since the soul of animals (for this is the substance of the living thing) is the substance by the account, the form, and what it is to be for a body of this kind (at any rate each part, if it is well defined, will not be defined without its work, which it cannot do without perception), the parts of the soul are prior, all or some, to the compound animal, and likewise to each particular animal; and the body and its parts are posterior to this substance, and it is not the substance but the compound that is divided into these as into matter. So these parts are prior to the compound in a way, and in a way not, since they cannot even be when separated: it is not a finger in any state that is the finger of a living thing, but a dead finger is a finger only in name. Some are at the same time as the whole: those that are governing, in which the account and the substance are first present, the heart, say, or the brain, for it makes no difference which of them is such. But man and horse, and the things that are in this way said of particulars but universally, are not substance, but a compound of this account and this matter taken universally; and the particular, Socrates, is already made of the last matter; and likewise in the other cases.
!note The soul as the form of the living body is the teaching of On the Soul (II.1, 412a19-b9): the soul is the first actuality of a natural body that has life potentially. "A dead finger is a finger only in name", as a painted or stone eye is an eye only in name (On the Soul II.1, 412b20-22).

@p
@greek μέρος μὲν οὖν ἐστὶ καὶ τοῦ εἴδους
A part, then, may be a part of the form (by the form I mean what it is to be), or of the compound of the form and the matter, or of the matter itself. But only the parts of the form are parts of the account, and the account is of the universal: for being a circle and the circle, and being a soul and the soul, are the same. But when we come to the compound, such as this circle, one of the particulars, whether perceptible or intelligible (by intelligible I mean the mathematical circles, by perceptible those of bronze and of wood), of these there is no definition; they are known by thinking or by perceiving; and when they pass out of actuality it is not clear whether they are or not; but they are always spoken of and known by the universal account. The matter is unknowable in itself. Matter is either perceptible or intelligible: perceptible, such as bronze and wood and all matter that can move; intelligible, what is present in the perceptible things not as perceptible, as the objects of mathematics.
!flag known by the aid of intuitive reason or sensation | Ross's "intuitive reason" renders νόησις, "thinking", here the mathematician's grasp of a particular circle drawn in imagination, which he sets beside perception. The modern version says "thinking".
!flag when they go out of our actual consciousness | The Greek says "when they pass out of actuality" (ἐκ τῆς ἐντελεχείας): when this circle is no longer being thought or seen. Ross reads it of our consciousness, Taylor of an "energy ... by which they were visible"; Aristotle's word is the one this work renders "actuality".
!note "Intelligible matter" (ὕλη νοητή): the extension in which the mathematician's objects are, as a circle drawn in thought has a size. Proclus, on the first book of Euclid (51-56), makes the imagination the place of this matter; the Platonists took it up from this chapter.

@p
@greek πῶς μὲν οὖν ἔχει περὶ ὅλου καὶ μέρους
How things stand about whole and part, and about prior and posterior, has been said. But when someone asks whether the right angle, the circle and the animal are prior, or the parts into which they are divided and of which they are made, we must answer that the question cannot be answered simply. If the soul too is the animal, or the living thing, or each one's soul is each one, and the circle is being a circle, and the right angle being a right angle and the substance of the right angle, then we must say that one thing is posterior to another in one way: posterior to the parts in the account, and to the parts of some particular right angle (for both the right angle with matter, the bronze one, and the one in particular lines, are so). The right angle without matter is posterior to the parts in the account, but prior to the parts in the particular; and we must not answer simply. But if the soul is something else and is not the animal, then too we must say of some that they are prior and of some not, as has been said.

@ch Metaph.VII.11 | Which parts belong to the form
Which parts belong to the form, and which to the compound? Where the form is seen in many matters, as the circle in bronze and stone, the matter is plainly no part of it; where it is always in the same matter, as man in flesh and bones, it is hard to separate in thought. Some reduce even the circle and the line to numbers, and so all things to one Form. That is useless: some things are this in that, and an animal cannot be defined without motion, nor so without its parts in a certain state. Mathematical things too have intelligible matter. The soul is the first substance, the body matter. The chapter sums up what has been said of what it is to be.

@taylor 10
@p
It is a reasonable puzzle, too, what kind of parts belong to the form and what kind not to it but to the compound. Yet unless this is clear it is not possible to define each thing: for the definition is of the universal and of the form. If, then, it is not plain what kinds of parts are as matter and what not, the account of the thing will not be plain either. In things that are seen to come to be in different materials, as a circle in bronze, stone and wood, it seems clear that neither the bronze nor the stone is anything of the circle's substance, since the circle is found apart from them. In things not seen apart, nothing prevents their being like these, as if all circles were seen to be of bronze: the bronze would be no less nothing of the form; but it is hard to take it away in thought. The form of man, for instance, always appears in flesh and bones and such parts. Are these, then, parts of the form and of the account? Or not, but matter, and because the form does not come to be in other materials too, we are unable to separate it?
!note The thought experiment "if all circles were of bronze" is the test Aristotle uses throughout: what can be taken away in thought without taking away the thing is no part of its form.

@p
@greek ἐπεὶ δὲ τοῦτο δοκεῖ μὲν ἐνδέχεσθαι ἄδηλον δὲ πότε
Since this seems possible, but it is unclear when, some raise the puzzle even about the circle and the triangle, saying that it is not proper to define them by lines and the continuous, but that these too are said likewise of the circle and triangle as flesh and bones of the man and bronze and stone of the statue; and they reduce all things to numbers, and say the account of the line is that of two. And of those who speak of the Forms, some say the dyad is the line itself, others that it is the Form of the line: for in some things, they say, the Form and that whose Form it is are the same (as the dyad and the Form of the dyad), but in the line not so. It follows, then, that there is one Form of many things whose Form appears different (which happened to the Pythagoreans too), and that one can make one thing the Form itself of all, and the others not Forms; and yet in this way all things will be one.
!note "Some say the dyad is the line itself": Plato's followers in the Academy, who derived the line from two, the plane from three and the solid from four (Book Μ, XIII.9, and Book Ν, XIV.3, take this up). The Platonists of late antiquity read such derivations as the procession of the forms from the One through number; Aristotle's objection is that this makes all things one.

@p
@greek ὅτι μὲν οὖν ἔχει τινὰ ἀπορίαν τὰ περὶ τοὺς ὁρισμούς
That the questions about definitions contain some puzzle, then, and for what reason, has been said. So to reduce all things in this way, and to take away the matter, is wasted labour: for some things are, presumably, this in that, or these in this state. And the comparison Socrates the younger used to make in the case of the animal is not a good one: it leads away from the truth, and makes one suppose that a man can be without his parts, as the circle can be without the bronze. But it is not alike: the animal is something perceptible, and cannot be defined without motion, and so not without its parts being in a certain state. For it is not the hand in any state that is a part of the man, but the hand that can do its work, and so only when it is alive; if not alive, it is not a part.
!flag the comparison which Socrates the younger | Ross's words are right. Socrates the younger is the young mathematician of Plato's Theaetetus (147c-d), Sophist (218b) and Statesman (257c-258a), a namesake of Socrates; his comparison is otherwise unknown, and Ross's note sends the reader to those dialogues.

@p
@greek περὶ δὲ τὰ μαθηματικὰ διὰ τί οὐκ εἰσὶ μέρη οἱ λόγοι τῶν λόγων
About the objects of mathematics: why are the accounts of the parts not parts of the accounts of the wholes, as the semicircles of the circle? It is not because these are not perceptible. Or does that make no difference? For there will be matter even of some things that are not perceptible; indeed there is some matter of everything that is not a what-it-is-to-be and a form itself by itself, but a this. So the semicircles will not be parts of the universal circle, but they will be parts of the particular circles, as was said before; for one matter is perceptible and the other intelligible. It is clear too that the soul is the first substance, the body matter, and man or animal the compound of both taken universally; and Socrates and Coriscus, if Socrates is also the soul, are said in two ways (some mean the soul, others the compound); but if simply this soul and this body, the particular is as the universal is.

@p
@greek πότερον δὲ ἔστι παρὰ τὴν ὕλην τῶν τοιούτων οὐσιῶν τις ἄλλη
Whether there is, besides the matter of such substances, some other substance, and we must look for some substance other than these, as numbers or something of the kind, must be looked into later. It is for the sake of this that we are trying to mark out the perceptible substances too, since in a way the study of perceptible substances is the work of natural science, and of second philosophy: for the student of nature must know not only about the matter but also about the substance by the account, and more so. As for definitions, how the things in the account are parts, and why the definition is one account (for clearly the thing is one; but by what is the thing one, since it has parts?), must be looked into later.
!flag the work of physics, i.e. of second philosophy | Ross's words are right: natural science is "second philosophy", second to the first philosophy of Book Ε (VI.1), which studies the unmoved substances. "Later" is Books Μ and Ν for the numbers, Book Η (VIII.6) and chapter 12 of this book for the unity of the definition.

@p
@greek τί μὲν οὖν ἐστὶ τὸ τί ἦν εἶναι καὶ πῶς αὐτὸ καθʼ αὑτό
