What, then, what it is to be is, and how it is by itself, has been said in general for every case; and why for some things the account of what it is to be contains the parts of the thing defined, and for others not; and that in the account of the substance the parts that are as matter will not be present (for they are not parts of that substance but of the compound; and of this there is in a way an account and in a way not: with the matter there is none, for that is indefinite, but by its first substance there is, as of man the account of the soul; for the substance is the form within, from which and the matter the compound is called substance, as the concavity, since from this and the nose come the snub nose and snubness), while in the compound substance, such as the snub nose or Callias, the matter too will be present; and that what it is to be and each thing are in some cases the same, as in the first substances (for instance curvature and being curvature, if this is first; by "first" I mean what is not said by one thing's being in another, an underlying thing, as matter), but things that are as matter, or taken together with matter, are not the same, nor even if they are one by accident, as Socrates and the musical: for these are the same by accident.
!note The summary of chapters 4 to 11. "The form within" (τὸ εἶδος τὸ ἐνόν): the form is in the thing, not apart from it, and it is the first substance of the thing; this is the answer Book Ζ gives to "what is substance?", worked out in chapter 17.

@ch Metaph.VII.12 | The unity of the definition
Why is what a definition defines one, "two-footed animal", and not many? Not as "pale man" is one, for the genus does not share in its differentiae. Definitions by division contain only the genus and the differentiae, and the genus is not apart from its species, or is as matter. If one divides rightly, by the differentia of the differentia, the last differentia is the substance of the thing and its definition; the earlier ones are said again in it, and there is no order in the substance.

@taylor 11
@p
Let us now speak first of definition, as far as it has not been dealt with in the Analytics; for the puzzle stated there is of use for our accounts of substance. I mean this puzzle: why is the thing whose account we call a definition one? For instance, of man, "two-footed animal": let this be his account. Why, then, is this one and not many, animal and two-footed? In "man" and "pale" there are many when the one does not belong to the other, and one when it belongs and the underlying thing, the man, is affected in some way: then one thing comes to be, and is, the pale man. But here the one does not share in the other: the genus is not thought to share in its differentiae (for then the same thing would share in contraries at once, since the differentiae by which the genus is differentiated are contraries). And even if it does share, the same argument holds, since the differentiae are many, such as footed, two-footed, featherless. Why are these one and not many? Not because they are present in one thing: for in that way there will be one thing out of all of them. But the things in the definition must be one: for the definition is a single account, and an account of substance, so that it must be the account of some one thing; for substance signifies one thing and a this, as we say.
!flag Aristotle here alludes to the second book of his Last Analytics | Taylor's note is right: the puzzle is stated in the Posterior Analytics (II.6, 92a29-33), why the definition is one; Taylor's "Last Analytics" is the old English name of the Posterior. Book Η (VIII.6) gives the full answer: the genus is as matter and the differentia as form, so that the two are one as potency and actuality are.

@p
@greek δεῖ δὲ ἐπισκοπεῖν πρῶτον περὶ τῶν κατὰ τὰς διαιρέσεις ὁρισμῶν
We must look first at definitions reached by division. There is nothing else in the definition but the first genus, as it is called, and the differentiae. The other genera are the first genus together with the differentiae taken with it: for instance, the first is "animal", the next "two-footed animal", and again "two-footed featherless animal"; and likewise if it is said through more terms. In general it makes no difference whether it is said through many or through few, and so neither whether through few or through two; and of the two, one is the differentia and the other the genus, as in "two-footed animal" "animal" is the genus and the other the differentia. If, then, the genus simply is not apart from the species of the genus, or if it is, but is as matter (for the voice is genus and matter, and the differentiae make the species, the letters, out of it), it is plain that the definition is the account made of the differentiae.
!note Division (διαίρεσις) is the method of Plato's Sophist and Statesman, which cut a kind into two again and again until the thing sought is reached. Aristotle keeps it, on condition that each cut is made by a differentia of the last differentia.

@p
@greek ἀλλὰ μὴν καὶ δεῖ γε διαιρεῖσθαι τῇ τῆς διαφορᾶς διαφορᾷ
But one must also divide by the differentia of the differentia. For instance, "footed" is a differentia of animal; next, the differentia of footed animal must be of it as footed. So one must not say that of the footed, one kind is feathered and another featherless, if one is to speak well (one will do this through inability), but rather that one kind is cloven-footed and the other not: for these are differentiae of the foot, since cloven-footedness is a kind of footedness. And in this way one always wants to go on until one comes to what has no differences. Then there will be as many kinds of foot as there are differentiae, and the footed animals will be equal in number to the differentiae. If this is so, it is plain that the last differentia will be the substance of the thing and its definition, since one must not say the same things several times in definitions: that is superfluous. Yet it does happen: when one says "footed two-footed animal", one has said nothing but "animal having feet, having two feet"; and if one divides this by its proper division, one will say it several times, as many times as there are differentiae.
!flag we say it through incapacity | Ross's words are right: to cut the footed into feathered and featherless is a cut by an accident of the footed, not by a differentia of the foot, and comes from not being able to find the right one. Plato's Statesman (262a-263e) gives the same warning against cutting a kind into a part and "all the rest".

@p
@greek ἐὰν μὲν δὴ διαφορᾶς διαφορὰ γίγνηται
If, then, a differentia of the differentia is taken each time, the last one will be the form and the substance. But if one divides by accidents, for instance if one divided the footed into the pale and the dark, there will be as many as there are cuts. So it is plain that the definition is the account made of the differentiae, and, if it is right, of the last of these. This would be clear if one changed the order of such definitions, as that of man, saying "two-footed footed animal": "footed" is superfluous when "two-footed" has been said. But there is no order in the substance: how are we to think of one part as posterior and the other as prior? About definitions by division, then, let this be enough said for a first account of what kind they are.
!note "The last differentia is the substance": the definition of man is "two-footed animal" only because "two-footed" already contains "footed" and "animal". The form of the thing is thus one, as the last differentia is one; Book Η (VIII.6) adds that the genus is to the differentia as matter to form.

@ch Metaph.VII.13 | No universal is substance
Turning to the universal, which some think is a cause and principle above all: no universal can be substance. A thing's substance is its own, the universal common; substance is not said of an underlying thing, the universal always is. Nor can it be substance as present in the substance, as animal in man, for then a quality would be prior to the this. Nothing said in common signifies a this, but a such; otherwise there is the third man. And no substance is made of substances present in it actually. But then no substance will be composite, and none definable: a puzzle answered later.

@taylor 12
@p
Since our inquiry is about substance, let us go back to it again. As the underlying thing is said to be substance, and what it is to be, and what is made of these, so too is the universal. About two of these we have spoken: about what it is to be, and about the underlying thing, that it underlies in two ways, either being a this, as the animal underlies its affections, or as the matter underlies the actuality. But the universal too is thought by some to be a cause most of all, and the universal to be a principle; so let us go on to this too. For it seems impossible that any of the things said universally should be substance.
!flag underlies the complete reality | Ross's "complete reality" renders ἐντελέχεια, "actuality", the state of having reached its end; Taylor says "energy". The modern version says "actuality", as everywhere in this work.
!note "Thought by some": the Platonists, for whom the Forms are causes and principles, and are universal (Book Α, I.6 and 9). Chapters 13 to 16 are Aristotle's main argument that the Forms, so taken, are not substances; the Platonists of late antiquity, Syrianus and Proclus, answered that the Forms are not universals in things but causes above them, and that Aristotle's universal is the "later-born" concept in the soul.

@p
@greek πρῶτον μὲν γὰρ οὐσία ἑκάστου ἡ ἴδιος ἑκάστῳ
First, the substance of each thing is what is peculiar to it, which does not belong to anything else; but the universal is common, for what is called universal is what by nature belongs to more than one. Of which, then, will this be the substance? Either of all, or of none; of all it cannot be; and if of one, the others too will be this one: for things whose substance is one, and whose what-it-is-to-be is one, are themselves one. Again, substance is what is not said of an underlying thing, but the universal is always said of some underlying thing.

@p
@greek ἀλλʼ ἆρα οὕτω μὲν οὐκ ἐνδέχεται ὡς τὸ τί ἦν εἶναι
But perhaps the universal cannot be substance in the way what it is to be is, but can be present in it, as animal in man and horse? Then plainly there is some account of it. It makes no difference even if there is not an account of everything in the substance: none the less this will be the substance of something, as man is of the man in whom it is present; so the same will follow again: it will be the substance of that in which it is present as peculiar to it, as animal is. Further, it is impossible and absurd that the this, the substance, if it is made of some things, should be made not of substances, nor of a this, but of a quality: for what is not substance, the quality, will then be prior to substance and to the this. Which is impossible: neither in account, nor in time, nor in coming to be can the affections be prior to the substance; for then they would be separate too. Further, in Socrates there will be a substance present in a substance, so that it will be the substance of two things. And in general it follows, if man and the things said in this way are substance, that nothing in the account is the substance of anything, nor is it apart from them or in anything else: I mean, for instance, that there is no animal besides the particular animals, nor any of the other things in the accounts.
!flag nor in time nor in knowledge | Ross's 1908 English follows a reading "in knowledge" (γνώσει), as his footnote says; his own Greek of 1924, which the Workshop gives, reads "in coming to be" (γενέσει), as Taylor's Greek did ("generation"). The modern version follows the 1924 text.
!flag For it is not pofllble that participated properties | Taylor's "participated properties" renders πάθη, "affections", the attributes a thing undergoes or has; "participated" is his own word, from the Platonists' language of participation. The modern version says "affections".

@p
@greek ἔκ τε δὴ τούτων θεωροῦσι φανερὸν ὅτι οὐδὲν τῶν καθόλου ὑπαρχόντων οὐσία ἐστί
Looking at it from these points of view, then, it is plain that nothing that belongs universally is a substance, and that nothing predicated in common signifies a this, but a such. Otherwise many other consequences follow, and among them the third man.
!note The "third man": if man is a this, besides the particular men, then man and the men together call for a further "man" over both, and so without end. Plato's Parmenides (132a-b) raises the same objection against the Forms, with largeness; Aristotle gave it this name in his lost work On the Forms. The Platonists answered that the Form is not one more instance of what it is the Form of.

@p
@greek ἔτι δὲ καὶ ὧδε δῆλον
It is clear in this way too: a substance cannot be made of substances present in it in actuality. For two things that are actually two are never actually one; but if they are potentially two, they will be one (as the double line is made of two halves, potentially at least: for actuality separates). So if the substance is one, it will not be made of substances present in it, and in this way; as Democritus rightly says, it is impossible for one to come to be from two, or two from one; for he makes the indivisible magnitudes the substances. It is plain, then, that it will be likewise with number, if number is a putting together of units, as some say: either the dyad is not one, or there is no unit in it in actuality.
!note Democritus' atoms are substances, and so, he said, two atoms never become one. Aristotle agrees with the principle and draws the opposite moral: a substance that is one must not contain substances actually, but only potentially, as a line contains its halves until it is cut.

@p
@greek ἔχει δὲ τὸ συμβαῖνον ἀπορίαν
What follows involves a puzzle. If no substance can be made of universals, because they signify a such and not a this, and no substance can be composed of substances in actuality, every substance would be incomposite, so that there would be no account of any substance. But it is thought by all, and was said long ago, that there is a definition either of substance only, or of it most of all; and now not even of this. So there will be a definition of nothing. Or rather, in one way there will be, and in another not. What is meant will be clearer from what follows.
!note "From what follows": chapters 15 and 17 of this book, and Book Η (VIII.6), where the definition is one because the genus is as matter, potential, and the differentia as form, actual.

@ch Metaph.VII.14 | Against the Forms made of genus and differentia
The same arguments tell against those who say the Forms are separate substances and also make the Form out of the genus and the differentiae. Is "animal" in man and in horse one in number, or different? If one, how is it one and separate in things apart, and how can it share in contraries, two-footed and many-footed? If different, there will be endless animals-themselves, and each will be a Form and a substance. In sensible things the absurdities are worse still. So there are no Forms of them in the way some say.

@taylor 13
@p
From these same considerations it is plain what follows for those who say the Forms are substances and separate, and at the same time make the Form out of the genus and the differentiae. For if there are Forms, and "animal" is in "man" and "horse", it is either one and the same in number with itself, or different. In account it is plainly one: whoever states it goes through the same account in either case. If, then, there is a man himself by himself, a this and separate, the things he is made of, such as "animal" and "two-footed", must also signify a this and be separate and substances: so "animal" too.
!note "Man himself" (αὐτὸς ὁ ἄνθρωπος): the Form of man, in the Academy's way of naming the Forms, "itself by itself" (Phaedo 78d, Symposium 211b).

@p
@greek εἰ μὲν οὖν τὸ αὐτὸ καὶ ἓν τὸ ἐν τῷ ἵππῳ καὶ τῷ ἀνθρώπῳ
If, then, the animal in the horse and in the man is one and the same, as you are with yourself, how will what is one in things that are apart be one, and why will this animal not also be apart from itself? Then, if it is to share in the two-footed and the many-footed, something impossible follows: contraries will belong at once to it, though it is one and a this. And if it does not, what is the way in which one says the animal is two-footed or footed? Perhaps they are put together and touch, or are mixed? But all these are absurd.
!flag if it participates of biped and multiped | Taylor's words are right: μεθέξει is "will share in", "will participate in", the Platonists' word for the relation of things to Forms, here turned against a Form that would have to share in contrary Forms. The modern version says "share in".

@p
@greek ἀλλʼ ἕτερον ἐν ἑκάστῳ
But suppose it is different in each. Then there will be, one might say, unlimited things whose substance is animal: for man is not made of animal by accident. Further, animal itself will be many. For the animal in each is substance (it is not said by virtue of something else; otherwise man would be made of that, and that would be his genus); and further, all the things man is made of will be Forms. So it will not be the Form of one thing and the substance of another (that is impossible): each of those in the animals, then, will be animal itself. Further, from what will this be made, and how from animal itself? Or how can that animal, whose substance is just this, be besides animal itself? Further, in the case of sensible things these consequences follow, and others more absurd still. If, then, it is impossible for things to be so, it is plain that there are not Forms of them in the way some say.
!flag Each, then, of the Ideas present in the species of animals will be the ideal animal | Ross's "the ideal animal" renders αὐτὸ ζῷον, "animal itself", the Academy's name for the Form of animal; Plato's Timaeus (30c-31b, 39e) calls the model of the cosmos "the living thing itself" (αὐτὸ ὃ ἔστι ζῷον), which the Platonists set at the summit of the intelligible world. The modern version says "animal itself".
!note The dilemma: the Form "animal" in "man" and "horse" is either one (and then one thing is in many separate things, and has contrary attributes) or many (and then there are unlimited "animals themselves"). Plato's Parmenides (131a-c) puts the first horn to Socrates with the day and the sail; Proclus' commentary answers that the Forms are present to things without being divided, as the day is.

@ch Metaph.VII.15 | No definition of particulars, nor of a Form
The compound comes to be and perishes; the account, as such, does not. So there is neither definition nor demonstration of particular sensible substances, which have matter and can be or not be: knowledge is of what cannot be otherwise. Nor can a Form be defined, for it is said to be a particular and separate, and an account is made of common names. Above all eternal things that are unique, like the sun and the moon, cannot be defined: whatever one says of the sun could belong to another. Why does no one who speaks of the Forms give a definition of one?

@taylor 14
@p
Since substance is of two kinds, the compound and the account (I mean that one is substance as the account taken together with the matter, the other as the account in general), the substances said in the first way can perish (for they can also come to be); but the account is not such as to perish (for it does not come to be either: being a house does not come to be, but being this house); they are and are not without coming to be and perishing, for it has been shown that no one begets or makes them. That is why there is neither definition nor demonstration of particular perceptible substances: they have matter, whose nature is such that it can both be and not be; so all the particular ones among them perish. If, then, demonstration is of what is necessary, and definition belongs to knowledge, and just as knowledge cannot be sometimes knowledge and sometimes ignorance, but what is such is opinion, so there can be neither demonstration nor definition of these, but opinion is of what can be otherwise, clearly there can be neither definition nor demonstration of them. For perishable things are unclear to those who have knowledge of them, when they pass out of perception; and though the accounts are kept in the soul, there will no longer be either definition or demonstration of them. So when someone who deals with definitions defines some particular, he must not forget that it can always be undone: for it is not possible to define it.
!note Knowledge (ἐπιστήμη) is of what cannot be otherwise; opinion (δόξα) of what can. The line is Plato's (Republic V, 476d-480a; Timaeus 27d-28a): what always is is grasped by intellect with an account, what comes to be and perishes by opinion with perception. Aristotle keeps the line and draws it within the sensible thing, between its form and its matter.

@p
@greek οὐδὲ δὴ ἰδέαν οὐδεμίαν ἔστιν ὁρίσασθαι
Nor, then, can any Form be defined. For the Form is, as they say, a particular, and separate; and the account must be made of names, but the one defining will not make a name (for it will be unknown), and the names laid down are common to all things; so these must belong to something else too. If one were to define you, for instance, he would say "an animal, lean" or "pale", or something else that will belong to another too. If someone should say that nothing prevents all of them belonging, taken apart, to many things, but together to this alone, we must say first that they belong to both of the elements too: "two-footed animal" belongs to animal and to the two-footed. (In eternal things this must even be so, since the elements are prior and parts of the compound; and indeed they are separate too, if "man" is separate: either neither is, or both are. If neither, the genus will not be besides the species; if it is, the differentia will be too.) Next, they are prior in being; and these are not taken away with the compound.
!note The argument turns on the Platonists' own account: if the Form of man is "two-footed animal", its parts, the Form of animal and the Form of two-footed, belong to other things too, and are prior; so the definition fits more than the one Form, and the Form, as a particular, has no definition proper to it.

@p
@greek ἔπειτα εἰ ἐξ ἰδεῶν αἱ ἰδέαι
Then, if the Forms are made of Forms (for the things they are made of are less composite), the things the Form is made of, as animal and two-footed, will also have to be predicated of many. If not, how will they be known? For there will be a Form that cannot be predicated of more than one. But that is not thought to be so: every Form is thought to be shareable. As has been said, then, it escapes notice that it is impossible to define in the case of eternal things, above all those that are unique, such as the sun or the moon. People go wrong not only by adding things which can be taken away and the sun still be, as "going round the earth" or "hidden at night" (for if it stood still or shone at night, it would no longer be the sun; but it is absurd if not, since "the sun" signifies a certain substance); but also by adding what can belong to something else: if another thing of the kind came to be, plainly it would be a sun. So the account is common. But the sun was one of the particulars, like Cleon or Socrates. Why does none of those who posit the Forms bring out a definition of one of them? If they tried, it would become clear that what has now been said is true.
!flag to be capable of being shared | Ross's words are right: μεθεκτή, "shareable", "participable", is the Platonists' own word, and it makes the Form a universal, said of many, against its being a particular. Proclus (Elements of Theology, props. 23-24) distinguished the unparticipated Form from the participated, to meet arguments of this kind.
!note Cleon, the Athenian leader of the Peloponnesian war, here a stock example of a particular man, as Socrates is.

@ch Metaph.VII.16 | Most things thought substances are potencies; neither one nor being is substance
Most of the things thought to be substances are potencies: the parts of animals, and earth, fire and air, which are like a heap until something one is made of them. Nor are one and being the substance of things, since nothing common is substance. Those who posit the Forms are right to make them separate, if they are substances, but wrong to make the one over many a Form; not knowing what the eternal substances are, they make them like the perishable ones, "man himself", "horse himself". Yet there must be eternal substances, as the stars would be even if we had not seen them.

@taylor 15
@p
It is plain that even of the things thought to be substances, most are potencies: the parts of animals (none of them is separated; and when they are separated, then too they all are as matter), and earth, fire and air; for none of them is one, but they are like a heap, until they are concocted and something one comes to be out of them. One might suppose above all that the parts of living things, and the parts of the soul close to them, are both, being in actuality and in potency, because they have principles of motion from something in their joints; which is why some animals live when they are cut in two. Yet they will all be in potency, when they are one and continuous by nature, not by force or by growing together; for such a thing is a deformity.
!flag are only so in capacity | Taylor's "capacity" renders δυνάμει, "in potency", against "in actuality"; the parts of a living thing, while it lives, are not things of their own but potentially so. The modern version says "potency", as everywhere in this work.
!note "Some animals live when cut in two": insects and worms, which On Youth and Old Age (468a-b) and On the Soul (I.5, 411b19-27) take as signs that the soul is present whole in each part. Plotinus (IV.2.1) uses the same fact for the soul's being undivided in the divided body.

@p
@greek ἐπεὶ δὲ τὸ ἓν λέγεται ὥσπερ καὶ τὸ ὄν
Since "one" is said as "being" is, and the substance of what is one is one, and things whose substance is one in number are one in number, it is plain that neither the one nor being can be the substance of things, just as being an element or a principle cannot; but we seek what, then, the principle is, so that we may bring the matter back to something better known. Of these, being and one are more substance than principle, element and cause; but not even these are, if nothing else common is substance either: for substance belongs to nothing but itself and to what has it, whose substance it is. Further, what is one cannot be in many places at once, but what is common belongs to many at once; so that it is clear that no universal is separately, besides the particulars.
!note Against the Platonists and the Pythagoreans who made the One and Being the substance of things (Book Β, III.4, the eleventh puzzle; Book Ι, X.2, answers it). For the Platonists of late antiquity the One is not a substance at all but beyond substance (Republic 509b), and so the argument did not touch their first principle; Syrianus says as much in his commentary on these books.

@p
@greek ἀλλʼ οἱ τὰ εἴδη λέγοντες τῇ μὲν ὀρθῶς λέγουσι χωρίζοντες αὐτά
But those who speak of the Forms are right in one way, in separating them, if they are substances; and wrong in another, because they say the one over many is a Form. The reason is that they cannot say what such substances are, the imperishable ones besides the particular and perceptible ones. So they make them the same in form as the perishable ones (for these we know), "man himself" and "horse himself", adding to the perceptible things the word "itself". And yet, even if we had not seen the stars, there would none the less, I suppose, have been eternal substances besides those we knew; so now too, even if we cannot say what they are, it is perhaps necessary that there be some. It is clear, then, that none of the things said universally is substance, and that no substance is made of substances.
!note "Right to separate them": Aristotle grants the Platonists that there are eternal, separate substances; he denies that they are universals, copies of perishable things with "itself" added. Book Λ (XII.6-10) gives his own account of them: the unmoved movers, intellects in actuality. The Platonists read the line as Aristotle's agreement with them on the main point.

@ch Metaph.VII.17 | Substance as cause: the form
Beginning again: substance is a principle and a cause. A "why" always asks why one thing belongs to another: why do these bricks and stones make a house? The answer is the cause, what it is to be, which in some things is the end and in others the first mover. So we ask why the matter is some definite thing; and the cause, by which it is, is the form, and this is the substance. A syllable is not just its letters, nor flesh fire and earth, but something else, not an element but the cause of their being this: the substance of each thing, its nature, a principle, not an element.

@taylor 16
@p
What, and of what kind, substance should be said to be, let us state again, making another start, as it were: for perhaps from this it will be clear too about that substance which is separate from the perceptible substances. Since, then, substance is a principle and a cause, let us proceed from there. The "why" is always sought in this way: why does one thing belong to another? To seek why the musical man is a musical man is either to seek, as has been said, why the man is musical, or something else. To seek why a thing is itself is to seek nothing (for the fact, that it is, must already be clear, as that the moon is eclipsed; and that a thing is itself is the one account and the one cause for all such questions, why the man is a man or the musical musical, unless one were to say that each thing is undivided from itself, and this was what being one is; but that is common to all and a short way). But one might ask why a man is an animal of such a kind. Here, then, it is clear that one is not asking why he who is a man is a man. One is asking, then, why something belongs to something (that it belongs must be clear; if not, one is asking nothing): for instance, why does it thunder? Why does a noise come about in the clouds? For in this way what is sought is one thing said of another. And why are these things, bricks and stones, a house?
!flag Let us state what should be said to be the genus and the differentia of substance | The Greek asks "what, and of what kind" (τί καὶ ὁποῖόν τι) substance should be said to be; Ross's English makes the two words the genus and the differentia of substance, which is his gloss. Taylor says "what, and what kind of a thing".
!note "Why is the moon eclipsed?" is the model question of the Posterior Analytics (II.2, 90a15-18; II.8): the answer, the earth's coming between, is the middle term, the cause, and also what the eclipse is. This chapter carries that model over to substance.

@p
@greek φανερὸν τοίνυν ὅτι ζητεῖ τὸ αἴτιον
It is plain, then, that one is seeking the cause. And this is what it is to be, to speak in the manner of argument about words: in some things it is that for the sake of which, as perhaps in a house or a bed, and in some the first thing that moved; for this too is a cause. But this second kind of cause is sought in coming to be and perishing, the other in being too. What is sought escapes notice most in things not said one of another, as when one asks what a man is, because it is said simply and one does not mark off that these things are this. But one must articulate the question before seeking; otherwise it becomes something between seeking nothing and seeking something. Since the being must be had and must belong, it is clear that one is asking about the matter, why it is something: why are these things a house? Because what it is to be a house belongs to them. And why is this, or this body having this, a man? So what is sought is the cause of the matter (and this is the form) by which it is something; and this is the substance. It is plain, then, that in the case of simple things there is no seeking nor teaching, but another way of inquiring into such things.
!flag But while the efficient cause is sought in the case of genesis and destruction | Ross names the two causes with the later schools' words, "efficient" and "final"; the Greek says only "such a cause" (the first mover) and "the other" (that for the sake of which, which is the form). The modern version keeps the Greek's plainer words.
!note "Simple things": things not composed of matter and form, which have no cause other than themselves; they are known not by seeking a middle term but by direct grasp, as Book Θ (IX.10) says of the truth of simple things, "touching" and "saying".

@p
@greek ἐπεὶ δὲ τὸ ἔκ τινος σύνθετον οὕτως ὥστε ἓν εἶναι τὸ πᾶν
Since what is composed of something in such a way that the whole is one, not like a heap but like a syllable (and the syllable is not its letters, nor is "ba" the same as b and a, nor is flesh fire and earth: for when they are broken up, the wholes, the flesh and the syllable, no longer are, but the letters are, and fire and earth), the syllable, then, is something, not only its letters, the vowel and the consonant, but something else too; and flesh is not only fire and earth, or the hot and the cold, but something else too. If, then, that something must be either an element or made of elements: if an element, the same argument will apply again (flesh will be made of this and fire and earth and something else, so that it will go on without end); and if made of elements, plainly not of one but of more (or else it will be that one itself), so that again we will say the same about it as about the flesh or the syllable. But it would seem that this is something, and not an element, and is the cause of this being flesh and that a syllable; and likewise in the other cases. And this is the substance of each thing: for this is the first cause of its being. And since some things are not substances, but those that are substances are formed according to nature and by nature, this nature too would appear to be substance, which is not an element but a principle. An element is what a thing is divided into, present in it as matter, as a and b of the syllable.
!note The end of Book Ζ: the substance of each thing is its form, the cause of its being what it is, not a further part beside its parts. Book Η takes up the sensible substances in these terms, matter and form as potency and actuality; Book Λ goes on to the substances that are separate. For the Platonists the chapter, with its "nature which is a principle", leads from the forms in matter up toward the Forms in intellect, which they took to be the forms' causes.

@treatise Metaph.VIII | Metaphysics, Book Η (VIII)
Book Η gathers up the inquiry into substance of Book Ζ and turns it to the substances all agree on, the sensible ones, which all have matter. Chapter 1 sums up Book Ζ and shows that matter too is substance, as what underlies every change. The rest of the book reads the sensible substance as matter and form, taken as potency and actuality: the differences that make a threshold a threshold and ice ice are the analogue of form, and a definition that gives them gives the actuality, one that gives the materials gives the potency (2); a name may signify the compound or the form alone, and the form is not a further element but the substance, as a number is one (3); each thing has its own proper matter, and one must state all its causes, and the nearest (4); not everything has matter, and the matter of a thing is related to contraries in different ways (5). The book ends by solving the puzzle of Book Ζ (VII.12): what makes a definition, and a thing, one is that the matter is potentially what the form is actually, so that nothing need be sought to join them (6).

@ch Metaph.VIII.1 | Summing up; sensible substance, and matter as substance
Book Ζ is summed up: the causes and principles of substances were sought; what it is to be, the underlying thing, the universal and the genus were put forward as substances, and the universal and the genus set aside. Now to the substances all agree on, the sensible ones, which all have matter. The underlying thing is substance in three ways: as matter, as form, and as the compound of the two. That matter too is substance is plain from change: in every change something underlies it, and in coming to be and perishing that is matter.

@p
We must draw conclusions from what has been said, gather up the main points, and so bring the inquiry to its end. We have said that what we are looking for is the causes, the principles and the elements of substances. Some substances are agreed on by everyone, while some thinkers have declared others of their own. Those agreed on are the natural substances: fire, earth, water, air and the other simple bodies; then plants and their parts; animals and the parts of animals; and finally the heaven and the parts of the heaven. Some thinkers, on their own account, say that the Forms and the objects of mathematics are substances. And our arguments have brought out further substances: what it is to be, and the underlying thing. Again, in another way, the genus seems to be substance more than the species, and the universal more than the particulars; and the Forms are bound up with the universal and the genus, since they are thought to be substances by the same argument. Since what it is to be is substance, and its account is the definition, we have discussed definition and what belongs to a thing in itself. And since the definition is an account, and an account has parts, we had to consider parts too: which are parts of the substance and which are not, and whether the parts of the substance are also parts of the definition. Further, neither the universal nor the genus is substance. The Forms and the objects of mathematics we must look into later, for some say that these exist besides the sensible substances.
!flag forms and the mathematical species | Taylor's "mathematical species" renders τὰ μαθηματικά, "the objects of mathematics", the numbers and figures that some of Plato's school set between the Forms and sensible things. The modern version says "the objects of mathematics", as throughout this work.
!note "Later": Books Μ and Ν (XIII and XIV), on the Forms and the numbers. The summary runs over Book Ζ: the four candidates for substance (VII.3), definition and what belongs in itself (VII.4-6, 10-12), the universal and the genus (VII.13-16).

@p
@greek νῦν δὲ περὶ τῶν ὁμολογουμένων οὐσιῶν ἐπέλθωμεν
Now let us go on to the substances that are agreed on. These are the sensible substances, and the sensible substances all have matter. The underlying thing is substance: in one way the matter (by matter I mean what is not a this in actuality, but is a this in potency); in another way the account and the form, which, being a this, is separable in account; and third, what is made of these, which alone comes to be and perishes, and is separable without qualification. For of the substances that answer to the account, some are separable and some are not.
!note The three ways of being substance are those of Book Ζ (VII.3): matter, form and the compound. "Separable in account": the form can be stated by itself, though it does not exist apart from its matter; the compound, the man or the horse, exists apart without qualification.

@p
@greek ὅτι δʼ ἐστὶν οὐσία καὶ ἡ ὕλη, δῆλον
That matter too is substance is plain. In all the changes between opposites there is something that underlies the changes: in change of place, what is now here and later somewhere else; in growth, what is now of this size and later smaller or larger; in alteration, what is now healthy and later sick. And likewise in change of substance there is something that is now coming to be and later perishing, and that now underlies as a this, and later underlies as by privation. The other changes follow on this one; but this one does not follow on one or two of the others. For it is not necessary that if something has matter for change of place, it should also have matter for coming to be and perishing. The difference between coming to be without qualification and coming to be in a certain respect has been stated in the works on nature.
!flag according to internal change, he who is now well | Taylor's "internal change" renders ἀλλοίωσις, "alteration", change of quality, as from healthy to sick; Ross says "alteration", as the modern version does. Aristotle's four kinds of change are of place, of size (growth and shrinking), of quality and of substance (coming to be and perishing).
!flag underlies it as the privation of positive character | The Greek says only "underlies as by privation" (ὡς κατὰ στέρησιν); Ross's "of positive character" spells out what the thing is deprived of, its form. The modern version keeps the Greek's bare words.
!note "The works on nature": the Physics (V.1, 225a12-20) and On Coming to Be and Perishing (I.2-3, 317a17-319b5). The heavenly bodies, which move in place but do not come to be or perish, have matter for motion only (VIII.4).

@ch Metaph.VIII.2 | The differences of sensible things, and actuality
Matter is agreed to be substance as potency; what is the substance of sensible things as actuality? Things differ in many ways besides Democritus' three: by the way their matter is put together, by position, time, place, and by sensible qualities; and "is" is said in as many ways. None of these differences is substance, but in each there is something analogous to it: the actuality. A definition from the materials gives the potency, one from the differences the actuality, one from both the compound, as in Archytas' definitions.

@p
Since the substance that is as underlying thing and as matter is agreed on, and this is substance in potency, it remains to say what the substance of sensible things is as actuality. Democritus seems to think there are three differences between things: the underlying body, the matter, is one and the same, but things differ in rhythm, that is, shape; in turning, that is, position; or in contact, that is, order. But there appear to be many differences. Some things are spoken of by the way their matter is put together, as things made by blending, like honey-water; others by binding, like a bundle; others by glue, like a book; others by nails, like a box; others by more than one of these. Others again by position, like the threshold and the lintel (these differ by lying in a certain way); others by time, like dinner and breakfast; others by place, like the winds; others by the affections of sensible things, like hardness and softness, density and rarity, dryness and wetness; some by some of these, others by all of them; and in general some by excess and some by deficiency. So it is clear that "is" too is said in just as many ways. A threshold is, because it lies thus, and its being means its lying thus; and being ice means being solidified thus. The being of some things will be defined by all of these: some of their parts are mixed, some blended, some bound, some solidified, and some use the other differences, like the hand or the foot. We must grasp, then, the kinds of the differences (for these will be the principles of being): those of the more and the less, for instance, or of the dense and the rare, and the other such, for all these are excess and deficiency. Whatever differs by shape, or by smoothness and roughness, differs by the straight and the curved. For other things their being will be their being mixed, and their not being the opposite.
!flag or by trope which is position | Taylor's "trope" and "ryfm" (as the scan has it) and "diathige" carry over Democritus' own words, τροπή ("turning"), ῥυσμός ("rhythm", the Abderite form of ῥυθμός) and διαθιγή ("touching"), which Aristotle glosses as position, shape and order. Ross translates them "turning", "rhythm" and "inter-contact"; the modern version gives Democritus' words and Aristotle's glosses both. Book Α (I.4, 985b13-19) explains them with the letters A and N, AN and NA, Z and N.
!flag some things are from transcendency, others from deficiency | Taylor's "transcendency" renders ὑπεροχή, "excess", "exceeding"; the word has nothing of the later sense of transcending. The modern version says "excess".

@p
@greek φανερὸν δὴ ἐκ τούτων ὅτι εἴπερ ἡ οὐσία αἰτία
It is plain from this, then, that if the substance is the cause of each thing's being, it is among these differences that we must look for the cause of the being of each of these things. None of them is substance, not even when coupled with the matter; yet in each there is something analogous. And as in substances what is predicated of the matter is the actuality itself, so in the other definitions too it is this most of all. If we had to define a threshold, for instance, we would say "wood or stone lying thus", and a house "bricks and timbers lying thus" (or in some cases we would add that for the sake of which as well); if ice, "water frozen" or "solidified thus"; a concord is "such and such a blending of high and low"; and in the same way with the rest.
!flag that which is predicated of matter is energy itself | Taylor's "energy" renders ἐνέργεια, "actuality", "being at work", the word this book and the next turn on; Ross says "actuality", as the modern version does throughout this work. Taylor uses "energy" in its old sense of activity, which is the Greek's: from ἔργον, "work".
!flag it is what most resembles full actuality | The Greek says only "and in the other definitions most of all" (καὶ ἐν τοῖς ἄλλοις ὁρισμοῖς μάλιστα): what is predicated of the matter is the actuality most of all, or the actuality is so predicated most of all in the other definitions. Ross's "what most resembles full actuality" is his reading of the compressed line.
!note "Concord" (συμφωνία): a consonant interval, such as the octave, which the Pythagoreans defined by a ratio of high and low; Ross says "harmony", Taylor "symphony". Plato's Timaeus (80a-b) and Philebus (17c-d, 26a) speak of the high and the low blended by limit.

@p
@greek φανερὸν δὴ ἐκ τούτων ὅτι ἡ ἐνέργεια ἄλλη ἄλλης ὕλης
It is plain from this, then, that the actuality is different for different matter, and so is the account: for some it is the putting together, for others the mixing, for others another of those we have named. That is why, of those who define, those who say what a house is by saying "stones, bricks, timbers" are speaking of the house in potency, for these are its matter; those who put forward "a shelter for goods and bodies", or something else of the kind, are speaking of the actuality; and those who combine the two are speaking of the third substance, the one made of these. For the account through the differences seems to be of the form and the actuality, and the one from the constituents rather of the matter. Likewise with the kind of definitions Archytas used to accept, for they are of the two together. What is still weather? Rest in a mass of air: the air is the matter, and the rest is the actuality and substance. What is calm? Smoothness of sea: the underlying thing, as matter, is the sea, and the actuality and form is the smoothness. It is plain from what has been said, then, what the sensible substance is and how it is: one as matter, another as form and actuality, and the third the one made of these.
!note Archytas of Tarentum, the Pythagorean mathematician and statesman, Plato's friend (the Seventh Letter, 338c-339b, 350a-b). His definitions join a matter (air, sea) to a form (rest, smoothness), and so state the compound.
!note "Shelter" (σκέπασμα): the house as defined by its use, which is its form; De Anima (I.1, 403b3-7) sets the same three definitions of a house side by side.

@ch Metaph.VIII.3 | The name, the form, and definition as number
A name may signify the compound or the form: "house" a shelter of bricks laid thus, or a shelter; "animal" a soul in a body, or a soul. What it is to be belongs to the form. The form is not one more element beside the elements, but the substance, the cause of being, which comes to be without becoming. So Antisthenes' puzzle has a point: the first parts of a thing cannot be defined, only the compound. If substances are numbers, they are so as definitions are: indivisible into further parts without change, and one, not a heap.

@p
We must not overlook that it is sometimes unclear whether a name signifies the compound substance or the actuality and form. Does "house", for instance, signify the common thing, "a shelter made of bricks and stones lying thus", or the actuality and form, "a shelter"? Is a line "a dyad in length" or "a dyad"? Is an animal "a soul in a body" or "a soul"? For the soul is the substance and actuality of a certain body. "Animal" might apply to both, not as said by one account, but as related to one thing. This makes a difference for another purpose, but none for the inquiry into sensible substance; for what it is to be belongs to the form and the actuality. Soul and being soul are the same, but being man and man are not the same, unless the soul too is to be called man; and so in one way they are the same, and in another not.
!flag whether it is a duad in length | Taylor's "duad" is δυάς, "dyad", "two", which the modern version says, as throughout this work. The line as "a dyad in length" is the Academy's: Book Ζ (VII.11, 1036b12-17) reports that some reduce the line to "two" itself, as its form, and Book Ν reports Plato's derivation of lines from the great and the small. Ross says "twoness".
!note "The soul is the substance and actuality of a certain body": the definition of On the Soul (II.1, 412a19-28), the soul as the first actuality of a natural body that has life in potency. The Platonists accepted it for the soul as the body's form, and held that the rational soul is also separable (Plotinus IV.7.8).

@p
@greek οὐ φαίνεται δὴ ζητοῦσιν ἡ συλλαβὴ ἐκ τῶν στοιχείων οὖσα
When we look, a syllable does not turn out to be its letters plus their putting together, nor a house bricks plus their putting together. And this is right: for the putting together or the mixing is not made of the things whose putting together or mixing it is. Likewise with all the rest: if a threshold is a threshold by position, the position is not made of the threshold, but rather the threshold of it. Nor is man animal plus two-footed: there must be something besides these, if these are matter, which is neither an element nor made of an element, but the substance, which people leave out when they state the matter. If, then, this is the cause of being, and the substance, they would not be stating the substance itself. (This must either be eternal, or perishable without perishing, and have come to be without coming to be. It has been shown and made clear elsewhere that no one makes or begets the form: it is this that is made, and what comes to be is the thing made of these. Whether the substances of perishable things are separable is not yet at all clear; except that for some it is clearly impossible, for those that cannot be besides the particular ones, such as a house or a utensil. Perhaps, indeed, neither these things themselves nor any of the other things not put together by nature are substances; for one might set down nature alone as the substance in perishable things.)
!note "Shown elsewhere": Book Ζ (VII.8, 1033a24-b19): no one makes the form, the sphere, but this bronze sphere. The form "perishable without perishing": it is and then is not, without a process of perishing, as the line has it at VIII.5.
!note "Nature alone" as substance among perishable things: Aristotle leaves open whether artefacts, houses and utensils, have substances at all. The Platonists, too, posited Forms of natural things and not of artefacts (Book Α, I.9, 991b6-7; Proclus on the Parmenides, Book III), though Plato's Republic (596b) speaks of the Form of the bed.

@p
@greek ὥστε ἡ ἀπορία ἣν οἱ Ἀντισθένειοι
So the puzzle that the followers of Antisthenes, and other such uneducated people, raised has a certain point. They said that it is not possible to define what a thing is (a definition, they said, is a long rigmarole), but it is possible to teach what sort of thing it is: of silver, for instance, one cannot say what it is, but one can say that it is like tin. So there is a kind of substance of which there can be a definition and an account: the compound, whether it is sensible or intelligible. But the first things it is made of cannot be defined, if indeed the defining account signifies something of something, and one part must be as matter and the other as form.
!flag may now be opportunely solved | The Greek says that the puzzle "has a certain timeliness" (ἔχει τινὰ καιρόν): it has a point, it is apt. Taylor makes Aristotle say that the puzzle "may now be opportunely solved", which is not in the Greek. Ross's English has lost words in the scan here; the modern version follows the Greek.
!flag the definition so called is a | Ross's "the definition so called is a 'long formula'" renders τὸν γὰρ ὅρον λόγον εἶναι μακρόν, "for the definition is a long account", that is, a long-winded speech, a rigmarole, as Ross's footnote says; Book Ν (1091a8) uses the phrase with the same scorn. The modern version says "a long rigmarole".
!flag whether it be the object of sense or of reason | Ross's "object of reason" renders νοητή, "intelligible": the compound grasped by thought, not by the senses, as the objects of mathematics are. The modern version says "intelligible", as for νοητόν throughout this work; chapter 6 speaks of "intelligible matter".
!note Antisthenes, a follower of Socrates, held that one can say of a thing only its own name, so that there is no contradiction and no definition (Book Δ, V.29, 1024b32-34). Plato's Theaetetus (201e-202c), in Socrates' "dream", gives a theory like his: the first elements have no account and can only be named. The Platonists took the dream as the doctrine that the simple is known not by account but by intellect.

@p
@greek φανερὸν δὲ καὶ διότι, εἴπερ εἰσί πως ἀριθμοὶ αἱ οὐσίαι
It is plain too why, if substances are in some way numbers, they are so in this way, and not, as some say, as numbers of units. For a definition is a kind of number: it is divisible, and into indivisible parts (for accounts are not unlimited), and number is of this kind. And as, when anything of which a number consists is taken away from it or added to it, it is no longer the same number but a different one, even if the least thing is taken away or added, so the definition and what it is to be will no longer be the same if anything is taken away or added. And the number must have something by which it is one, which these people cannot say, if it is one (for either it is not one, but like a heap, or, if it is, they must say what makes one out of many); and the definition is one, but likewise they cannot say what makes it one. This happens with reason, for the same account applies; and substance is one in the way we have said, not, as some say, as being a kind of unit or point, but each is an actuality and a certain nature. And as number does not admit of the more and the less, neither does substance as form; if any substance does, it is the one with matter. Let this be enough, then, on the coming to be and perishing of what are called substances, how it is possible and how impossible, and on the reduction of things to number.
!flag each is a complete reality and a definite nature | Ross's "complete reality" renders ἐντελέχεια, "actuality", as in Book Ζ (VII.13); Taylor says "energy". The modern version says "actuality", as everywhere in this work.
!note "As some say": the Pythagoreans and those of Plato's school who made substances numbers built of units. Aristotle's answer: a substance is one as a definition is, by its form, not as a sum of units; the question what makes a number one, and not a heap, comes back in Book Μ (XIII.8) and Book Ν. Proclus (Elements of Theology, props. 1-6) begins from the same question, how every manifold shares in the one.

@ch Metaph.VIII.4 | Proper matter, and stating all the causes
Even if all things come from the same first matter, each has its own proper matter, and one thing may have several, one coming from another. Different things can come from one matter by a different mover, but some things need a different matter: a saw cannot be made of wood. In seeking causes, state all of them, and the nearest: of man, the matter, the mover, the form and the end. Eternal natural substances have no such matter, or only matter for motion in place; things by nature that are not substances, like an eclipse or sleep, have a subject, not matter.

@p
About the material substance we must not overlook that, even if all things come from the same first cause, or from the same things as first causes, and the same matter is the starting point of what comes to be, there is still a matter proper to each thing. The proper matter of phlegm, for instance, is the sweet or the fat, and of bile the bitter or something else; though perhaps these come from the same thing. There come to be several matters of the same thing when one matter is the matter of the other: phlegm, for instance, comes from the fat and from the sweet, if the fat comes from the sweet; and from bile, by the bile's being broken down into its first matter. For one thing comes from another in two ways: either as a further stage along the way, or by the other's being broken down into its principle. When the matter is one, different things can come to be through the moving cause: from wood, for instance, both a box and a bed. But some different things must have different matter: a saw, for instance, could not come to be from wood, nor is this in the power of the moving cause, for it will not make a saw from wool or from wood. If, after all, the same thing can be made from other matter, clearly the art, the principle as mover, is the same; for if both the matter and the mover were different, so would be what has come to be.
!flag either by progression, or analyfation | Taylor's "progression" renders πρὸ ὁδοῦ, "further along the road": one thing comes from another as a later stage of the way it develops, as the fat from the sweet. The scan of Ross has lost a line here, between "analysed" and "things may be produced"; the modern version follows the Greek.

@p
@greek ὅταν δή τις ζητῇ τὸ αἴτιον
When one looks for the cause, then, since causes are spoken of in several ways, one must state all the possible causes. What is the cause of a man as matter? The menses, surely. What, as mover? The seed, surely. What, as form? What it is to be. What, as that for the sake of which? The end. (But perhaps these last two are the same.) And one must state the nearest causes. What is the matter? Not fire or earth, but the matter proper to the thing.
!flag The final cause? His end | Ross names the causes with the later schools' words, "material", "moving", "formal" and "final"; the Greek asks only what the cause is "as matter", "as mover", "as form" and "as that for the sake of which". The modern version keeps the Greek's plainer words, as in Book Ζ (VII.17).
!note The four causes of a man are those of the Generation of Animals (I.1, 715a4-11; I.20-21): the menses as matter, the seed as the source of motion, the form and the end, which are one, since the end of coming to be is the form. "Perhaps the same": the form and the end often coincide (Physics II.7, 198a24-27), since the end of coming to be is to have the form.

@p
@greek περὶ μὲν οὖν τὰς φυσικὰς οὐσίας καὶ γενητὰς
With natural substances that come to be, one must proceed in this way, if one is to proceed rightly, since these are the causes and this is their number, and one must know the causes. But with natural substances that are eternal there is another account. For perhaps some have no matter, or not matter of this kind, but only matter that can move in place. Nor do the things that are by nature but are not substances have matter: the underlying thing is the substance. What is the cause of an eclipse, for instance? What is its matter? There is none: the moon is what undergoes it. What is the cause as what moves and destroys the light? The earth. The cause as that for the sake of which perhaps does not exist. The cause as form is the account; but this is unclear unless the account includes the cause. What is an eclipse? Privation of light. But if one adds "by the earth's coming in between", this is the account with the cause. With sleep it is unclear what it is that first undergoes it. The animal, one says? Yes, but the animal in virtue of what, and what first? The heart, or something else. Then, by what is it produced? Then, what is the affection, the affection of that part and not of the whole? That it is such and such an immobility? Yes, but this belongs to it by the first part's undergoing what?
!note "Natural substances that are eternal": the heavenly bodies, whose matter is only for motion in place (Book Λ, XII.2, 1069b24-26), the fifth body of On the Heavens (I.2-3). The Platonists, after Plato's Timaeus (40a-b), held them to be visible gods.
!note The eclipse is the model definition of the Posterior Analytics (II.2, 90a15-18; II.8, 93a29-b7): the account that includes the cause, "privation of light by the earth's coming between", says both what the eclipse is and why. Sleep is treated in On Sleep and Waking (2-3, 455b13-458a32), where the first part affected is the heart.

@ch Metaph.VIII.5 | What has no matter; matter and contraries
Some things are and are not without coming to be or perishing, like points, if they exist, and forms in general: it is the wood that comes to be white, not the white. These have no matter; only what comes to be and changes into one another does. The matter of a thing is related to contraries differently: the body is the matter of health by its state and form, of disease by privation and decay against nature. Wine is not the matter of vinegar, nor the living potentially dead: the change goes back through the matter, as night comes from day.

@p
Since some things are and are not without coming to be and perishing, like points, if they are, and the forms in general (for it is not the white that comes to be, but the wood comes to be white, if everything that comes to be comes to be from something and comes to be something), not all contraries could come to be from one another. A white man comes from a black man in one way, and white from black in another. Nor does everything have matter, but only those things that come to be and change into one another. Those that, without changing, are or are not, have no matter.
!flag in general forms and shapes | The Greek says only "and the forms in general" (καὶ ὅλως τὰ εἴδη); Ross's "and shapes" is his own, to make plain that the forms here are the forms in things, the white, the shape, not the Platonic Forms. The modern version says "forms", in lower case.
!note "Without coming to be": the form is not a thing that comes to be, but what the thing comes to be (Book Ζ, VII.8; VIII.3 above); the point is the limit of a line, which is when the line is divided, without coming to be (Book Β, III.5, 1002a32-b11).

@p
@greek ἔχει δʼ ἀπορίαν πῶς πρὸς τἀναντία
There is a puzzle about how the matter of each thing is related to contraries. If the body, for instance, is in potency healthy, and disease is contrary to health, is it in potency both? And is water in potency wine and vinegar? Or is it the matter of the one by its state and its form, and of the other by privation and decay against its nature? There is a puzzle too why wine is not the matter of vinegar, nor in potency vinegar (though vinegar comes from it), and why the living man is not in potency dead. Or are they not, but the decays are by accident; and the matter of the animal is itself, by decay, the potency and matter of a corpse, and water of vinegar? For these come from those as night from day. All things that change into one another in this way must go back to their matter: if an animal comes from a corpse, the corpse goes first into its matter, and only then becomes an animal; and vinegar goes into water, and only then becomes wine.
!flag the matter of health, according to habit | Taylor's "habit" renders ἕξις, "state", "having", the positive condition of which disease is the privation; Ross says "positive state". The modern version says "state", as in Book Δ (V.20).
!note "As night from day": not as from its matter, but after it in time; night is not day changed. The puzzle prepares the account of potency in Book Θ (IX.7): a thing is in potency only what it can become by one step, without being first undone.

@ch Metaph.VIII.6 | What makes the definition, and the thing, one
The puzzle of Book Ζ returns: what makes a definition one, and the thing defined, man, one and not many, animal and two-footed? Not participation in two Forms, animal itself and two-footed itself. If one part is matter and the other form, one in potency and the other in actuality, the puzzle vanishes: nothing makes them one but the maker who brings the potency to act. What has no matter is one and a being straight off. The last matter and the form are the same thing, the one in potency, the other in actuality.

@p
About the puzzle stated concerning definitions and numbers, what is the cause of their being one? Of all things that have several parts, and whose whole is not like a heap but is something besides the parts, there is some cause; since even among bodies, for some contact is the cause of their being one, for others stickiness or some other affection of this kind. A definition is one account, not by being bound together, like the Iliad, but by being of one thing. What is it, then, that makes man one, and why is he one and not many, animal and two-footed, above all if there is, as some say, an animal itself and a two-footed itself? For why is man not those things themselves, so that men would be by participation not in man, nor in one thing, but in two, animal and two-footed? And in general man would not be one but more than one, animal and two-footed.
!flag an ideal animal and an ideal biped | Ross's "ideal animal" and "ideal biped" render αὐτό τι ζῷον καὶ αὐτὸ δίπουν, "an animal itself and a two-footed itself", the Academy's names for the Forms, as in Book Ζ (VII.14). The scan of Ross has lost the lines before these words; the modern version follows the Greek there.
!note "The puzzle stated": Book Ζ (VII.12, 1037b8-27) and VIII.3 above. The Iliad is one by being bound together, a single story told in many lines; a definition, Aristotle says, is one by being of one thing (On Interpretation 5, 17a8-15; Poetics 20, 1457a28-30).

@p
@greek φανερὸν δὴ ὅτι οὕτω μὲν μετιοῦσιν
It is plain that if people proceed as they are used to defining and speaking, they cannot give the reason and solve the puzzle. But if, as we say, one part is matter and the other form, and one is in potency and the other in actuality, what is sought would no longer seem a puzzle. For this puzzle is the same as if the definition of "cloak" were "round bronze": the name would be a sign of the account, so that what is sought is what makes the round and the bronze one. The puzzle no longer appears, because the one is matter and the other form. What, then, is the cause of this, of what was in potency being in actuality, besides the maker, in things that come to be? There is no other cause of the sphere in potency being a sphere in actuality: this was what it is to be for each. Of matter, some is intelligible and some sensible; and of the account, one part is always matter and the other actuality, as "the circle is a plane figure". But whatever has no matter, either intelligible or sensible, is straight off just what it is, something one, as it is something that is: a this, a quality, a quantity. That is why neither "being" nor "one" is in definitions; and what it is to be is straight off something one, as it is something that is. That is why there is no other cause of any of these things' being one, nor of their being something that is: each is straight off something that is and something one, not as in the genus of being or of one, nor as if these were separable, besides the particulars.
!flag were the definition of | Ross's English puts "X" for ἱμάτιον, "cloak", as his footnote says, the word Aristotle uses as a stand-in name in Book Ζ (VII.4, 1029b28) and On Interpretation (8, 18a19); Taylor keeps "garment". The modern version says "cloak".
!flag except the efficient cause | The Greek says "besides the maker" (παρὰ τὸ ποιῆσαν), the one who made the thing; Taylor's "efficient cause" is the later schools' name. The modern version keeps the Greek's plainer word.
!note "Intelligible matter": the matter of the objects of mathematics, the extension in which a circle is thought, as against the bronze of a bronze circle (Book Ζ, VII.10, 1036a9-12; VII.11, 1037a4-5). Plotinus (II.4.2-5) took the phrase up for a matter in the intelligible world itself; Proclus, on Euclid (51-56 Friedlein), made the imagination the intelligible matter of the geometer's figures.
!note "Not in definitions": being and one are not genera (Book Β, III.3, 998b22-27), and so are not parts of any definition; each category is one and a being straight off. The Platonists set the One above being, and the "one" of each thing as its share in it.

@p
@greek διὰ ταύτην δὲ τὴν ἀπορίαν οἱ μὲν μέθεξιν λέγουσι
Because of this puzzle some speak of participation, and are at a loss what the cause of participation is and what participating is. Others speak of communion, as Lycophron says knowledge is a communion of knowing and the soul; others say life is a putting together or a binding of soul with body. Yet the same account applies to all cases: being healthy will be a communion or a binding or a putting together of soul and health, the bronze's being a triangle a putting together of bronze and triangle, and a thing's being white a putting together of surface and whiteness. The reason is that they are looking for a unifying account, and a difference, between potency and actuality. But, as has been said, the last matter and the form are the same and one thing, the one in potency, the other in actuality; so that to ask what the cause of their being one is, is like asking the cause of anything's being one. For each thing is something one, and what is in potency and what is in actuality are in a way one. So there is no other cause, unless something moved the thing from potency to actuality. And whatever has no matter is, without qualification, just what it is, something one.
!flag the proximate matter and the form are one | Ross's "proximate matter" renders ἡ ἐσχάτη ὕλη, "the last matter": last on the way down from the form, the matter nearest to it, as the bronze is to the statue, not the elements; Taylor says "the last matter". The modern version keeps the Greek's "last".
!note "Participation" (μέθεξις): Plato's word for the relation of sensible things to Forms (Phaedo 100c-d; Parmenides 130e-131e). Aristotle's complaint, from Book Α (I.9, 991a20-22), is that it is a metaphor with no account of how it works. Proclus answered it in the Elements of Theology (props. 23-24, 65): what is participated is present to the participant without being divided, by the power of the cause, not by mixture or putting together.
!note Lycophron, a sophist and pupil of Gorgias, known also from the Politics (III.9, 1280b10-12) for calling law "a guarantor of just dealings with one another".

@treatise Metaph.IX | Metaphysics, Book Θ (IX)
Book Θ turns from being in the categories to being in potency and in actuality, the second way in which "being" is said. It begins with potency in the strictest sense, the power of acting and of being acted on: its kinds, and incapacity as its privation (chapter 1); powers with reason, which can do contraries, against powers without reason, which do one thing (2). Against the Megarians, who said that a thing can act only when it is acting, Aristotle shows that potency and actuality are different (3); what is possible is that from which, if it is supposed actual, nothing impossible follows (4); and a power with reason acts when desire or choice decides (5). Then actuality: it is grasped by analogy, as the building to what can build, the waking to the sleeping; and an actuality in which the end is present, like seeing or living well, differs from a motion, like learning or building, which stops at its end (6). When a thing is in potency (7). Actuality is prior to potency in account, in time in one way, and in substance; the eternal things are wholly in actuality, and the sun and stars never tire (8). A good actuality is better than the potency, so that there is no evil apart from bad things, and none in the eternal things; geometrical truths are found by actually drawing the figures (9). The book ends with being as true: truth in composite things is to think them combined as they are combined; with simple things, truth is to touch and say them, and there is no error, only not touching (10).

@ch Metaph.IX.1 | Potency in the strict sense; incapacity
After substance, being as potency and actuality. First potency in the strictest sense, though not the most useful here: the potency related to motion. All the powers so called, apart from those named by likeness, as in geometry, are principles of change in another thing, or in the thing itself as other: the power to act, the power to be acted on, the state of not being acted on for the worse. In one way the power to act and to be acted on is one, in another two. Incapacity is the privation contrary to such a power, and privation is said in several ways.

@p
We have spoken of what is primarily, the being to which all the other categories of being are referred: substance. For it is by the account of substance that the other things are said to be, quantity and quality and the rest so spoken of; all of them will involve the account of substance, as we said in the first discussions. And since being is said in one way as a what, or a quality, or a quantity, and in another way by potency and actuality and by function, let us also determine about potency and actuality. First about potency in the sense in which it is most strictly so called, though it is not the most useful for what we now want; for potency and actuality extend further than the things spoken of only with reference to motion. But when we have spoken of this, we shall make the others clear as well in our determinations about actuality.
!flag in respect of potency and complete reality, and of function | Ross's "complete reality" renders ἐντελέχεια, "actuality", as in Books Ζ and Η; his "function" renders τὸ ἔργον, "the work", from which ἐνέργεια is named (IX.8, 1050a21-23). The modern version says "actuality" and "function". This book uses ἐντελέχεια and ἐνέργεια nearly alike, both "actuality".
!note "The first discussions": Book Ζ (VII.1, 1028a10-b2), where all the other categories are said to be by reference to substance. The two ways of being are the categories (Books Ζ and Η) and potency and actuality (this book); the third and fourth, being by accident and being as true, were set aside in Book Ε (VI.2-4), and being as true comes back in chapter 10.

@p
@greek ὅτι μὲν οὖν λέγεται πολλαχῶς ἡ δύναμις
We have determined elsewhere that "potency" and "can" are said in many ways. Of these, let us set aside all the powers so called by equivocation; for some are so called by a likeness, as in geometry we say things are "possible" or "impossible" by their being or not being in a certain way. But those that are related to the same form are all principles of some kind, and are so called by reference to one first power, which is a principle of change in another thing, or in the thing itself as other. One kind is the power of being acted on, the principle in the very thing acted on of its being changed by another thing, or by itself as other; another is the state of being unaffected for the worse, and of not being destroyed by another thing, or by itself as other, as by a principle of change. In all these definitions the account of the first power is present. Again, these are called powers either of only acting or of only being acted on, or of doing so well; so that in their accounts too the accounts of the earlier powers are somehow present.
!flag a state of insusceptibility to change for the worse | Ross's words are right: ἕξις ἀπαθείας, "a state of not being affected", is the power to resist, as a hard thing has a power not to be crushed (Book Δ, V.12, 1019a26-32). The modern version says "the state of being unaffected for the worse".
!note "Elsewhere": Book Δ (V.12), on "power" and "possible". "In geometry": a line is "in power" (δυνάμει) a square, the square on it; the Greek mathematicians said a line "can" the square it makes. Plato's Theaetetus (147d-148b) uses the word so, of the lines whose squares are commensurable.

@p
@greek φανερὸν οὖν ὅτι ἔστι μὲν ὡς μία δύναμις
It is plain, then, that in one way the power of acting and of being acted on is one (for a thing is capable both by having itself the power of being acted on and by another's being able to be acted on by it), and in another way different. One is in what is acted on: it is because it has a certain principle, and because the matter too is a certain principle, that what is acted on is acted on, one thing by one, another by another. The oily can be burned, and what yields in a certain way can be crushed, and likewise with the rest. The other is in the agent, as heat and the art of building are, the one in what can heat and the other in the builder. That is why, in so far as a thing has grown together into one, it is not acted on at all by itself: for it is one, and not something other. And incapacity and the incapable are the privation contrary to such a power; so that every power is of the same thing, and in the same respect, as an incapacity. Privation is said in several ways: of what does not have a thing, and of what would naturally have it but does not, either in general or when it would naturally have it, and either in a certain way, as completely, or in any way at all. And in some cases, if things that would naturally have something do not have it by force, we say they are deprived of it.
!flag in so far as a thing is an organic unity | Ross's "organic unity" renders ᾗ συμπέφυκεν, "in so far as it has grown together", grown into one by nature; Ross's phrase is a later idea. The modern version says "has grown together into one".
!flag impotency, which is contrary to such a power | Taylor's "impotency" renders ἀδυναμία, "incapacity", "want of power"; the English word has since narrowed its sense. The modern version says "incapacity".

@ch Metaph.IX.2 | Powers with reason and powers without
Some principles are in lifeless things, some in living things and in the soul, and in the part of the soul that has reason; so some powers are without reason and some with it. Every art is a power. A power with reason is of contraries, medicine of health and of disease, while one without reason is of one thing, as heat of heating only.
