The reason is that knowledge is an account, and the same account makes plain a thing and its privation, though not alike. The power to do well implies the power to do; not the other way round.

@p
Since some such principles are present in lifeless things, and others in living things, and in the soul, and in the part of the soul that has reason, it is clear that of the powers too some will be without reason and some with reason. That is why all the arts, the productive kinds of knowledge, are powers: they are principles of change in another thing, or in the thing itself as other. And those with reason are each the same power of contraries, but those without reason are one of one: the hot, for instance, is a power only of heating, but medicine of disease and of health.
!flag accompanied by knowledge of a rational formula | Ross's "accompanied by knowledge of a rational formula" renders μετὰ λόγου, "with reason", "with an account": a power that works through knowing what it makes, as against heat, which heats without knowing. Ross's "rational formula" for λόγος is his own; the modern version says "with reason", and "account" for λόγος as throughout this work.
!note The division of the soul into a part that has reason and a part without it is the Nicomachean Ethics' (I.13, 1102a26-b28), after the Academy. For the Platonists, after Plato's Republic (IV, 436a-441c), the rational soul is the soul's own and highest part, and its power of contraries is the root of its freedom (Plotinus VI.8.1-6).

@p
@greek αἴτιον δὲ ὅτι λόγος ἐστὶν ἡ ἐπιστήμη
The reason is that knowledge is an account, and the same account makes plain both the thing and its privation, though not in like manner; in one way it is of both, and in another rather of what is present. So such kinds of knowledge must be of contraries, of the one in themselves and of the other not in themselves; for the account too is of the one in itself, and of the other, in a way, by accident. For it makes plain the contrary by denial and removal, since the contrary is the first privation, and this is the removal of the other. Since contraries do not come to be in the same thing, while knowledge is a power by having an account, and the soul has a principle of motion, the healthy produces only health, the heating only heat and the cooling only cold, but the one who knows produces both. For there is an account of both, though not in like manner, and it is in a soul which has a principle of motion; so that the soul will move both from the same principle, joining them to the same thing. That is why the things that are capable by reason produce contraries to the things capable without reason: they are held under one principle, the account.
!flag since by negation and ablation it manifests | Taylor's "ablation" renders ἀποφορά, "removal", "taking away": the contrary is known as the absence of its opposite, disease as the removal of health. The modern version says "removal".
!flag the calorific only heat and the frigorific only cold | Ross's Latin words render τὸ θερμαντικόν and τὸ ψυκτικόν, simply "what can heat" and "what can cool". The modern version says "the heating" and "the cooling".
!note The same knowledge is of contraries: the argument of Plato's Hippias Minor (365d-369b, 375d-376b), that the one who knows the truth is best able to lie, and of the Republic (I, 333e-334a), that the guard is also the best thief. Aristotle draws a different moral: what decides is not the knowledge but desire or choice (IX.5).

@p
@greek φανερὸν δὲ καὶ ὅτι τῇ μὲν τοῦ εὖ δυνάμει
It is plain too that the power of only acting or being acted on follows on the power of doing so well, but the second does not always follow on the first. For whoever does something well must also do it, but whoever only does it need not do it well.

@ch Metaph.IX.3 | Against the Megarians: potency is not actuality
The Megarians say that a thing can act only when it is acting. Then no one would be a builder except when building, and would lose the art each time he stopped; nothing would be hot or sweet unless perceived, as Protagoras says; we would be blind many times a day; and what is not happening could never happen, so that motion and coming to be would be done away with. So potency and actuality are different. What is capable is that to which nothing impossible follows if its actuality belongs to it. The word "actuality" has come from motions to other things.

@p
There are some, such as the Megarians, who say that a thing can act only when it is acting, and when it is not acting it cannot: the one who is not building, for instance, cannot build, but only the one who is building, when he is building; and likewise with the rest. The absurd consequences of this are not hard to see. Clearly no one will be a builder unless he is building (for being a builder is being able to build), and likewise with the other arts. If, then, it is impossible to have such arts without having learned them at some time and acquired them, and impossible not to have them without having lost them at some time (by forgetting, or by some affection, or by time; not by the destruction of the object, for that always is), then when he stops he will not have the art, and yet when he builds again straight away, how will he have got it? And likewise with lifeless things: nothing will be cold or hot or sweet or perceptible at all if it is not being perceived, so that they will find themselves saying what Protagoras says. Indeed, nothing will even have perception unless it is perceiving, actually at work. If, then, the blind is what does not have sight, though it would naturally have it, when it would naturally have it, and while it still is, the same people will be blind many times a day, and deaf.
!note The Megarians: the school of Euclides of Megara, a companion of Socrates present at his death (Phaedo 59c), to whom Plato and others withdrew after it. Diodorus Cronus, of the same school, held that only what is or will be is possible, his "Master Argument" (Epictetus, Discourses II.19). "What Protagoras says": that man is the measure of all things, so that a thing is as it appears to each (Plato's Theaetetus 152a-c; Book Γ, IV.5).

@p
@greek ἔτι εἰ ἀδύνατον τὸ ἐστερημένον δυνάμεως
Again, if what is deprived of power is incapable, what is not happening will be incapable of happening; but whoever says that what is incapable of happening is or will be will say what is false, for this is what "incapable" meant. So these arguments do away with both motion and coming to be. For what stands will always stand, and what sits will always sit: it will not get up if it is sitting, since what cannot get up will be incapable of getting up. If, then, we cannot say this, it is plain that potency and actuality are different (these arguments make potency and actuality the same, which is why it is no small thing they seek to do away with). So it is possible for something to be capable of being and not be, and capable of not being and yet be; and likewise with the other categories: capable of walking and not walking, and capable of not walking and walking. A thing is capable if, should the actuality of which it is said to have the power belong to it, nothing impossible will follow. I mean, for instance, if something is capable of sitting and can sit, then if sitting belongs to it, nothing impossible will follow; and likewise if it is capable of being moved or moving, of standing or making stand, of being or coming to be, or of not being or not coming to be.
!note "No small thing": the Megarian argument would do away with motion and coming to be, as Parmenides and Zeno did, from whom the Megarians drew. The definition of the capable, "nothing impossible follows if it is supposed actual", is taken up in the next chapter.

@p
@greek ἐλήλυθε δʼ ἡ ἐνέργεια τοὔνομα
The name "actuality", which is set alongside "complete actuality", has come to the other things above all from motions; for motion is thought to be actuality above all. That is why people do not grant being moved to things that are not, though they grant them some other predicates: they say that things that are not are objects of thought and desire, but not that they are moved. And this is because they are not in actuality, but would be in actuality if they were moved. For of things that are not, some are in potency; but they are not, because they are not in complete actuality.
!flag dianoetic and desirable objects | Taylor's "dianoetic" renders διανοητά, "objects of thought", things one can think about; the word is the Greek carried over, as in Book Ε (VI.1). The modern version says "objects of thought".
!note "Actuality" (ἐνέργεια) and "complete actuality" (ἐντελέχεια) are both Aristotle's coinages: the first from ἔργον, "work", being at work; the second from τέλος, "end", having one's end within. The modern version says "actuality" for both, and "complete actuality" where they stand side by side.

@ch Metaph.IX.4 | The possible and the false
If the possible is what was said, it cannot be true to say "this is possible, but will not be", as if the diagonal could be measured but never will be; that would do away with the impossible. The false and the impossible are not the same: that you are standing now is false, not impossible. And if B must be when A is, then if A is possible, B must be possible too.

@p
If the possible is what has been said, or follows from it, it is plain that it cannot be true to say "this is possible, but will not be", so that the impossible would escape us in this way. I mean, for instance, if someone, not reckoning with what the impossible is, were to say that it is possible for the diagonal to be measured, but that it will not be measured, because nothing prevents a thing that is capable of being or coming to be from not being or not being going to be. But from what has been laid down this must follow: if we were to suppose that what is not, but is possible, is or has come to be, nothing impossible will follow. But here it will, for the diagonal's being measured is impossible. For the false and the impossible are not the same: that you are standing now is false, but not impossible.
!flag even are equal to odd numbers | These words are not in the Greek of this chapter; Taylor adds them from the proof that the diagonal is incommensurable with the side, which Aristotle gives in the Prior Analytics (I.23, 41a26-27): if it were commensurable, odd numbers would be equal to even.
!note "Measured": the diagonal of the square is incommensurable with its side; no common measure goes into both. The discovery, credited to the Pythagoreans, is Aristotle's standing example of the impossible; Plato's Theaetetus (147d-148b) and Laws (VII, 819d-820c) speak of it.

@p
@greek ἅμα δὲ δῆλον καὶ ὅτι, εἰ τοῦ Α ὄντος
At the same time it is clear that if, when A is, B must be, then if A is possible, B too must be possible. For if B need not be possible, nothing prevents its not being possible. Let A, then, be possible. Then, when A was possible, if A were supposed, nothing impossible would follow; and then B must be. But B was impossible. Let it be impossible, then. If B is impossible, A must be so too. But the first was impossible; and so the second too. If, then, A is possible, B too will be possible, if they were so related that when A is, B must be. If, then, A and B being so related, B is not possible in this way, A and B will not be related as was supposed. And if, when A is possible, B must be possible, then if A is, B too must be. For to say that B must be possible if A is possible means this: that if A is, both when and in the way in which it was possible for it to be, B too must then and in that way be.
!note The text of the middle sentences is uncertain; Ross's English brackets "Let it be impossible ... therefore B also is possible", and his Greek of 1924 reads them as the modern version gives them. The rule is the one of the Prior Analytics (I.15, 34a5-24): from the possible, nothing impossible follows.

@ch Metaph.IX.5 | How powers are acquired, and what decides
Some powers are inborn, like the senses; others come by habit or by learning, and these one must exercise first. A power without reason acts of necessity when agent and patient meet; a power with reason, being of contraries, would then do contraries at once, which is impossible. So something else decides: desire or choice. Whatever a thing desires decisively, it will do, when it is in the state to do it and meets what is to be acted on; and it cannot do two contraries at once, even if it wishes to.

@p
All powers being either inborn, like the senses, or acquired by habit, like the power of playing the flute, or by learning, like that of the arts, those that come by habit and by reason one must have exercised first in order to have; those not of this kind, and those of being acted on, one need not.
!note The three origins of powers, nature, habit and teaching, are those of the Nicomachean Ethics (II.1, 1103a14-b25): we become builders by building and just by doing just acts. Plato's Meno opens on the same question, whether virtue comes by teaching, by practice or by nature (70a).

@p
@greek ἐπεὶ δὲ τὸ δυνατὸν τὶ δυνατὸν
Since what is capable is capable of something, and at some time, and in some way, and with whatever else must be added in the definition; and since some things can move by reason, and their powers are with reason, while others are without reason, and their powers without reason; and the first must be in a living thing, while the second can be in both: powers of the second kind, when the agent and the patient meet in the way they are capable of, must act and be acted on, but powers of the first kind need not. For those are all productive of one thing each, but these of contraries, so that they would produce contraries at the same time; and this is impossible. So something else must be what decides: I mean desire or choice. For whichever of the two a thing desires decisively, it will do, when it is in the state in which it is capable and meets what is to be acted on. So everything capable by reason, when it desires that of which it has the power, and in the way it has it, must do it; and it has it when what is to be acted on is present and in a certain state; otherwise it will not be able to act. (There is no further need to add "if nothing outside prevents it": it has the power as it is a power of acting, and this is not in all conditions but in certain ones, among which outside hindrances will be ruled out, for they are excluded by some of the things added in the definition.) That is why, even if someone wishes or has an appetite to do two things, or contraries, at the same time, he will not do them: it is not in this way that he has the power for them, nor is it a power of doing them at the same time, since he will do the things it is a power of in the way it is.
!flag I mean by this, desire or will | Ross's "will" renders προαίρεσις, "choice", the deliberate choice of the Nicomachean Ethics (III.2-3), as in Book Ε (VI.1); Taylor says "free-will". The modern version says "choice".
!flag But I call this appetite, or free-will | Taylor's "free-will" for προαίρεσις reads a later idea of the will into the word, as in Book Δ (V.1); his "appetite" for ὄρεξις, "desire", is closer. The modern version says "desire or choice".

@ch Metaph.IX.6 | What actuality is; actuality and motion
Actuality is the thing's being present, not in the way we call potency: Hermes in the wood, the half-line in the whole, the knower who is not thinking. It cannot be defined, but is seen by analogy: as the building to what can build, the waking to the sleeping, the seeing to what has its eyes shut, what is shaped to the matter. Some actualities are as motion to potency, others as substance to matter. The unlimited is in potency differently, never to be separate. And there is a difference between a motion, which is incomplete and stops at its end, like learning or building, and an actuality in which the end is present, like seeing, thinking and living well: at the same time one sees and has seen.

@p
Since we have spoken of the potency that is related to motion, let us determine about actuality, what actuality is and what kind of thing it is. For as we distinguish, it will at the same time be clear about the capable, that we call capable not only what is of a nature to move another thing, or to be moved by another, either without qualification or in some way, but also in another sense; and it is in search of this that we went through the earlier senses too.
!flag let us discuss actuality, its genus | The Greek asks "what actuality is and what kind of thing it is" (τί τέ ἐστιν ... καὶ ποῖόν τι); Ross's "its genus and its differentia" is his gloss, as in Book Ζ (VII.17). The modern version keeps the Greek's plainer words.

@p
@greek ἔστι δὴ ἐνέργεια τὸ ὑπάρχειν τὸ πρᾶγμα
Actuality, then, is the thing's being present, not in the way we call "in potency". We say "in potency", for instance, that a Hermes is in the wood and the half in the whole, because it could be taken out; and we call even the one who is not thinking a knower, if he is able to think; and the other is in actuality. What we mean is plain from the particular cases by induction, and one must not look for a definition of everything, but also see the analogy at a glance: that as what is building is to what can build, so is the waking to the sleeping, what is seeing to what has its eyes shut but has sight, what has been shaped out of the matter to the matter, and what has been worked up to what is unworked. Let actuality be marked off by the one part of this difference, and the capable by the other. Things are said to be in actuality not all in like manner, but by analogy: as this is in this or to this, so that is in that or to that; for some are as motion to potency, and others as substance to some matter.
!flag are understanding and have understood | Ross's "understanding" renders φρονεῖ, "is thinking wisely", the exercise of practical wisdom (φρόνησις); Taylor says "is prudent". The modern version says "is wise". The line is the next paragraph's.
!note "Hermes in the wood": a figure of the god, as a sculptor would carve from a block; the example is a commonplace (Book Δ, V.7, 1017b7). Plotinus (II.5, "On what is in potency and what in actuality") takes up these pages, and asks whether there is potency in the intelligible world: there, he answers, all is actuality, and matter alone is wholly potency.
!note "See the analogy at a glance": actuality has no genus above it by which to be defined; it is grasped in the cases, by seeing that they stand alike. Proclus (Elements of Theology, props. 77-79) builds on these pages: what is in potency is brought to actuality by what is already actual.

@p
@greek ἄλλως δὲ καὶ τὸ ἄπειρον καὶ τὸ κενόν
The unlimited and the void, and all such things, are said to be in potency and in actuality in another way from many of the things that are, such as what sees, what walks and what is seen. For these can at some time be truly said without qualification: the seen because it is being seen, or because it can be seen. But the unlimited is not in potency in the sense that it will be in actuality, separate, except in knowledge. For it is the division's never giving out that gives this actuality its being in potency, not its being separated.
!note The unlimited is "in potency" because division can always go on, never because an unlimited will one day be actual (Physics III.6, 206a14-b27). The Platonists set the unlimited (ἄπειρον) beside limit as the two principles after the One (Philebus 16c-17a, 23c-27c; Proclus, Elements of Theology, props. 89-92).

@p
@greek ἐπεὶ δὲ τῶν πράξεων ὧν ἔστι πέρας
Since of the actions that have a limit none is an end, but all are of the things related to the end, as slimming is (the things themselves, while one is slimming them, are in motion in this way, the things for whose sake the motion is not yet being present), these are not action, or not complete action (for they are not an end); but that in which the end is present is action too. At the same time one sees and has seen, is wise and has been wise, thinks and has thought; but one is not learning and has learned, nor being cured and has been cured. One lives well and has lived well at the same time, and is happy and has been happy. If not, it would have to stop at some time, as when one slims; but as it is, it does not: one lives and has lived. Of these, then, we must call the one kind motions, and the other actualities. For every motion is incomplete, slimming, learning, walking, building: these are motions, and incomplete ones. For one is not at the same time walking and has walked, nor building and has built, nor coming to be and has come to be, nor being moved and has been moved; they are different, and so are moving and having moved. But the same thing at the same time has seen and is seeing, is thinking and has thought. The one kind I call actuality, the other motion. What, then, being in actuality is, and what kind of thing it is, let it be plain to us from these and such considerations.
!note The test of the tenses: an actuality is complete at every moment, so that "sees" and "has seen" are true together; a motion is complete only at its end, when it stops. The Nicomachean Ethics (X.4, 1174a13-b14) uses it for pleasure, which is whole at every moment, like seeing. Plotinus (VI.1.16) asks whether the line holds; for the Platonists, the intellect's thinking is the actuality without motion, eternal, and the soul's reasoning, step by step, is like motion.
!note The passage (1048b18-35) is missing from some manuscripts and from the medieval Latin translations, and no ancient commentary on it survives; Ross's text keeps it, as the modern version does.

@ch Metaph.IX.7 | When a thing is in potency
A thing is not in potency at any and every time: earth is not yet a man in potency, nor perhaps even the seed. What comes to be by thought is in potency when nothing outside or in the matter hinders it once it is willed; what comes to be by nature, when it will become the thing through its own principle, nothing outside hindering. The matter is called not "that" but "that-en", wooden, not wood; and the first thing so called is the first matter. The underlying thing of affections is a this, a man; of forms it is matter.

@p
We must determine when each thing is in potency and when not; for it is not at any and every time. Is earth, for instance, a man in potency? No, but rather when it has already become seed, and perhaps not even then. So too not everything could be healed by medicine, or by luck, but there is something that is capable of it, and this is what is healthy in potency. The mark of what comes to be in complete actuality from being in potency, by thought, is that it comes to be when it has been willed, nothing outside hindering; and on the other side, in what is healed, that nothing in it hinders. Likewise a house is in potency: if nothing in the thing acted on and in the matter hinders it from becoming a house, and there is nothing that must be added or taken away or changed, this is a house in potency; and so in the same way with the other things whose principle of coming to be is outside. And of those whose principle is in the very thing that has it, those are in potency that will be through themselves, if nothing outside hinders. The seed, for instance, is not yet (for it must fall into another and change), but when through its own principle it is already such, then it is this in potency; in the first state it needs another principle, as earth is not yet a statue in potency (for it will be bronze by changing).
!note "By thought" and "by nature": the works of art and of nature, as Ross's note says, whose principles are outside the thing (the doctor, the builder) or in it (the seed). The seed "must fall into another": into the mother, where it changes (Generation of Animals II.1-4).

@p
@greek ἔοικε δὲ ὃ λέγομεν εἶναι οὐ τόδε
It seems that what we call not "this" but "that-en" (a box is not "wood" but "wooden", nor is wood "earth" but "earthen"; and again, earth, if it is so, not something else but "that-en") is always in potency, without qualification, the thing after it. A box, for instance, is not "earthen" nor "earth" but "wooden", for wood is a box in potency, and this is the matter of a box: wood in general of a box in general, and this wood of this box. If there is something first, which is no longer called "that-en" after something else, this is first matter: if earth, for instance, is "airy", and air is not fire but "fiery", fire is first matter, not being a this. For the thing of which something is said, the underlying thing, differs by being a this or not. The underlying thing of affections, for instance, is a man, body and soul, and the affection is the musical, the white. (When music has come to be in it, it is called not "music" but "musical", and the man is not "whiteness" but "white", nor "walking" or "motion" but "walking" or "moving", as with the "that-en".) Wherever it is so, the last thing is substance; but where it is not so, and what is predicated is a form and a this, the last thing is matter and material substance. And it turns out rightly that the "that-en" is said both of the matter and of the affections, for both are indefinite. When, then, a thing is to be called in potency and when not, has been said.
!flag is prime matter; while the ultimate substratum | Ross's "prime matter" renders πρώτη ὕλη, "first matter", which is right; but the words after, "while the ultimate substratum, if it is an individual, is not matter but substance", follow a reading his 1924 Greek does not keep. The modern version follows the 1924 text: fire would be first matter, "not being a this".
!note "That-en" (ἐκείνινον), a word Aristotle makes from "that" (ἐκεῖνο) with the ending of "wooden" (ξύλινον): the matter is named by an adjective of the thing made, as the subject is named by an adjective of its affection. The Platonists' "first matter" beneath the elements, formless, is Plato's receptacle (Timaeus 49a-52b), which Plotinus (II.4) and the commentators read into this line.

@ch Metaph.IX.8 | Actuality is prior to potency
Actuality is prior to every potency, and to nature too: in account, since the capable is defined by its actuality; in time, in that the actual of the same species comes first, man from man, and the learner learns to play the lyre by playing it; and in substance, because what is later in coming to be is prior in form, and everything goes toward its end, which is the actuality: animals do not see in order to have sight, but have sight in order to see. The substance and the form are actuality. More strictly still, the eternal things are prior to the perishable, and nothing eternal is in potency: the sun and the stars and the whole heaven are always at work and never tire.

@p
Since we have determined in how many ways "prior" is said, it is plain that actuality is prior to potency. I mean not only the potency determined as a principle of change in another thing or in the thing itself as other, but in general every principle of motion or of rest. For nature too is in the same genus as potency: it is a principle of motion, though not in another thing but in the thing itself as itself. To every such potency actuality is prior both in account and in substance; in time it is in one way, and in another not.
!note "In how many ways 'prior' is said": Book Δ (V.11). The three priorities, in account, in time and in substance, are those of Book Ζ (VII.1, 1028a32-b2), where substance is first in all three ways.

@p
@greek τῷ λόγῳ μὲν οὖν ὅτι προτέρα, δῆλον
That it is prior in account is clear. What is capable in the first sense is capable because it can become actual: I mean, for instance, by "able to build" what can build, by "able to see" what can see, and by "visible" what can be seen; and the same account holds for the rest, so that the account and the knowledge of the one must come before the knowledge of the other. In time it is prior in this way: the actual thing that is the same in species is prior, though not the same in number. I mean that the matter, the seed and what is able to see, which are in potency a man, corn and seeing, but not yet in actuality, are prior in time to this man who already is in actuality, to the corn and to the one seeing; but before them in time are other things in actuality from which they came to be. For what is in actuality always comes to be from what is in potency by something in actuality, as man from man, musician by musician, there always being something that moves first; and what moves is already in actuality. It was said in the discussions of substance that everything that comes to be comes to be something from something and by something, and this the same in species. That is why it seems impossible to be a builder without having built anything, or a lyre-player without having played the lyre at all; for the one learning to play the lyre learns to play by playing, and likewise with the others. From this came the sophistical refutation, that one who does not have the knowledge will do what the knowledge is of, since the learner does not have it. But since of what is coming to be something has come to be, and of what is moving in general something has moved (this is clear in the discussions of motion), the learner too must perhaps have something of the knowledge. At any rate in this way too it is clear that actuality is prior to potency in this way also, in coming to be and in time.
!note "In the discussions of substance": Book Ζ (VII.7-8): man begets man. "In the discussions of motion": the Physics (VI.6, 236b32-237b22), that whatever is changing has already changed in part. The sophism of the learner is that of Plato's Euthydemus (275d-277c): does one learn what one knows or what one does not know? Plato's Meno (80d-e) gives it as the paradox of inquiry, which recollection answers.

@p
@greek ἀλλὰ μὴν καὶ οὐσίᾳ γε
But it is prior in substance too. First, because what is later in coming to be is prior in form and in substance, as the man to the boy and the human being to the seed: the one already has the form, the other not. And because everything that comes to be goes toward a principle and an end (for that for the sake of which is a principle, and coming to be is for the sake of the end), and the actuality is the end, and it is for this that the potency is acquired. Animals do not see in order to have sight; they have sight in order to see. Likewise people have the art of building in order to build, and the theoretical power in order to think; they do not think in order to have the theoretical power, except those who are practising, and these do not think except in a limited way, or because they have no need to think. Further, the matter is in potency because it may come to the form; and when it is in actuality, then it is in the form.
!flag it is also prior in substantiality | Ross's "substantiality" renders οὐσίᾳ, "in substance", as in the chapter's first lines; the modern version says "in substance".
!note "Have sight in order to see": the end comes first in being, though last in coming to be. For the Platonists the line is one of Aristotle's agreements with Plato's Timaeus (46e-47c), where the gods gave sight so that we might see the heavens and so come to philosophy. The words "or because they have no need to think" are uncertain; the 1924 text marks them as corrupt.

@p
@greek ὁμοίως δὲ καὶ ἐπὶ τῶν ἄλλων, καὶ ὧν κίνησις τὸ τέλος
Likewise with the rest, even those whose end is a motion. That is why, as teachers think they have reached the end when they have shown the pupil at work, so does nature. For if it does not happen so, we shall have Pauson's Hermes again: it will be unclear whether the knowledge is inside or outside, as it was with that figure. For the work is the end, and the actuality is the work; that is why the name "actuality" is said from "work", and tends toward complete actuality. Since in some cases the last thing is the use (as in sight it is seeing, and nothing else besides this comes from sight), while from some things something comes to be (from the art of building a house besides the building), none the less in the one case the act is the end, and in the other more an end than the potency is. For the building is in what is being built, and comes to be, and is, at the same time as the house. Where, then, what comes to be is something besides the use, the actuality is in what is being made (the building in what is being built, the weaving in what is being woven, and likewise with the rest, and in general the motion in what is being moved); but where there is no other work besides the actuality, the actuality is in the agents themselves (the seeing in the one seeing, the contemplation in the one contemplating, and the life in the soul, and so happiness too, for it is a certain kind of life). So it is plain that the substance and the form are actuality. By this argument, then, it is plain that actuality is prior to potency in substance; and, as we said, in time one actuality always comes before another, back to that of what always moves first.
!flag the eternal prime mover | The Greek says "what always moves first" (τοῦ ἀεὶ κινοῦντος πρώτως); Ross's "prime mover" is the later schools' name, as in Book Δ. Book Λ (XII.7-8) shows what it is: the unmoved mover, an intellect in actuality.
!note Pauson's Hermes: Ross's note, from Alexander, tells of a figure of Hermes by the painter Pauson (named in the Poetics 2, 1448a6, and the Politics VIII.5, 1340a36) which one could not tell whether it was painted on the surface or set inside the stone. "Happiness is a certain kind of life": the Nicomachean Ethics (I.7, 1098a16-18; X.7-8), where the best life is contemplation, the actuality of intellect.

@p
@greek ἀλλὰ μὴν καὶ κυριωτέρως
But it is prior in a stricter sense too. For eternal things are prior in substance to perishable ones, and nothing eternal is in potency. The argument is this. Every potency is at the same time of the contradictory: for what is not capable of belonging could not belong to anything, but everything capable may not be at work. What is capable of being, then, may both be and not be; the same thing, then, is capable both of being and of not being. And what is capable of not being may not be; and what may not be is perishable, either without qualification, or in the very respect in which it is said that it may not be, in place, or in quantity, or in quality: "without qualification" means in substance. Nothing, then, of what is imperishable without qualification is in potency without qualification (though nothing prevents its being so in some respect, as in quality or place); so all of them are in actuality. Nor is anything of what is of necessity (and yet these are first: if these were not, nothing would be); nor is motion, if there is an eternal motion; nor, if there is something eternally moved, is it moved in potency, except from where to where (and nothing prevents its having matter for this). That is why the sun and the stars and the whole heaven are always at work, and there is no fear that they may some time stand still, as the students of nature fear. Nor do they tire in doing this; for their motion is not concerned with the potency of the contradictory, as it is for perishable things, so that the continuity of the motion should be laborious; for the cause of this is the substance that is matter and potency, not actuality. The things in change, too, such as earth and fire, imitate the imperishable ones; for these too are always at work, since they have their motion of themselves and in themselves. The other potencies, from what has been determined, are all of the contradictory: for what can move something in this way can also move it not in this way, at least those with reason; and those without reason will be of the contradictory by being present or not. If, then, there are any natures or substances such as those who deal in arguments say the Forms are, there will be something much more knowing than knowledge itself, and much more moved than motion itself; for these are more actualities, and those are potencies of them. That actuality is prior both to potency and to every principle of change, then, is plain.
!flag more scientific than the Idea of science | Ross's "the Idea of science" renders αὐτὸ ἐπιστήμη, "knowledge itself", Plato's way of naming the Form (Parmenides 134a-b; Phaedrus 247d); the Greek does not say "Idea". The modern version says "knowledge itself", and "Form" for ἰδέα.
!note "As the students of nature fear": the early natural philosophers, who thought the heaven might stop; On the Heavens (II.1, 284a18-26) rejects for the same reason the myth of Atlas, who holds it up. "Imitate the imperishable": fire and earth always move to their places, as the stars always move in circles; Plato's Timaeus (37c-38c) has time imitate eternity by its circling.
!note The argument against the Forms: a Form of knowledge, a universal, would be only a potency of the particular acts of knowing, and so less than they. The Platonists answered that the Forms are not universals of this kind but intellects in actuality, the thoughts of the divine intellect (Syrianus on Book Μ; Plotinus V.9.5-8), and so the very actuality Aristotle asks for.

@ch Metaph.IX.9 | The good actuality is better than the potency; geometry
A good actuality is better and more honourable than the potency, which is of both contraries alike; a bad actuality is worse. So evil is not besides the things, but later in nature than the potency; and in the eternal things there is no evil, no fault and no corruption. Geometrical truths too are found by actuality: by dividing the figures one finds them, and they are plain as soon as one has drawn the line; for thinking is an actuality.

@p
That the actuality is better and more honourable than the good potency is plain from this. Whatever is spoken of as capable is the same thing capable of contraries: what is said to be able to be healthy, for instance, is the same as what is able to be ill, and at the same time; for the same power is of being healthy and of being sick, of resting and of moving, of building and of pulling down, of being built and of falling down. Being capable of contraries, then, belongs at the same time; but contraries cannot belong at the same time, and the actualities too cannot belong at the same time (being healthy and being sick, for instance). So one of these must be the good, while the power is of both in like manner, or of neither; the actuality, then, is better. And with bad things the end and the actuality must be worse than the power; for what is capable is the same, capable of both contraries. Clearly, then, the bad is not something besides the things; for the bad is later in nature than the power. So in the things that are from the beginning and eternal there is nothing bad, no fault, nothing corrupted (for corruption is one of the bad things).
!flag the bad does not exist apart from bad things | The Greek says "the bad is not besides the things" (παρὰ τὰ πράγματα): there is no evil by itself, no Form of evil, apart from things done or undergone; Ross's "bad things" narrows the sense. The modern version keeps the Greek's "things".
!note No evil by itself, and none in the eternal things: the Platonists agreed. Proclus, On the Existence of Evils, holds that evil has no being of its own but a parasitic existence (παρυπόστασις), a falling short in a particular power, and that there is none among the gods; Plotinus (I.8) had made matter, as pure privation, evil itself, which Proclus denies.

@p
@greek εὑρίσκεται δὲ καὶ τὰ διαγράμματα ἐνεργείᾳ
The geometrical constructions too are found by actuality: people find them by dividing. If they had already been divided, they would be plain; but as it is, they are present in potency. Why are the angles of a triangle equal to two right angles? Because the angles about one point are equal to two right angles. If, then, the line parallel to the side had been drawn up, it would be clear at once, to one who saw it, why. Why is the angle in a semicircle a right angle, in every case? If three lines are equal, the two halves of the base and the line set up from the middle, it is clear at a glance to one who knows the earlier proposition. So it is plain that what is in potency is found by being brought into actuality. The reason is that thinking is an actuality; so that the potency comes from actuality, and that is why people come to know by making (for the single actuality is later in coming to be than the potency).
!flag geometrical relations are discovered | Ross's "geometrical relations" renders τὰ διαγράμματα, "diagrams", the figures drawn and the propositions proved on them, as Euclid's Elements were called. The modern version says "geometrical constructions".
!flag thinking is the actuality of thought | The Greek says only "thinking is an actuality" (ἡ νόησις ἐνέργεια); Ross adds "of thought". The modern version keeps the Greek's words.
!note The two proofs are Euclid's (Elements I.32 and III.31): draw the line through the vertex parallel to the base, and the angles of the triangle are seen to make up the two right angles about one point; join the centre of the semicircle to the vertex, and the three equal radii show the right angle. Proclus, on Euclid I (Friedlein 379-384), gives the proof of I.32 and ascribes it to the Pythagoreans, and reads the geometer's drawing as the soul's unfolding of the forms it holds within.

@ch Metaph.IX.10 | Being as true: the composite and the simple
Being is said by the categories, by potency and actuality, and as true and false. In things, truth is their being combined or divided: whoever thinks the divided divided and the combined combined is right. It is not because we think you pale that you are pale; it is because you are pale that we speak truly. With things that can be otherwise, the same opinion becomes true and false; with things that cannot, it is always the same. With simple things, which are not composite, truth is to touch and say them, and ignorance is not to touch them; there is no error about them, except by accident. And about unchanging things there is no error in time.

@p
Since being and not being are said in one way by the figures of the categories, in another by the potency or actuality of these or their contraries, and in another as true and false, and this in things is their being combined or divided, so that whoever thinks the divided to be divided and the combined to be combined is right, and whoever is in a state contrary to the things is in error: when is what is called truth or falsehood present, and when not? For we must consider what we mean by this. It is not because we think truly that you are pale that you are pale; it is because you are pale that we who say so speak truly. If, then, some things are always combined and cannot be divided, others are always divided and cannot be combined, and others admit of contraries, being is being combined and being one, and not being is not being combined but being more than one. About things that can be otherwise, then, the same opinion and the same account come to be false and true, and it is possible at one time to be right and at another to be in error; but about things that cannot be otherwise, the same opinion does not come to be at one time true and at another false, but the same opinions are always true and always false.
!flag while being and non-being in the strictest sense | Ross's 1908 English says that being "in the strictest sense" is the true and the false; his own 1924 Greek brackets the words "most strictly being" (κυριώτατα ὄν) as not Aristotle's, since Book Ε (VI.4) and this book make being as true the least strict sense, in thought rather than in things. The modern version follows the 1924 text.
!flag regarding contingent facts, then | Ross's "contingent facts" renders τὰ ἐνδεχόμενα, "the things that can be otherwise", as one is now pale and now not; the modern version says "things that can be otherwise".
!note "Because you are pale": truth follows the thing, not the thing our thought; the Categories (12, 14b11-22) says the same. Plato's Sophist (263a-d) defines the true statement as one that says of things that are as they are, and the false as one that says of them otherwise.

@p
@greek περὶ δὲ δὴ τὰ ἀσύνθετα
But about the incomposite things, what is being or not being, and the true and the false? Such a thing is not composite, so as to be when it is combined and not be if it is divided, like "the wood is pale" or "the diagonal is incommensurable"; nor will truth and falsehood still belong likewise to these. Rather, as truth is not the same in these cases, so neither is being. The true or false here is this: to touch and to say is true (for saying is not the same as affirming), and not to know is not to touch. For it is not possible to be in error about what a thing is, except by accident; and likewise about the incomposite substances, for it is not possible to be in error about them. And they are all in actuality, not in potency; for otherwise they would come to be and perish, but as it is, being itself does not come to be or perish, for it would have to come from something. About the things that are just what something is, and actualities, it is not possible to be in error, but only to think them or not; but what they are is inquired into about them, whether they are of such a kind or not. As for being as the true, and not being as the false: in one case it is true if the things are combined, and false if not combined; in the other, if it is, it is in this way, and if it is not in this way, it is not at all. And the true is thinking these things; there is no falsehood, nor error, but ignorance, and not such ignorance as blindness is; for blindness is as if one had no power of thinking at all.
!flag which are essences and exist in actuality | Ross's "essences" renders ὅπερ εἶναί τι, "just what something is", the things that are simply what they are, without matter and without parts; Ross's footnote sends the reader to VIII.3. The modern version keeps the Greek's words.
!flag truth is obtained by contact | Taylor's words are right: θιγεῖν is "to touch", the immediate grasp of what is simple, with no combining of subject and predicate. The modern version says "to touch". On the Soul (III.6, 430a26-b31) gives the same account of the thinking of indivisibles.
!note "To touch and to say" (θιγεῖν καὶ φάναι): the simple, without matter, is known not by an account that joins something to something, but by a direct grasp, which either reaches it or does not. The Platonists read the line with Plato's Seventh Letter (341c-d), where knowledge of the highest is kindled "like a light from a leaping fire", and with Plotinus' "touching" of the intellect and the One (V.3.10, VI.9.4).
!note "The incomposite substances": the substances without matter of Book Λ, the unmoved movers, which are in actuality, never in potency, and so cannot come to be or perish. "Being itself does not come to be": as Parmenides (fr. 8) argued, what is cannot come from what is not.

@p
@greek φανερὸν δὲ καὶ ὅτι περὶ τῶν ἀκινήτων
It is plain too that about unchanging things there is no error in respect of time, if one takes them to be unchanging. If one thinks the triangle does not change, for instance, one will not think that it sometimes has its angles equal to two right angles and sometimes not (for then it would change); but one may think that one thing has something and another not, as that no even number is prime, or that some are and some are not. But about a number that is one in number, not even this: for one will no longer think that one has it and another not, but will be right or in error as about what is always so.
!note The end of Book Θ, and of the inquiry into being in Books Ζ, Η and Θ. Book Ι turns to the one, which is said in as many ways as being. "No even number is prime": an error about a kind, that some numbers of it have a property and some not (two is in fact even and prime); about a single number, the error can only be about what is always so.
